\displaystyle i_{\tau,M}(\Upsilon_{K+\nabla^{2}_{z}H(\cdot,u(\cdot))})-i_{\tau,M}(\Upsilon_{K})-\nu_{\tau,K}(\Upsilon_{K}),

(2.29)

\displaystyle m^{0}(\tilde{\psi}_{H,K},w)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\nu_{\tau,M}(\Upsilon_{K+\nabla^{2}_{z}H(\cdot,u(\cdot))})=\dim{\rm Ker}(\Upsilon_{K+\nabla^{2}_{z}H(\cdot,u(\cdot))}(\tau)-M),

(2.30)

where
i_{\tau,M}
is defined by (
A.16
) and
\Upsilon_{K+\nabla^{2}_{z}H(\cdot,u(\cdot))}
is the fundamental matrix solution of

\dot{Z}(t)=J(K(t)+\nabla^{2}_{z}H(t,u(t)))Z(t)\;\forall t\in[0,\tau].

A direct proof of (
2.30
) can also be given by
following
[
19
, Proposition 3.14]
. When
\det(\Upsilon_{K}(\tau)-M)\neq 0
,
\tilde{\psi}_{H,K}
in
(
2.29
) and (
2.30
) is replaced by
{\psi}_{H,K}
in (
2.28
),
and
u=v=(-\tilde{\Lambda}_{M,\tau,K})^{-1}w
.

Corollary 2.13
.

For
M\in{\rm Sp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
, let

H:[0,\tau]\times\mathbb{R}^{2n}\to\mathbb{R},(t,u)\to H(t,u)

be measurable in
t
for each
u\in\mathbb{R}^{2n}
and twice continuously differentiable
in
u
for almost every
t\in[0,\tau]
. Assume that there exists
\ell>0
,
\alpha>0
,
\delta>0
, and

\beta,\gamma\in L^{2}(0,T;\mathbb{R}^{+})
such that
for almost every
t\in[0,\tau]
,

\displaystyle|(\nabla^{2}_{z}H(t,\xi)\eta,\eta)_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}|\leq\ell|\eta|^{2}\quad\forall\xi,\eta\in\mathbb{R}^{2n},

\displaystyle\delta|\xi|^{2}-\beta(t)\leq H(t,\xi)\leq\alpha|\xi|^{2}+\gamma(t)\quad\forall\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{2n}.

Then for any real
\kappa<-\ell
, and
{H}_{\kappa}(t,\xi):=H(t,\xi)-\frac{\kappa}{2}|\xi|^{2}
, functionals

\displaystyle\Phi(v)=\int^{T}_{0}\left[\frac{1}{2}(J\dot{v}(t),v(t))_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}+H({v}(t),t)\right]dt=\Phi_{H_{\kappa},\kappa I_{2n}}(v),

\displaystyle\Psi_{\kappa}(v):=\Psi_{H_{\kappa},\kappa I_{2n}}(v)=\int^{\tau}_{0}\left[\frac{1}{2}(J\dot{v}(t)+\kappa v(t),v(t))_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}+({H}_{\kappa})^{\ast}(t;-J\dot{v}(t)-\kappa v(t))\right]dt

are
C^{1}
and twice Gâteaux differentiable on
W^{1,2}_{M}([0,\tau],{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})
, and the following holds:

(i)

If
\bar{u}\in W^{1,2}_{M}([0,\tau],{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})
is a critical point of
\Phi
,
then each
u\in\bar{u}+{\rm Ker}(\Lambda_{M,\tau,\kappa I_{2n}})

is also a critical point of
\Psi_{\kappa}
on
W^{1,2}_{M}([0,\tau],{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})

and
\Psi_{\kappa}({u})=-\Phi(\bar{u})
.
Conversely, if
\bar{v}\in W^{1,2}_{M}([0,\tau],{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})
is a critical point of
\Psi_{\kappa}
,
then there exists a unique
\xi\in\mathcal{E}_{M,\tau,\kappa I_{2n}}
such that
\bar{u}:=\bar{v}-v_{\xi}
, where

v_{\xi}(t)=\Upsilon_{\kappa I_{2n}}(t)\xi
,
is a critical point of
\Phi
on
W^{1,2}_{M}([0,\tau],{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})

and satisfies
\Phi(\bar{u})=-\Psi_{\kappa}(\bar{v})
; this unique
\xi
is equal to

(\Upsilon_{\kappa I_{2n}}(t))^{-1}\big(\bar{v}(t)-\nabla_{z}(H_{\kappa})^{\ast}(t;-J\dot{\bar{v}}(t)-\kappa\bar{v}(t))\big)

and therefore

\bar{u}(t)=\nabla_{z}(H_{\kappa})^{\ast}(t;-J\dot{\bar{v}}(t)-K(t)\bar{v}(t))
.

(ii)

If
\det(\Upsilon_{\kappa I_{2n}}(\tau)-M)\neq 0
,
\Phi
and
\Psi_{\kappa}
have the same critical point sets
on
{W}^{1,2}_{M}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})
and
\Phi(u)=-\Psi_{\kappa}(u)
for each critical point
u
of them.

(iii)

The restrictions of
\Phi
and
\Psi_{\kappa}
to
\tilde{W}^{1,2}_{M,\kappa I_{2n}}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})
,
denoted by
\tilde{\Phi}
and
\tilde{\Psi}_{\kappa}
, have the same critical point sets and

\Phi(u)=-\Psi_{\kappa}(u)
for each critical point
u
of them.

(iv)

Any critical point of
\tilde{\Psi}_{\kappa}
is also one of
\Psi_{\kappa}
on
{W}^{1,2}_{M}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})
.

Moreover, we can take a real
\kappa<-\ell
such that

\det(\Upsilon_{\kappa I_{2n}}(\tau)-M)\neq 0
, and therefore

\bar{v}
is a critical point of
\Psi_{\kappa}
on
{W}^{1,2}_{M}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})

if and only if
\bar{w}:=-\Lambda_{M,\tau,\kappa I_{2n}}\bar{v}
is that of the
C^{1}
and twice Gâteaux differentiable functional

\psi_{\kappa}(w):=\int^{\tau}_{0}\left[\frac{1}{2}(w(t),((\Lambda_{M,\tau,\kappa I_{2n}})^{-1}w)(t))_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}+(H_{\kappa})^{\ast}(t;w(t))\right]dt

(2.31)

on
{L}^{2}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})
; in this case there holds

\displaystyle m^{-}({\psi}_{\kappa},\bar{w})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle i_{\tau,M}(\Upsilon_{\nabla^{2}_{z}H(\cdot,\bar{v}(\cdot))})-i_{\tau,M}(\Upsilon_{\kappa I_{2n}})-\nu_{\tau,M}(\Upsilon_{\kappa I_{2n}}),

(2.32)

\displaystyle m^{0}({\psi}_{\kappa},\bar{w})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\nu_{\tau,M}(\Upsilon_{\nabla^{2}_{z}H(\cdot,\bar{v}(\cdot))})=\dim{\rm Ker}(\Upsilon_{\nabla^{2}_{z}H(\cdot,\bar{v}(\cdot))}(\tau)-M),

(2.33)

where
\Upsilon_{\kappa I_{2n}}(t)=\exp(t\kappa J)
, and
\Upsilon_{\nabla^{2}_{z}H(\cdot,\bar{v}(\cdot))}
is the fundamental matrix solution of

\dot{Z}(t)=J(H_{t})^{\prime\prime}(\bar{v}(t))Z(t)=J\nabla^{2}_{z}H(t,\bar{v}(t))Z(t).

Proof.

For any
-\kappa>\ell
, by the assumptions it is easily seen that
H_{\kappa}
satisfies
(
2.19
) and (
2.14
). Therefore applying Theorem
2.11

to
K(t)=\kappa I_{2n}
,
H=H_{\kappa}
and therefore
\tilde{\psi}_{H,K}=\psi_{\kappa}
we obtain the conclusions above “Moreover”.

Since
\Upsilon_{\kappa I_{2n}}(t)=\exp(t\kappa J)
, by Proposition
A.5
we can choose a real
\kappa<-\ell
such that

\det(\Upsilon_{\kappa I_{2n}}(\tau)-M)\neq 0
. Other claims follow from Remark
2.12
and the fact that

\kappa I_{2n}+\nabla^{2}_{z}H_{\kappa}(\cdot,\bar{v}(\cdot))=\nabla^{2}_{z}H(\cdot,\bar{v}(\cdot))
.
∎

Proofs of Theorems

,

1.7
and Corollary

,

The following remark about parameter space
\Lambda
is effective for this section and next sections.

Remark 3.1
.

The parameter space
\Lambda
may be, respectively, replaced by its subsets

in

These four sets

,

Our proofs will be completed with theorems in

,

and Theorems

,

,

.
To this goal let us define

\bar{H}:\Lambda\times[0,\tau]\times{{\mathbb{R}}}^{2n}\to{\mathbb{R}}
by

\bar{H}(\lambda,t,z)={H}(\lambda,t,z+u_{\lambda}(t))-(z,\nabla_{z}{H}(\lambda,t,u_{\lambda}(t)))_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}.

(3.1)

Since each
\Lambda\times[0,\tau]\ni(\lambda,t)\mapsto u_{\lambda}(t)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2n}
is continuous,
it is clear that
\bar{H}
satisfies Assumption
1.2
and
\bar{u}\equiv 0\in\mathbb{R}^{2n}
satisfies

\dot{u}(t)=J\nabla_{z}\bar{H}(\lambda,t,u(t))\;\forall t\in[0,\tau]\quad\hbox{and}\quad u(\tau)=Mu(0)

(3.2)

for each
\lambda\in\Lambda
.
Note that a function
u:[0,\tau]\to{\mathbb{R}}^{2n}
satisfies
(
1.3
) with the parameter value
\lambda
if and only if the function
w(t):=u(t)-u_{\lambda}(t)

is a solution of (
3.2
). Hence we have

Claim 3.2
.

For
X=W^{1,2}_{M}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})
or
C^{1}_{M}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})
, the bifurcation problem of (
1.3
) in
\Lambda\times X

with respect to the branch
\{(\lambda,u_{\lambda})\,|\,\lambda\in\Lambda\}

is equivalent to that of (
3.2
) in
\Lambda\times X

with respect to the trivial branch
\{(\lambda,0)\,|\,\lambda\in\Lambda\}
.

Since
\nabla_{z}\bar{H}(\lambda,t,0)=\nabla_{z}H(\lambda,t,u_{\lambda}(t))

for all
(\lambda,t)\in\Lambda\times[0,\tau]
,
\gamma_{\lambda}:[0,\tau]\to{\rm Sp}(2n,\mathbb{R})

is also the fundamental matrix solution of

\dot{Z}(t)=J\nabla_{z}\bar{H}(\lambda,t,0)Z(t).

Therefore in what follows we only need to prove Theorems
1.4
,
1.7

in the case where
u_{\lambda}\equiv 0
for all

\lambda\in\Lambda
. (This means
\nabla_{z}{H}(\lambda,t,0)=0\;\forall(\lambda,t)
.)

Modify
H
outside an open neighborhood of
0\in\mathbb{R}^{2n}
.
By Remark
3.1

we always assume that
\Lambda
is compact and sequential compact
in what follows.
By Assumption
1.1

\Lambda\times[0,\tau]\ni(\lambda,t)\mapsto\nabla^{2}_{z}H(\lambda,t,0)\in\mathscr{L}_{s}(\mathbb{R}^{2n})

is continuous. Therefore we can get a constant
C>0
and
a compact neighborhood
U
of
0
in
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
such that

-CI_{2n}\leq\nabla^{2}_{z}H(\lambda,t,z)\leq CI_{2n},\quad\forall(\lambda,t,z)\in\Lambda\times[0,\tau]\times U.

Choose an open neighborhood of
U_{0}
of
0
in
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
such that
Cl(U_{0})\subset U
, and a smooth cut-off function

\chi:\mathbb{R}^{2n}\to[0,1]
such that
\chi|_{U_{0}}\equiv 1
and
\chi|_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}\setminus U}\equiv 0
.
Define

\tilde{H}:\Lambda\times[0,\tau]\times\mathbb{R}^{2n}\to{\mathbb{R}}

by
\tilde{H}(\lambda,t,z)=\chi(z)H(\lambda,t,z)
. Then
it is easily computed that for any
\xi,\eta\in\mathbb{R}^{2n}
,

\displaystyle(\nabla^{2}_{z}\tilde{H}({\lambda,t},z)\xi,\eta)_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(H_{\lambda,t})^{\prime}(z)[\xi]\chi^{\prime}(z)[\eta]+\chi(z)(\nabla^{2}_{z}H(\lambda,t,z)\xi,\eta)_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}

\displaystyle+\chi^{\prime}(z)[\xi](H_{\lambda,t})^{\prime}(z)[\eta]+H(\lambda,t,z)(\chi^{\prime\prime}(z)\xi,\eta)_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}.

Since
H
and
\nabla_{z}H
are continuous by Assumption
1.1
, it follows from the compactness of
\Lambda
that there exists a constant
C^{\prime}>0
such that

-C^{\prime}I_{2n}\leq\nabla^{2}_{z}\tilde{H}(\lambda,t,z)\leq C^{\prime}I_{2n},\quad\forall(\lambda,t,z)\in\Lambda\times[0,\tau]\times\mathbb{R}^{2n}.

Note for some small
\epsilon>0
that
\|u\|_{1,2}\leq\epsilon
implies
u([0,\tau])\subset U_{0}
, and
that we are only concerned with solutions of (
1.3
) near
0\in W_{M}^{1,2}([0,\tau];\mathbb{R}^{n})
.
Replacing
H
by
\tilde{H}
we can assume
that
H
satisfies

-CI_{2n}\leq\nabla^{2}_{z}H(\lambda,t,z)\leq CI_{2n},\quad\forall(\lambda,t,z)\in\Lambda\times[0,\tau]\times\mathbb{R}^{2n}.

(3.3)

For a given
(\lambda,t,z)\in\Lambda\times[0,\tau]\times\mathbb{R}^{2n}
, using Taylor expansion we have
\theta\in(0,1)
such that

\displaystyle H(\lambda,t,z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle H(\lambda,t,0)+(\nabla_{z}H(\lambda,t,0),z)_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}+\frac{1}{2}\big(\nabla_{z}^{2}H(\lambda,t,\theta z)z,z\big)_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle H(\lambda,t,0)+\frac{1}{2}\big(\nabla^{2}_{z}H(\lambda,t,\theta z)z,z\big)_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}.

Since
(\lambda,t)\to H(\lambda,t,0)
is continuous,
and hence bounded, from the above expression it follows that there exist constants
c^{\prime}_{1}>0,c^{\prime}_{2}>0
such that

-c^{\prime}_{1}|z|^{2}-c^{\prime}_{2}\leq H(\lambda,t,z)\leq c^{\prime}_{1}|z|^{2}+c^{\prime}_{2},\quad\forall(\lambda,t,z)\in\Lambda\times[0,\tau]\times\mathbb{R}^{2n}.

(3.4)

Proof of Theorem
1.4
(I).

By the above arguments we can also assume that
H
satisfies
(
3.3
) and (
3.4
). Then we may choose
\kappa<0
,

,

such that

(i)

\det(e^{\tau\kappa J}M-I_{2n})\neq 0
;

(ii)

each
H_{\kappa}(\lambda,t,z):=H(\lambda,t,z)-\frac{\kappa}{2}|z|^{2}
satisfies

c_{1}I_{2n}\leq\nabla^{2}_{z}H_{\kappa}(\lambda,t,z)\leq c_{2}I_{2n},\quad\forall(\lambda,t,z);

(3.5)

(iii)

c_{1}|z|^{2}-c_{3}\leq H_{\kappa}(\lambda,t,z)\leq c_{2}|z|^{2}+c_{3}
for all
(\lambda,t,z)
.

Let
(H_{\kappa})^{\ast}(\lambda,t;z)=(H_{\kappa}(\lambda,t,\cdot))^{\ast}(z)
. These and Assumption
1.1
imply:

(iv)

For
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{2n}
and
\xi^{\ast}=\nabla_{z}H_{\kappa}(\lambda,t,\xi)

\xi=\nabla_{z}(H_{\kappa})^{\ast}(\lambda,t;\xi^{\ast})\quad\hbox{and}\quad I_{2n}=\nabla^{2}_{z}(H_{\kappa})^{\ast}(\lambda,t;\xi^{\ast})\nabla^{2}_{z}H_{\kappa}(\lambda,t,\xi).

(3.6)

(v)

Because of (

), it holds for all
(\lambda,t,z)
that

\frac{1}{c_{2}}I_{2n}\leq\nabla_{z}^{2}(H_{\kappa})^{\ast}(\lambda,t;z)\leq\frac{1}{c_{1}}I_{2n}.

(3.7)

(vi)

(H_{\kappa})^{\ast}:\Lambda\times[0,\tau]\times{{\mathbb{R}}}^{2n}\to{\mathbb{R}}

also satisfies Assumption
1.1
, that is, it
is a continuous function such that each

,
(\lambda,t)\in\Lambda\times[0,\tau]
, is

and all possible partial derivatives of it depend continuously on

(These may follow from (

) and the implicit function theorem.)

(vii)

\frac{1}{c_{2}}|z|^{2}-c_{3}\leq(H_{\kappa})^{\ast}(\lambda,t;z)\leq\frac{1}{c_{1}}|z|^{2}+c_{3}
for all
(\lambda,t,z)
.

By these and Corollary
2.13
the functionals
\Phi(\lambda,\cdot):W^{1,2}_{M}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})\to{\mathbb{R}}
defined by

\displaystyle\Phi(\lambda,v):=\int^{\tau}_{0}\left[\frac{1}{2}(J\dot{v}(t),v(t))_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}+H(\lambda,t,{v}(t))\right]dt

(3.8)

and
\Psi_{\kappa}(\lambda,\cdot):W^{1,2}_{M}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})\to{\mathbb{R}}
defined by

\Psi_{\kappa}(\lambda,v)=\int^{\tau}_{0}\left[\frac{1}{2}(J\dot{v}(t)+\kappa v(t),v(t))_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}+(H_{\kappa})^{\ast}(\lambda,t;-J\dot{v}(t)-\kappa v(t))\right]dt,

(3.9)

are
C^{1}
and twice Gâteaux-differentiable, and have same critical point sets,
which exactly correspond to solutions of (

) with the parameter value
\lambda
.
Moreover,
\Phi(\lambda,u)=-\Psi_{\kappa}(\lambda,u)
for any critical point

of them.
As noted in Remark
2.12
,
(i) also implies that

\Lambda_{M,\tau,\kappa I_{2n}}:W^{1,2}_{M}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})\to L^{2}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})

(3.10)

is a Banach space isomorphism, and thus the functional

\psi_{\kappa}(\lambda,\cdot)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\rm def}}{{=}}\Psi_{\kappa}(\lambda,\cdot)\circ(-\Lambda_{M,\tau,\kappa I_{2n}})^{-1}:L^{2}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})\to{\mathbb{R}}

given by

\psi_{\kappa}(\lambda,u)=\int^{\tau}_{0}\left[\frac{1}{2}(u(t),((\Lambda_{M,\tau,\kappa I_{2n}})^{-1}u)(t))_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}+(H_{\kappa})^{\ast}(\lambda,t;u(t))\right]dt

(3.11)

has the same analytical properties as those of
\Psi_{\kappa}(\lambda,\cdot)
. In particular,
\psi_{\kappa}(\lambda,\cdot)

is
C^{1}
and twice Gâteaux-differentiable.

Let us prove that
[
50
, Theorem 3.1]
(Theorem
C.6
) is applicable to

\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}(\cdot)=\psi_{\kappa}(\lambda,\cdot),\quad H=L^{2}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n}),\quad X=L^{2}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})

(thus any Banach space
X
which is dense in
H=L^{2}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})
).
To this end we define

A_{M,\tau,\kappa}:L^{2}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})\to L^{2}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n}),\;u\mapsto\iota\circ(\Lambda_{M,\tau,{\kappa I_{2n}}})^{-1}u,

(3.12)

where

\iota:W^{1,2}_{M}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})\to L^{2}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})
is the inclusion. By (
2.6
)

[({\Lambda}_{M,\tau,\kappa I_{2n}})^{-1}u](t)=\Upsilon_{\kappa I_{2n}}(t)\mathfrak{J}^{-1}\int^{\tau}_{0}\Upsilon_{\kappa I_{2n}}(t)^{-1}Ju(t)dt-\Upsilon_{\kappa I_{2n}}(t)\int^{t}_{0}\Upsilon_{\kappa I_{2n}}(s)^{-1}Ju(s)ds.

(3.13)

Then
A_{M,\tau,\kappa}
is a compact self-adjoint operator.
It is easily proved that
\psi_{\kappa}(\lambda,\cdot)
has the gradient

\nabla_{v}\psi_{\kappa}(\lambda,v)=A_{M,\tau,\kappa}v+\nabla_{z}(H_{\kappa})^{\ast}(\lambda,\cdot;v(\cdot))

(3.14)

and that
L^{2}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})\ni v\mapsto\nabla_{v}\psi_{\kappa}(\lambda,v)\in L^{2}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})

has a Gâteaux derivative

B_{\lambda}(v):=D_{v}\nabla_{v}\psi_{\kappa}(\lambda,v)=A_{M,\tau,\kappa}+\nabla^{2}_{z}(H_{\kappa}^{\ast})(\lambda,\cdot;v(\cdot))\in\mathscr{L}_{s}(L^{2}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})).

(3.15)

Denote by

P_{\lambda}(v)=\nabla^{2}_{z}(H_{\kappa})^{\ast}(\lambda,\cdot,v(\cdot))\quad\hbox{and}\quad Q_{\lambda}(v)=A_{M,\tau,\kappa}.

Both are in
\mathscr{L}_{s}(L^{2}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n}))
.
Clearly,
Q_{\lambda}
satisfies (iii)-(iv) of
[
50
, Theorem 3.1]
.
By (
3.7
),

P_{\lambda}(v)\in\mathscr{L}_{s}(L^{2}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n}))
is uniformly positive definite with respect to
(\lambda,v)
, i.e., it satisfies
(ii) of
[
50
, Theorem 3.1]
.

We also need to prove that
P_{\lambda}
satisfies (i) of
[
50
, Theorem 3.1]
,
that is, for any
h\in L^{2}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})
there holds

\displaystyle\|P_{\lambda_{k}}(v_{k})h-P_{\mu}(0)h\|_{2}^{2}=\int^{\tau}_{0}|\nabla^{2}_{z}(H_{\kappa})^{\ast}(\lambda_{k},t;v_{k}(t))h(t)-\nabla_{z}^{2}(H_{\kappa})^{\ast}(\mu,t;0)h(t)|^{2}dt\to 0

provided that
(v_{k})\subset L^{2}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})

and
(\lambda_{k})\subset\Lambda
converge to
0\in L^{2}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})
and
\mu\in\Lambda
, respectively.
Arguing indirectly, we can assume after passing subsequences if necessary that

v_{k}\to 0
a.e., and

\|P_{\lambda_{k}}(v_{k})h-P_{\mu}(0)h\|_{2}\geq\varepsilon_{0}
for some
\varepsilon_{0}>0
and all

.

(3.16)

By the above (iv) the map

(\lambda,t,z)\mapsto\nabla_{z}^{2}(H_{\kappa}^{\ast})(\lambda,t;z)

is continuous, and thus

\nabla_{z}^{2}(H_{\kappa})^{\ast}(\lambda_{k},t;v_{k}(t))h(t)\to\nabla_{z}^{2}(H_{\kappa})^{\ast}(\mu,t;0)h(t)
almost everywhere.

Observe that (
3.7
) implies

\displaystyle|\nabla^{2}_{z}(H_{\kappa})^{\ast}(\lambda_{k},t;v_{k}(t))h(t)-\nabla^{2}_{z}(H_{\kappa})^{\ast}(\mu,t;0)h(t)|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 4(|\nabla_{z}^{2}(H_{\kappa})^{\ast}(\lambda_{k},t;v_{k}(t))h(t)|^{2}+|\nabla^{2}_{z}(H_{\kappa})^{\ast}(\mu,t;0)h(t)|^{2})\leq\frac{8}{c_{1}^{2}}|h(t)|^{2}.

Using Lebesgue dominated convergence theorem we deduce

\displaystyle\lim_{k\to\infty}\int^{\tau}_{0}|\nabla^{2}_{z}(H_{\kappa})^{\ast}(\lambda_{k},t;v_{k}(t))h(t)-\nabla_{z}^{2}(H_{\kappa})^{\ast}(\mu,t;0)h(t)|^{2}dt=0.

This contradicts (
3.16
). The desired claim is proved.

Since
(\mu,0)\in\Lambda\times W^{1,2}_{M}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})
is a bifurcation point along sequences of
the problem (
1.3
) if and only if it is that of solutions of

\nabla_{u}\Phi(\lambda,u)=0
(or equivalently
\nabla_{u}\Psi_{\kappa}(\lambda,u)=0
)
in
\Lambda\times W^{1,2}_{M}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})
, we deduce that
(\mu,0)\in\Lambda\times W^{1,2}_{M}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})
is a bifurcation point along sequences
of the problem (
1.3
) if and only if
(\mu,0)\in\Lambda\times L^{2}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})

is a bifurcation point along sequences for solutions of
\nabla_{w}\psi_{\kappa}(\lambda,w)=0

in
\Lambda\times L^{2}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})
.

It follows from the assumption of Theorem

(I) and
[

(Theorem
C.6
) that
0\in L^{2}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})
is a degenerate critical point of

and therefore

by (

).
Note that
\gamma_{\mu}=\Upsilon_{\nabla_{z}^{2}H(\mu,\cdot,0)}
by the definition of

. Hence

\displaystyle\nu_{\tau,M}(\gamma_{\mu})=\dim{\rm Ker}(\gamma_{\mu}(\tau)-M)=\dim{\rm Ker}(\Upsilon_{\nabla^{2}_{z}H(\mu,\cdot,0)}(\tau)-M)>0.

Note
:

(II).

Proof of Theorem
1.4
(II).

The arguments before the final paragraph in the proof of Theorem
1.4
(I)
are still valid.
Clearly, the isomorphism
\Lambda_{M,\tau,\kappa I_{2n}}
in (
3.10
)
gives rise to a Banach space isomorphism from
C^{1}_{M}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})
onto
C^{0}_{M}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})
,
denoted by
\Lambda_{M,\tau,\kappa I_{2n}}^{c}
for the sake of clearness.
If
w\in C^{0}_{M}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})
then
u:=A_{M,\tau,\kappa}w
belongs to

u\in W^{1,2}_{M}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})
and satisfies
J\dot{u}+\kappa u=\Lambda_{M,\tau,\kappa I_{2n}}u=w
.
It follows that
J\dot{u}=w-u\in C^{0}_{M}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})
and hence

u\in C^{1}_{M}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})
.

Let us prove in five steps that the family

\{\mathcal{L}_{\lambda}(\cdot)=\psi_{\kappa}(\lambda,\cdot)\,|\,\lambda\in\Lambda\}

satisfies the conditions of Theorem
C.4

with
\lambda^{\ast}=\mu
,
H=L^{2}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})
and
X=C^{0}_{M}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})
except for the condition that
{\rm Ker}(B_{\lambda^{\ast}}(0))\neq\{0\}
.

Step 1
(
Prove
\psi_{\kappa}
to be continuous
). By a contradiction we assume that

\psi_{\kappa}
is not continuous at some point
(\bar{\lambda},\bar{u})\in\Lambda\times L^{2}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})
.
Since
\Lambda\times L^{2}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})
is first countable, there exists
\varepsilon>0
and a sequence
\{(\lambda_{k},u_{k})\}^{\infty}_{k=1}
in
\Lambda\times L^{2}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})
converging to

(\bar{\lambda},\bar{u})
such that

|\psi_{\kappa}({\lambda_{k}},{u}_{k})-\psi_{\kappa}(\bar{\lambda},\bar{u})|\geq\varepsilon,\quad\forall k=1,2,\cdots.

(3.17)

By (vii) in the proof of Theorem
1.4
(I) we have

|H^{\ast}_{\kappa}(\lambda,t;z)|\leq\frac{1}{c_{1}}|z|^{2}+c_{3}\quad\forall(\lambda,t,z).

(3.18)

Since
H_{\kappa}^{\ast}:\Lambda\times[0,\tau]\times{{\mathbb{R}}}^{2n}\to{\mathbb{R}}
is continuous,
it follows from Lebesgue dominated convergence theorem that

\lim_{\lambda\to\bar{\lambda}}\int^{\tau}_{0}H_{\kappa}^{\ast}(\lambda,t;\bar{u}(t))dt=\int^{\tau}_{0}H_{\kappa}^{\ast}(\bar{\lambda},t;\bar{u}(t))dt.

Moreover, (
3.18
) and
[
51
, Proposition C.1]
imply that maps

L^{2}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})\to L^{1}([0,\tau]),\;u\mapsto H_{\kappa}^{\ast}(\lambda,\cdot;u(\cdot))

are uniformly continuous at
\bar{u}
with respect to
\lambda\in\Lambda^{\ast}:=\{\bar{\lambda},\lambda_{k}\,|\,k\in\mathbb{N}\}
. Then
we have a neighborhood
\Lambda_{0}
of
\bar{\lambda}
in
\Lambda
and a natural number
N
such that

\displaystyle\left|\int^{\tau}_{0}H_{\kappa}^{\ast}(\lambda,t;\bar{u}(t))dt-\int^{\tau}_{0}H_{\kappa}^{\ast}(\bar{\lambda},t;\bar{u}(t))dt\right|<\frac{\varepsilon}{4},\quad\forall\lambda\in\Lambda_{0},

\displaystyle\left|\int^{\tau}_{0}H_{\kappa}^{\ast}(\lambda_{k},t;{u}_{k}(t))dt-\int^{\tau}_{0}H_{\kappa}^{\ast}({\lambda}_{k},t;\bar{u}(t))dt\right|<\frac{\varepsilon}{4}\quad\forall k>N,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\left|\int^{\tau}_{0}(u_{k}(t),((\Lambda_{M,\tau,\kappa I_{2n}})^{-1}u_{k})(t))_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}dt-\int^{\tau}_{0}(\bar{u}(t),((\Lambda_{M,\tau,\kappa I_{2n}})^{-1}\bar{u})(t))_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}dt\right|<\frac{\varepsilon}{4}

if
k>N
.
We can assume
\lambda_{k}\in\Lambda_{0}
for all
k>N
. It follows from these and (
3.11
) that

|\psi_{\kappa}({\lambda}_{k},{u}_{k})-\psi_{\kappa}(\bar{\lambda},\bar{u})|<\varepsilon,\quad\forall k>N.

This contradicts (
3.18
).
Hence
\{\mathcal{L}_{\lambda}\,|\,\lambda\in\Lambda\}
is a continuous family.

Step 2
. By (
3.6
)-(
3.7
) and (
3.14
)
it is easily seen that
\nabla_{v}\psi_{\kappa}(\lambda,v)\in X
for each
v\in X
.
Thus

\mathscr{A}:\Lambda\times X\to X,\;(\lambda,v)\mapsto\nabla_{v}\psi_{\kappa}(\lambda,v)=A_{M,\tau,\kappa}v+\nabla_{z}H_{\kappa}^{\ast}(\lambda,\cdot;v(\cdot))

is well-defined. By a contradiction we assume that
\mathscr{A}
is not continuous at some point
(\bar{\lambda},\bar{w})\in\Lambda\times X
.
Then there exists
\varepsilon>0
and a sequence
\{(\lambda_{k},u_{k})\}^{\infty}_{k=1}
in
\Lambda\times X
converging to

(\bar{\lambda},\bar{u})
such that

\|\mathscr{A}({\lambda_{k}},{u}_{k})-\mathscr{A}(\bar{\lambda},\bar{u})\|_{C^{0}}\geq\varepsilon,\quad\forall k=1,2,\cdots.

(3.19)

(Here we have also used the first countability of
\Lambda
and
X
.)
By (
3.10
) and (
3.12
) we deduce that
\|A_{M,\tau,\kappa}u_{k}-A_{M,\tau,\kappa}\bar{u}\|_{C^{0}}\to 0
as
k\to\infty
.
Therefore there exists a natural number
N
such that
\|A_{M,\tau,\kappa}u_{k}-A_{M,\tau,\kappa}\bar{u}\|_{C^{0}}<\varepsilon/2
for all
k\geq N
.
It follows from this and (
3.19
) that

\|\nabla_{z}H_{\kappa}^{\ast}(\lambda_{k},\cdot;{u}_{k}(\cdot))-\nabla_{z}H_{\kappa}^{\ast}(\bar{\lambda},\cdot;\bar{u}(\cdot))\|_{C^{0}}\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{2},\quad\forall k\geq N.

(3.20)

These mean that there exists a sequence
\{t_{k}\}_{k=N}^{\infty}
in
[0,\tau]
such that

|\nabla_{z}H_{\kappa}^{\ast}(\lambda_{k},t_{k};{u}_{k}(t_{k}))-\nabla_{z}H_{\kappa}^{\ast}(\bar{\lambda},t_{k};\bar{u}(t_{k}))|_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{2},\quad\forall k\geq N.

(3.21)

Passing to a subsequence we can assume
t_{k}\to\bar{t}\in[0,\tau]
. Since
\nabla_{z}H_{\kappa}^{\ast}(\lambda,t;z)
is continuous and

\displaystyle|{u}_{k}(t_{k})-\bar{u}(\bar{t})|_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle|{u}_{k}(t_{k})-\bar{u}(t_{k})|_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}+|\bar{u}({t}_{k})-\bar{u}(\bar{t})|_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|{u}_{k}-\bar{u}\|_{C^{0}}+|\bar{u}({t}_{k})-\bar{u}(\bar{t})|_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}\to 0,

it follows from (
3.21
) that

0=|\nabla_{z}H_{\kappa}^{\ast}(\bar{\lambda},\bar{t};\bar{u}(\bar{t}))-\nabla_{z}H_{\kappa}^{\ast}(\bar{\lambda},\bar{t};\bar{u}(\bar{t}))|_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}
.
This contradiction shows that
\mathscr{A}
is continuous.

Step 3
. We claim that
\mathscr{A}_{\lambda}
is
C^{1}
.
In fact, as in the proof of Proposition
2.10
we obtain that

\mathscr{A}_{\lambda}(\cdot):=\mathscr{A}(\lambda,\cdot)
has the Gâteaux derivative at
w\in X
,

D\mathscr{A}_{\lambda}(w):X\to\mathscr{L}(X),\;\xi\mapsto\nabla_{z}^{2}(H_{\kappa})^{\ast}(\lambda,\cdot;w(\cdot))\xi(\cdot).

For any given
w\in X
, since
(t,z)\mapsto\nabla^{2}_{z}(H_{\kappa})^{\ast}(\lambda,t;z)
is uniformly continuous
on a compact neighborhood of
[0,\tau]\times w([0,\tau])
in
[0,\tau]\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2n}
, using the inequality

\displaystyle\|D\mathscr{A}_{\lambda}(u)-D\mathscr{A}_{\lambda}(w)\|_{\mathscr{L}(X)}\leq\max_{0\leq t\leq\tau}\|\nabla_{z}^{2}(H_{\kappa})^{\ast}(\lambda,t;u(t))-\nabla_{z}^{2}(H_{\kappa})^{\ast}(\lambda,t;w(t))\|_{\mathbb{R}^{2n\times 2n}}

for
u\in X
we can deduce that
\|D\mathscr{A}_{\lambda}(u)-D\mathscr{A}_{\lambda}(w)\|_{\mathscr{L}(X)}\to 0

as
\|u-w\|_{C^{0}}\to 0
and therefore that
\mathscr{A}_{\lambda}
is
C^{1}
.

Step 4
. For
B_{\lambda}(v)
given by (
3.15
) we claim

\{u\in H\,|\,{B}_{\lambda}(0)u=su,\,s\leq 0\}\subset X\quad\hbox{and}\quad\{u\in H\,|\,{B}_{\lambda}(0)u\in X\}\subset X.

(3.22)

That is, both (D1) in
[
50
, Hypothesis 1.1]
and (C) in

[
50
, Hypothesis 1.3]
hold with
B_{\lambda}(0)
.

In fact, suppose that
u\in H

and
\varrho\leq 0
satisfy
B_{\lambda}(0)u=\varrho u
.
Then

(A_{M,\tau,\kappa}u)(t)+\nabla^{2}_{z}(H_{\kappa})^{\ast}(\lambda,t;0)u(t)=\varrho u(t).

By (
3.7
),
\nabla^{2}_{z}(H_{\kappa})^{\ast}(\lambda,t;0)-\varrho I_{2n}\geq\frac{1}{c_{2}}I_{2n}

and so
\nabla^{2}_{z}(H_{\kappa})^{\ast}(\lambda,t;0)-\varrho I_{2n}
is invertible.
Since

A_{M,\tau,\kappa}u=\iota\circ(\Lambda_{M,\tau,\kappa I_{2n}})^{-1}u=(\Lambda_{M,\tau,\kappa I_{2n}})^{-1}u\in W^{1,2}_{M}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n}),

we deduce that
u(t)=-[\nabla^{2}_{z}(H_{\kappa})^{\ast}(\lambda,t;0)-\varrho I_{2n}]^{-1}(A_{M,\tau,\kappa I_{2n}}u)(t)
is continuous, i.e.,
u\in X
.
(Actually, this is unnecessary in the condition (D) in
[
50
, Hypothesis 1.1]
).

In order to prove the second inclusion in (
3.22
)
let
u\in H
be such that
v:=B_{\lambda}(0)u\in X=C^{0}_{M}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})
. Then

(A_{M,\tau,\kappa}u)(t)+\nabla^{2}_{z}(H_{\kappa})^{\ast}(\lambda,t;0)u(t)=v(t).

By (
3.10
),
A_{M,\tau,\kappa}u\in W^{1,2}_{M}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})\subset X
.
This implies that
u\in X
since
t\mapsto\nabla^{2}_{z}(H_{\kappa})^{\ast}(\lambda,t;0)
is continuous and

\nabla^{2}_{z}(H_{\kappa})^{\ast}(\lambda,t;0)
is invertible.

Step 5
.
In the proof of Theorem
1.4
(I) we have proved that

P_{\lambda}(v)=\nabla^{2}_{z}(H_{\kappa})^{\ast}(\lambda,\cdot;v(\cdot))
and
Q_{\lambda}(v)=A_{M,\tau,\kappa}
satisfy
the conditions (i), (ii), (iii) and (iv) in
Theorem
C.4
with
\lambda^{\ast}=\mu
.
Combing these with the above Steps 3, 4 we see that
Hypothesis
C.3
and so the condition (v) in Theorem
C.4
with
\lambda^{\ast}=\mu
is satisfied.
Because the condition
{\rm Ker}(B_{\lambda^{\ast}}(0))\neq\{0\}
is not used in the proofs above
we have actually showed that Hypothesis
C.3
is satisfied with any
\lambda\in\Lambda
.

As in the final paragraph in the proof of Theorem
1.4
,
it follows from (
2.32
) and (
2.33
) that the Morse index and nullity of

\mathcal{L}_{{\lambda}}
at
0\in H
are

\displaystyle m^{-}_{\lambda}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle m^{-}({\psi}_{\kappa}(\lambda,\cdot),0)=i_{\tau,M}(\gamma_{\lambda})-i_{\tau,M}(\Upsilon_{\kappa I_{2n}})-\nu_{\tau,M}(\Upsilon_{\kappa I_{2n}}),

(3.23)

\displaystyle m^{0}_{\lambda}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle m^{0}({\psi}_{\kappa}(\lambda,\cdot),0)=\nu_{\tau,M}(\gamma_{\lambda})=\dim{\rm Ker}(\gamma_{\lambda}(\tau)-M),

(3.24)

respectively, where
\Upsilon_{\kappa I_{2n}}(t)=\exp({t\kappa J})
.
Let
\Xi=-i_{\tau,M}(\Upsilon_{\kappa I_{2n}})-\nu_{\tau,M}(\Upsilon_{\kappa I_{2n}})
.
Under the assumptions in Theorem
1.4
(II), for each
k\in\mathbb{N}
we derive from (
3.23
)-(
3.24
)

\displaystyle[m^{-}_{\lambda_{k}^{-}},m^{-}_{\lambda_{k}^{-}}+m^{0}_{\lambda_{k}^{-}}]\cap[m^{-}_{\lambda_{k}^{+}},m^{-}_{\lambda_{k}^{+}}+m^{0}_{\lambda_{k}^{+}}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[i_{\tau,M}(\gamma_{\lambda_{k}^{-}})+\Xi,i_{\tau,M}(\gamma_{\lambda_{k}^{-}})+\nu_{\tau,M}(\gamma_{\lambda_{k}^{-}})+\Xi]\cap[i_{\tau,M}(\gamma_{\lambda_{k}^{+}})+\Xi,i_{\tau,M}(\gamma_{\lambda_{k}^{+}})+\nu_{\tau,M}(\gamma_{\lambda_{k}^{+}})+\Xi]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Xi+[i_{\tau,M}(\gamma_{\lambda_{k}^{-}}),i_{\tau,M}(\gamma_{\lambda_{k}^{-}})+\nu_{\tau,M}(\gamma_{\lambda_{k}^{-}})]\cap[i_{\tau,M}(\gamma_{\lambda_{k}^{+}}),i_{\tau,M}(\gamma_{\lambda_{k}^{+}})+\nu_{\tau,M}(\gamma_{\lambda_{k}^{+}})]=\emptyset

and either
m^{0}_{\lambda_{k}^{+}}=\nu_{\tau,M}(\gamma_{\lambda_{k}^{+}})=0
or
m^{0}_{\lambda_{k}^{-}}=\nu_{\tau,M}(\gamma_{\lambda_{k}^{-}})=0
.
Hence from the conclusion (B) of Theorem
C.4
we immediately conclude that
there exists a sequence
\{(\lambda_{k},w_{k})\}_{k\geq 1}
in
\hat{\Lambda}\times L^{2}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})
converging to

(\mu,0)
such that each
w_{k}\neq 0
and satisfies
\nabla_{w}\psi_{\kappa}(\lambda_{k},w_{k})=0
,
k=1,2,\cdots
. Then

u_{k}:=-(\Lambda_{M,\tau,\kappa I_{2n}})^{-1}w_{k}+u_{\lambda_{k}}\in W^{1,2}_{M}([0,\tau];\mathbb{R}^{2n}),\quad k=1,2,\cdots,

satisfy (
1.3
) and
0<\|u_{k}-u_{\lambda_{k}}\|_{1,2}=\|(\Lambda_{M,\tau,\kappa I_{2n}})^{-1}w_{k}\|_{1,2}\to 0

as
k\to\infty
. By Proposition
1.3
(i) [or its proof]
we obtain that
u_{k}\in C^{1}_{M}([0,\tau];\mathbb{R}^{2n})
and
0<\|u_{k}-u_{\lambda_{k}}\|_{C^{1}}\to 0
.
The required conclusions are proved.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.4
(III).

Recall that we have assumed
\Lambda=\alpha([0,1])
.
From the proof of Theorem
1.4
(II) it is easily seen that the conditions of Theorem
C.5

are satisfied. Then there exists a sequence
\{(t_{k},w_{k})\}_{k\geq 1}\subset[0,1]\times L^{(}[0,\tau];\mathbb{R}^{2n})
such that

\bullet

t_{k}\to\bar{t}
and
0<\|w_{k}\|_{2}\to 0
,

\bullet

each
w_{k}
satisfies
\nabla_{w}\psi_{\kappa}(\alpha(t_{k}),w_{k})=0
,
k=1,2,\cdots
,

\bullet

\alpha(\bar{t})
is not equal to
\lambda^{+}
(resp.
\lambda^{-}
) if
m^{0}_{\lambda^{+}}=0
(resp.
m^{0}_{\lambda^{-}}=0
),
where
m^{0}_{\lambda}
is given by (
3.24
).

As above we define
u^{k}:=-(\Lambda_{M,\tau,\kappa I_{2n}})^{-1}w_{k}+u_{\alpha(t_{k})}\in W^{1,2}_{M}([0,\tau];\mathbb{R}^{2n})
,

k=1,2,\cdots
, and using Proposition
1.3
(i) [or its proof] it is easily checked that they satisfy the required conclusions.
∎

Remark 3.3
.

Even if
(\Lambda,M,\tau)=(I,I_{2n},2\pi)
, where
I
is an interval containing
\mu
as an interior point,
we cannot still guarantee that
\psi_{\kappa}
is
C^{1}
. Therefore the conclusion cannot be derived from

[
8
, Theorem 4.6]
(a result due to Mawhin and Willem
[
57
, Theorem 8.9]
).

Proof of Theorem
1.7
.

Follow the notations in the proofs of Theorems
1.4
.
But now the parameter space
\Lambda
is a real interval. Comparing the conditions in Theorem
C.4

with those of
[
52
, Theorem 3.6]

we have actually checked in the proof of Theorem
1.4
(II)
that
\mathcal{L}_{\lambda}(\cdot)=\psi_{\kappa}(\lambda,\cdot)
with
\lambda^{\ast}=\mu
,
H=L^{2}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})

and
X=C^{0}_{M}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})
satisfy conditions in
[
52
, Theorem 3.6]
(Theorem
C.7
)
except for the condition (f).

We claim that the latter can be also satisfied.
In fact, by the assumptions of Theorem
1.7
and (
3.23
)-(
3.24
),
m^{0}({\psi}_{\kappa}(\mu,\cdot),0)>0

and
m^{0}({\psi}_{\kappa}(\lambda,\cdot),0)=0

if
\lambda\in\Lambda\setminus\{\mu\}
is close to
\mu
. Moreover,

m^{-}({\psi}_{\kappa}(\lambda,\cdot),0)
takes, respectively, values
m^{-}({\psi}_{\kappa}(\mu,\cdot),0)
and

m^{-}({\psi}_{\kappa}(\mu,\cdot),0)+m^{0}({\psi}_{\kappa}(\mu,\cdot),0)

as
\lambda\in\Lambda
varies in two deleted half neighborhoods of
\mu
.

Then by Theorem
C.7
(
[
52
, Theorem 3.6]
), one of the following assertions holds:

(i)

(\mu,0)
is not an isolated solution in
\{\mu\}\times L^{2}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})

of
\nabla_{w}\psi_{\kappa}(\lambda,w)=0
.

(ii)

For each
\lambda\in\Lambda\setminus\{\mu\}
near
\mu
,

\nabla_{w}\psi_{\kappa}(\lambda,w)=0
has a solution
w_{\lambda}\in C^{0}_{M}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})
different from
0
,
which converges to
0
in
C^{0}_{M}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})
as
\lambda\to\mu
.

(iii)

For a given neighborhood
\mathcal{U}
of
0
in
C^{0}_{M}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})
there is an one-sided neighborhood
\Lambda^{0}
of
\mu
such that for any
\lambda\in\Lambda^{0}\setminus\{\mu\}
,
\nabla_{w}\psi_{\kappa}(\lambda,w)=0

has at least two distinct nonzero solutions
w_{\lambda}^{1}
and
w_{\lambda}^{2}
in
\mathcal{U}
,
which can also be required to satisfy
\psi_{\kappa}(\lambda,w_{\lambda}^{1})\neq\psi_{\kappa}(\lambda,w_{\lambda}^{2})

provided that
\nu_{\tau,M}(\gamma_{\mu})>1
and
\nabla_{w}\psi_{\kappa}(\lambda,w)=0

has only finitely many solutions in
\mathcal{U}
.

Since the Banach space isomorphism
-\Lambda_{M,\tau,\kappa I_{2n}}^{c}:C^{1}_{M}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})\to C^{0}_{M}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})

maps the set of solutions of
\nabla_{u}\Psi_{\kappa}(\lambda,u)=0

in
C^{1}_{M}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})
onto that of
\nabla_{w}\psi_{\kappa}(\lambda,w)=0
in
C^{0}_{M}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})
, and
for each critical point
w
of
\psi_{\kappa}(\lambda,\cdot)
it holds that

\psi_{\kappa}(\lambda,w)=\Psi_{\kappa}\left(\lambda,(-\Lambda_{M,\tau,\kappa I_{2n}}^{c})^{-1}w\right)=-\Phi\left(\lambda,(-\Lambda_{M,\tau,\kappa I_{2n}}^{c})^{-1}w\right),

the conclusions in the first part of Theorem
1.7
follow from these and Proposition
1.3
(i)
as in the proof of Theorem
1.4
(III).

For the part of “Moreover”, note that the Banach space isomorphism

\Lambda_{M,\tau,\kappa I_{2n}}
is equivariant with respect to
the natural actions of
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}=\{id,-id\}
on spaces

W^{1,2}_{M}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n}),\quad L^{2}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n}),\quad C^{1}_{M}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})\quad\hbox{and}\quad C^{0}_{M}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})

and that functionals

\Psi_{\kappa}(\lambda,\cdot)
and
\psi_{\kappa}(\lambda,\cdot)
in (
3.9
) and (
3.11
)
are invariant under the
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
-action, i.e., even.
Comparing the conditions in Theorem
C.7
(
[
52
, Theorem 3.6]
or
[
50
, Theorem 4.6]
) with those of
Theorem
C.8
(
[
52
, Theorem 3.7]
or
[
50
, Theorem 5.12]
) we immediately use the latter and
[
52
, Remark 3.9]
(or
[
50
, Remark 5.14]
)
to obtain the desired claims.
∎

Proof of Corollary
1.9
.

If
(\mu,\bar{u})\in{\mathbb{R}}\times W^{1,2}_{M}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})

is a bifurcation point for (
1.3
) with
H(\lambda,t,z)=H_{0}(t,z)+\lambda\hat{H}(t,z)
and
\Lambda={\mathbb{R}}
,
then
\nu_{\tau,M}(\gamma_{\mu}(\tau))>0
by Theorem
1.4
(I).

Suppose now that
\nu_{\tau,M}(\gamma_{\mu}(\tau))>0
.
As done above the proof of Theorem
1.4
(I),
(changing a compact neighborhood
U
of
0
in
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
as a compact neighborhood
U
of

\bar{u}([0,\tau])
in
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
),
by modifying values of
H_{0}
and
\hat{H}
outside
an open neighborhood of
[0,\tau]\times\bar{u}([0,\tau])\subset[0,\tau]\times\mathbb{R}^{2n}
with a cutting-off technique we may assume that
H
satisfies

\displaystyle-CI_{2n}\leq\nabla_{z}^{2}H_{0}(t,z),\;\nabla_{z}^{2}\hat{H}(t,z)\leq CI_{2n},\quad\forall(t,z)\in[0,\tau]\times\mathbb{R}^{2n},

\displaystyle-c^{\prime}_{1}|z|^{2}-c^{\prime}_{2}\leq H_{0}(t,z),\;\hat{H}(t,z)\leq c^{\prime}_{1}|z|^{2}+c^{\prime}_{2},\quad\forall(t,z)\in[0,\tau]\times\mathbb{R}^{2n}

for some constants
C>0,c^{\prime}_{1}>0,c^{\prime}_{2}>0
. Therefore there exist constants
\kappa<0
and

c_{i}>0
,
i=1,2,3
such that (i)-(iii) in the proof of Theorem
1.4
(I)
are satisfied with

H_{\kappa}(\lambda,t,z):=H_{0}(t,z)+\lambda\hat{H}(t,z)-\frac{\kappa}{2}|z|^{2}

for all
(\lambda,t,z)\in[\mu-1,\mu+1]\times[0,\tau]\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2n}
. In particular, (
3.5
) implies

c_{1}I_{2n}\leq\nabla_{z}^{2}H_{0}(t,z)+\lambda\nabla_{z}^{2}\hat{H}(t,z)-\kappa I_{2n}\leq c_{2}I_{2n}

(3.25)

for all
(\lambda,t,z)\in[\mu-1,\mu+1]\times[0,\tau]\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2n}
.
Let
\bar{w}=-\Lambda_{M,\tau,\kappa I_{2n}}\bar{u}
and
\psi_{\kappa,\lambda}(u):=\psi_{\kappa}(\lambda,u)
be given by (
3.11
). Then
for all
\xi,\eta\in{L}^{2}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})
,

\displaystyle{\psi}^{\prime\prime}_{\kappa,\lambda}(\bar{w})[\xi,\eta]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int^{\tau}_{0}\Big[({A}_{M,\tau,\kappa}\xi)(t),\eta(t))_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}+([\nabla_{z}^{2}H_{0}(t,\bar{u}(t))+\lambda\nabla_{z}^{2}\hat{H}(t,\bar{u}(t))-\kappa I_{2n}]^{-1}\xi(t),\eta(t))_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}})\Big]dt

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2q_{M,B_{\lambda}|A}(\xi,\eta),

where
B_{\lambda}(t)=\nabla^{2}_{z}H_{0}(t,\bar{u}(t))+\lambda\nabla_{z}^{2}\hat{H}(t,\bar{u}(t))
,

A(t)=\kappa I_{2n}
, and
q_{M,B|A}
is as in (
A.24
).
By (
3.25
), for all
(\lambda,t)\in[\mu-1,\mu+1]\times[0,\tau]
we have

c_{1}I_{2n}\leq B_{\lambda}(t)-A(t)\leq c_{2}I_{2n}.

(3.26)

Since
\gamma_{\lambda}=\Upsilon_{B_{\lambda}}
,
it follows from (
A.28
) and (
A.26
) that

\left.\begin{array}[]{ll}m^{-}({\psi}_{\kappa,\lambda},\bar{w})=j_{\tau,M}(B_{\lambda}|A)\quad\hbox{and}\\
m^{0}({\psi}_{\kappa,\lambda},\bar{w})=\nu_{\tau,M}(B_{\lambda}|A)=\nu_{\tau,M}(B_{\lambda})=\nu_{\tau,M}(\gamma_{\lambda}).\end{array}\right\}

(3.27)

Firstly, let us assume
\nabla^{2}_{z}\hat{H}(t,\bar{u}(t))>0
for all
t\in[0,\tau]
. Then by (
3.26
) we get

B_{\lambda_{2}}>B_{\lambda_{1}}\geq A+c_{1}I_{2n}\quad\hbox{for any}\quad\mu-1\leq\lambda_{1}<\lambda_{2}\leq\mu+1.

Because of these we derive from (
A.29
) that

\displaystyle j_{\tau,M}(B_{\lambda_{2}}|A)\geq j_{\tau,M}(B_{\lambda_{1}}|A)+\nu_{\tau,M}(B_{\lambda_{1}}|A)

(3.28)

for any
\mu-1\leq\lambda_{1}<\lambda_{2}\leq\mu+1
.
By (
A.25
), both
j_{\tau,M}(B|A)
and
\nu_{\tau,M}(B|A)
are nonnegative integers. Hence
(
3.28
) implies that there exist at most finitely many points

\lambda\in[\mu-1,\mu+1]
where
\nu_{\tau,M}(B_{\lambda}|A)=\nu_{\tau,M}(B_{\lambda})=\nu_{\tau,M}(\Upsilon_{B_{\lambda}})=\nu_{\tau,M}(\gamma_{\lambda})\neq 0
.
It follows that
\{\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}\,|\,\nu_{\tau,M}(\gamma_{\lambda})>0\}
is a discrete set in
{\mathbb{R}}
.
The first claim is proved in the present case.

In order to prove (
1.9
) let us
take
\rho\in(0,1]
so small that
\nu_{\tau,M}(B_{\lambda}|A)=\nu_{\tau,M}(B_{\lambda})=0
for
\lambda\in[\mu-\rho,\mu+\rho]\setminus\{\mu\}
.
Since
\nabla_{z}^{2}\hat{H}(t,\bar{u}(t))>0\;\forall t\in[0,\tau]
, by (
A.30
) we get

\left.\begin{array}[]{ll}j_{\tau,M}(B_{\mu_{1}})\leq j_{\tau,M}(B_{\mu_{2}})\leq j_{\tau,M}(B_{\mu})&<j_{\tau,M}(B_{\mu})+\nu_{\tau,M}(B_{\mu})\\
&\leq j_{\tau,M}(B_{\lambda_{1}})\leq j_{\tau,M}(B_{\lambda_{2}})\end{array}\right\}

(3.29)

for any
\mu-\rho\leq\mu_{1}<\mu_{2}<\mu<\lambda_{1}<\lambda_{2}<\mu+\rho
.
Moreover, by (
A.28
) we can derive from

[
1
, Proposition 2.3.3]
that

B\mapsto j_{\tau,M}(B|A)\quad\hbox{and}\quad B\mapsto j_{\tau,M}(B|A)+\nu_{\tau,M}(B|A)

are lower-semi continuous and
upper-semi continuous, respectively. These and (
A.27
) imply that

B\mapsto i_{\tau,M}(B)\quad\hbox{and}\quad B\mapsto i_{\tau,M}(B)+\nu_{\tau,M}(B)

are lower-semi continuous and upper-semi continuous, respectively.
Using (
3.29
) again we deduce

\displaystyle j_{\tau,M}(B_{\mu_{1}})=j_{\tau,M}(B_{\mu})\;\;\forall\mu_{1}\in[\mu-\rho,\mu),

\displaystyle j_{\tau,M}(B_{\lambda_{1}})=j_{\tau,M}(B_{\lambda_{1}})+\nu_{\tau,M}(B_{\lambda_{1}})=j_{\tau,M}(B_{\mu})+\nu_{\tau,M}(B_{\mu})\;\;\forall\lambda_{1}\in(\mu,\mu+\rho].

By Theorem
A.2
, these lead to (
1.9
).

Similarly, if
\nabla_{z}^{2}\hat{H}(t,\bar{u}(t))<0\;\forall t\in[0,\tau]
, we may obtain reversed inequalities
to those in (
3.28
) and (
3.29
), and therefore the discreteness of
\Sigma
.
Then as above we have

\displaystyle j_{\tau,M}(B_{\mu_{1}})=j_{\tau,M}(B_{\mu})\;\;\forall\mu_{1}\in(\mu,\mu+\rho],

\displaystyle j_{\tau,M}(B_{\lambda_{1}})=j_{\tau,M}(B_{\lambda_{1}})+\nu_{\tau,M}(B_{\lambda_{1}})=j_{\tau,M}(B_{\mu})+\nu_{\tau,M}(B_{\mu})\;\;\forall\lambda_{1}\in[\mu-\rho,\mu)

and so (
1.9
).

Finally, other conclusions may follow from (
1.9
)-(
1.9
) and Theorem
1.7
.
∎

Proof of Corollary
1.10
.

Step 1
(
Prove
(I)).
Define
F:(0,1]\times[0,\tau]\times{{\mathbb{R}}}^{2n}\to{\mathbb{R}}
by

F(\lambda,t,z)=\lambda H(\lambda t,z).

It satisfies Assumption
1.1
and
\nabla_{z}F(\lambda,t,\bar{u})=0
for all
(\lambda,t)
.
Let

B_{\lambda}(t)=\nabla_{z}^{2}F(\lambda,t,\bar{u})=\lambda\nabla_{z}^{2}H(\lambda t,\bar{u})=\lambda B(\lambda t),

and let
\gamma_{\lambda}
be the fundamental matrix solution of

\dot{Z}=JB_{\lambda}(t)Z
on
[0,\tau]
. It is easily checked that
\gamma_{\lambda}(t)=\Upsilon_{B}(\lambda t)

for all
\lambda\in(0,1]
and
t\in[0,\tau]
.
Since
B(t)>0
for all
t\in[0,\tau]
,
by (
A.33
) we have

\displaystyle i_{\tau,M}(\Upsilon_{B})-i_{\tau,M}(\xi_{2n})-\dim{\rm Ker}(I_{2n}-M)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{0<s<\tau}\nu_{s,M}(\Upsilon_{B}|_{[0,s]})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{0<\lambda<1}\nu_{\lambda\tau,M}(\Upsilon_{B}|_{[0,\lambda\tau]})=\sum_{0<\lambda<1}\nu_{\tau,M}(\gamma_{\lambda}).

It follows this and the assumption
i_{\tau,M}(\Upsilon_{B})\neq i_{\tau,M}(\xi_{2n})+\dim{\rm Ker}(I_{2n}-M)
that

\{0<\lambda<1\,|\,\nu_{\tau,M}(\gamma_{\lambda})>0\}
is nonempty and only
consists of finitely many numbers
\lambda_{1}<\cdots<\lambda_{l}
.
Let
\tau_{k}=\lambda_{k}\tau
,
k=1,\cdots,l
. Then

\displaystyle\nu_{\tau_{k},M}(\Upsilon_{B}|_{[0,\tau_{k}]}):=\dim{\rm Ker}(\Upsilon_{B}(\tau_{k})-M)=\dim{\rm Ker}(\gamma_{\lambda_{k}}(\tau)-M)=\nu_{\tau,M}(\gamma_{{\lambda_{k}}})\neq 0

for
k=1,\cdots,l
.
Moreover, for each
\lambda\in(0,1]
we may derive from (
A.33
) that

\displaystyle i_{\tau,M}(\gamma_{\lambda})-i_{\tau,M}(\xi_{2n})-\dim{\rm Ker}(I_{2n}-M)=j_{\tau,M}(B_{\lambda}|0)

\displaystyle=\sum_{0<t<\tau}\nu_{t,M}(\gamma_{\lambda}|_{[0,t]})=\sum_{0<t<\lambda\tau}\dim{\rm Ker}(\Upsilon_{B}(t)-M).

Hence for each
\lambda_{k}
,
k=1,\cdots,l
, we obtain

\left.\begin{array}[]{ll}&i_{\tau,M}(\gamma_{\lambda})=i_{\tau,M}(\gamma_{\lambda_{k}})\quad\hbox{if $\lambda_{k-1}<\lambda\leq\lambda_{k}$},\\
&i_{\tau,M}(\gamma_{\lambda})=i_{\tau,M}(\gamma_{\lambda_{k}})+\nu_{\tau,M}(\gamma_{\lambda_{k}})\quad\hbox{if $\lambda_{k}<\lambda\leq\lambda_{k+1}$}\end{array}\right\}

(3.30)

where
\lambda_{0}=0
and
\lambda_{l+1}=1
. Note that

u:[0,\tau]\to{\mathbb{R}}^{2n}
satisfies the boundary value problem

\dot{u}(t)=J\nabla_{z}F(\lambda,t,u(t))\;\forall t\in[0,\tau]\quad\hbox{and}\quad u(\tau)=Mu(0)

(3.31)

if and only if
v:[0,\lambda\tau]\to{\mathbb{R}}^{2n},\;t\mapsto u(t/\lambda)
satisfies

\dot{v}(t)=J\nabla_{z}H(\lambda{t},v(t))\;\forall t\in[0,\lambda\tau]\quad\hbox{and}\quad v(\lambda\tau)=Mv(0),

(3.32)

and in this situation there holds

\int^{\lambda\tau}_{0}\left[\frac{1}{2}(J\dot{v}(t),v(t))_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}+H(\lambda t,{v}(t))\right]dt=\int^{\tau}_{0}\left[\frac{1}{2}(J\dot{u}(t),u(t))_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}+F(\lambda,t,{u}(t))\right]dt.

Because of (
3.30
), applying Theorem
1.7
to
F
leads to the desired results.

Step 2
(
Prove
(II)).
Define
\hat{H}:[0,\tau]\times{{\mathbb{R}}}^{2n}\to{\mathbb{R}}
by

\hat{H}(t,z)=-H(\tau-t,z)
. It satisfies Assumption
1.1
and
\nabla_{z}\hat{H}(t,\bar{u})=0
for all
(\lambda,t)
.
Note that
\hat{B}(t)=\nabla_{z}^{2}\hat{H}(t,\bar{u})=-\nabla_{z}^{2}H(\tau-t,\bar{u})=-B(\tau-t)
is positive definite
for each
t
, and that
\Upsilon_{\hat{B}}(t)=\Upsilon_{B}(\tau-t)\Upsilon_{B}(\tau)^{-1}

for all
t\in[0,\tau]
. Applying the conclusions in (I) to
\hat{H}
we get
at least one and at most finitely many numbers
in
(0,\tau)
,
\delta_{1}<\cdots<\delta_{l}
, such that

\nu_{\delta_{k},M}(\Upsilon_{\hat{B}}):=\dim{\rm Ker}(\Upsilon_{\hat{B}}(\delta_{k})-M)\neq 0,\quad k=1,\cdots,l,

and that (I) holds if
H
and
\tau_{k}
are replaced by
\hat{H}
and
\delta_{k}
,
k=1,\cdots,l
.
Moreover, for
0<\rho<\tau
,
u:[0,\rho]\to\mathbb{R}^{2n}
satisfies the following boundary value problem

\dot{u}(t)=J\nabla_{z}\hat{H}(\frac{\rho}{\tau}t,u(t))\;\forall t\in[0,\rho]
and
u(\rho)=Mu(0)

if and only if
w:[\tau-\rho^{2}/\tau,\tau]\to\mathbb{R}^{2n}
given by
w(t)=u(\tau-\frac{\rho}{\tau}t)

satisfies

\hbox{$\dot{w}(t)=\frac{\tau}{\rho}J\nabla_{z}{H}(t,w(t))\;\forall t\in[\tau-\rho^{2}/\tau,\tau]$ \quad and\quad$Mw(\tau)=w(\tau-\rho^{2}/\tau)$};

and in this situation there holds

\int^{\rho}_{0}\left[\frac{1}{2}(J\dot{u}(t),u(t))_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}+\hat{H}(\frac{\rho}{\tau}t,{u}(t))\right]dt=\int^{\tau}_{\tau-\rho^{2}/\tau}\left[\frac{1}{2}(J\dot{w}(t),w(t))_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}+\frac{\tau}{\rho}H(t,{w}(t))\right]dt.

Using these and the conclusions in (I) it is easy for us to derive (II).
∎

4
Proofs of Theorems
1.14
,
1.18
,

1.19
,
1.20
,
1.21
and Corollary
1.16

All proofs are completed in two subsections.
Before giving these we begin with the following.

Variational spaces
.
Consider the Hilbert spaces

\displaystyle\textsf{L}_{\tau,M}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{v\in L^{2}_{\rm loc}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})\,|\,v(t+\tau)=Mv(t)\;\hbox{for a.e.}\;t\in{\mathbb{R}}\},

\displaystyle\textsf{W}_{\tau,M}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{v\in W^{1,2}_{\rm loc}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})\,|\,v(t+\tau)=Mv(t)\;\hbox{for all}\;t\in{\mathbb{R}}\}

equipped with inner products (
2.1
) and (
2.2
) respectively.
There exists a natural (continuous)
\mathbb{R}
-action on
\textsf{L}_{\tau,M}
and
\textsf{W}_{\tau,M}
:

(\theta,v)\mapsto\theta\cdot v

(4.1)

given by
(\theta\cdot v)(t):=v(\theta+t)
for any
t\in\mathbb{R}
.

Let
\mathcal{S}
be one of the spaces
C^{i}_{M}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})
,
{W}^{1,2}_{M}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})
and
L^{2}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})
.
For each
u\in\mathcal{S}
we define
u^{M}:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{2n}
by (
1.7
).
Clearly, if
u\in C^{i}_{M}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})
, then
u^{M}\in C^{i}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})
and satisfies
u^{M}(t+\tau)=Mu^{M}(t)\;\forall t\in{\mathbb{R}}
.
When
u\in L^{2}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})
we have
u^{M}(t+\tau)=Mu^{M}(t)\;\forall t\in{\mathbb{R}}\setminus(\tau{\mathbb{Z}})
, and so

u^{M}(t+\tau)=Mu^{M}(t)
for a.e.
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
. Thus there exists a natural (continuous)
\mathbb{R}
-action on
\mathcal{S}
,

\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{S},\;(\theta,u)\mapsto(\theta\ast u)(\cdot):=(\theta\cdot u^{M})|_{[0,\tau]}.

(4.2)

Clearly, the actions in (
4.1
) and (
4.2
) are via Banach isometry isomorphisms if
M
is also an orthogonal matrix, and
become
S^{1}
-actions, where
S^{1}=\mathbb{R}/(l\tau\mathbb{Z})
, if

M^{l}=I_{2n}
for some integer
l\geq 1
. Moreover Hilbert space isomorphisms

{W}^{1,2}_{M}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})\to\textsf{W}_{\tau,M}\quad\hbox{and}\quad L^{2}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})\to\textsf{L}_{\tau,M}

given by
u\to u^{M}

are equivariant with respect to the actions in (
4.1
) and (
4.2
).
Because of these,
variational spaces will be chosen as
{W}^{1,2}_{M}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})
and
L^{2}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})

in the following proofs
though natural choices should be
\textsf{M}_{\tau,M}
and
\textsf{L}_{\tau,M}
.

Let
{{\mathbb{R}}}_{u}
denote the isotropy group at a point
u\in\mathcal{S}
of the action in (
4.2
).
It is a subgroup of
(\mathbb{R},+)
. If
\mathbb{R}_{u}
contains a sequence
(\theta_{k})\subset\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}

converging to
0
, then
\mathbb{R}_{u}
must be dense in
\mathbb{R}

and so is equal to
\mathbb{R}
because the continuity of the map
\theta\mapsto\theta\cdot u
implies that
\mathbb{R}_{u}
is closed.
Therefore

\mathbb{R}_{u}
must be equal to either
{\mathbb{R}}
, or
\{0\}
or
\varrho{\mathbb{Z}}
for some real
\varrho>0
.
It follows that
u
is constant in the first case, injective in the second case, and
has a period
\varrho=k\tau
for some
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
in the final case.

Proposition 4.1
.

Let
H:\mathbb{R}^{2n}\to{\mathbb{R}}
be
C^{1,1}
and
M
-invariant.
Suppose that
u:[0,\tau]\to{\mathbb{R}}^{2n}
satisfies
\dot{u}=J\nabla H(u)
and
u(\tau)=Mu(0)
. Then
u
is
C^{2}
and

(i)

if
{\mathbb{R}}_{u}={\mathbb{R}}
,
u
is equal to a constant vector
u_{0}
in
{\rm Ker}(M-I_{2n})
;

(ii)

if
{\mathbb{R}}_{u}=\{0\}
,
\theta\mapsto\theta\ast u

is an one-to-one
C^{1}
immersion from
\mathbb{R}
to
C^{1}_{M}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})
;

(iii)

if
{\mathbb{R}}_{u}=\varrho{\mathbb{Z}}
for some
\varrho>0
, i.e.,
\varrho
is a minimal period of
u
,
the
{\mathbb{R}}
-orbit
\{\theta\ast u\,|\,\theta\in{\mathbb{R}}\}

is a
C^{1}
embedded
S^{1}={\mathbb{R}}/(\varrho{\mathbb{Z}})
in
C^{1}_{M}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})
.

Moreover, if
M=I_{2n}
then
\tau\mathbb{Z}\subset\mathbb{R}_{u}
and therefore the case (ii) does not occur.

(ii) comes from the existence and uniqueness theorem for solutions of

\dot{u}=J\nabla H(u)
with initial values. (iii) uses the elementary topological fact that
a continuous one-to-one map from a compact space to a Hausdorff space is a homeomorphism.

4.1
Proof of Theorem
1.14
and Corollary
1.16

Proof of Theorem
1.14
.

Define

\hat{H}:\Lambda\times{{\mathbb{R}}}^{2n}\to{\mathbb{R}}
by

\hat{H}(\lambda,z)={H}(\lambda,z+v_{\lambda}(t)).

Clearly, it satisfies Assumption
1.13
with
v_{\lambda}\equiv 0
.
Therefore in what follows we always write
\hat{H}
as
H
and assume
v_{\lambda}=0
for all
\lambda\in\Lambda
.

When
\bar{v}
is viewed as a constant value map from
[0,\tau]
to
{\mathbb{R}}^{2n}
we write it as
\bar{u}
, i.e.,

\bar{u}=\bar{v}|_{[0,\tau]}
. Then
M\bar{u}=\bar{u}
and
\bar{u}
is a fixed point for the
{\mathbb{R}}
-action in (
4.2
).

As in the first paragraph of Section
3

we can modify
H
as
\tilde{H}(\lambda,t,z)=\chi(z)H(\lambda,t,z)
so that
it is also
M
-invariant. (Indeed,
\hat{U}_{0}:=\sum^{l}_{j=0}M^{j}U_{0}
is
M
-invariant and
Cl(\hat{U}_{0})

may be contained in
U
by shrinking
U_{0}
. We only need to replace
\chi
by
\hat{\chi}
,
where
\hat{\chi}(z):=\chi(\sum^{l}_{j=0}M^{j}z)
.)

Follow the notations in proofs of Theorems
1.4
,
1.7
.
As pointed out at the beginning of the proof of Theorem
1.7

we have actually checked in the proof of Theorem
1.4
(II)
that
\mathcal{L}_{\lambda}(\cdot)=\psi_{\kappa}(\lambda,\bar{w}+\cdot)
with

\bar{w}=-\Lambda_{M,\tau,\kappa I_{2n}}\bar{u}=-J\dot{\bar{u}}-\kappa\bar{u}=-\kappa\bar{u}

(and hence
M\bar{w}=\bar{w}
),
H=L^{2}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})
and
X=C^{0}_{M}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})
satisfy conditions in
Theorem
C.7
(
[
52
, Theorem 3.6]
or
[
50
, Theorem 4.6]
)
except for the condition (f).
Note that
\mathcal{L}_{\lambda}(\cdot)
are also invariant for the
S^{1}
-action with
S^{1}=\mathbb{R}/(lT\mathbb{Z})
given by
(
4.2
).

Step 1
(
Prove the alternative of (i) or (ii)
).
Let us prove that Theorem
C.8
(
[
52
, Theorem 3.7]
or
[
50
, Theorem 5.12]
) can be used.
Because of the assumption (b),
it suffices to prove that

the fixed point set of the induced
S^{1}
-action on

H_{\mu}^{0}:={\rm Ker}(B_{\mu}(\bar{w}))
is
\{0\}
,

(4.3)

where
B_{\mu}(\bar{w})
is given by (
3.15
).
Let
\xi\in H^{0}_{\mu}
be a fixed point for the above
S^{1}
-action. Then it is constant and satisfies
M\xi=\xi
.
Moreover,

B_{\mu}(\bar{w})\xi=A_{M,\tau,\kappa}\xi+\nabla^{2}_{z}(H_{\kappa})^{\ast}(\mu;\bar{w})\xi=0
, that is,

\xi=-\Lambda_{M,\tau,\kappa I_{2n}}(\nabla_{z}^{2}(H_{\kappa})^{\ast}(\mu;\bar{w})\xi)=-\kappa\nabla_{z}^{2}(H_{\kappa})^{\ast}(\mu;\bar{w})\xi=-\kappa[\nabla^{2}_{z}H(\mu,\bar{u})-\kappa I_{2n}]^{-1}\xi,

where the second equality comes from (
2.3
), and
the third equality is because

\nabla_{z}^{2}(H_{\kappa})^{\ast}(\mu;\bar{w})\nabla^{2}_{z}H_{\kappa}(\mu,\bar{u})=I_{2n}

by the equality
\bar{w}=-J\dot{\bar{u}}-\kappa\bar{u}=\nabla_{z}H(\mu,\bar{u})-\kappa\bar{u}=\nabla_{z}H_{\kappa}(\mu,\bar{u})
and

[
20
, page 92, Proposition 10]
.
Then the fixed point set of the induced
S^{1}
-action on
H^{0}_{\mu}
is equal to

\{\nabla^{2}_{z}H(\mu,\bar{u})\xi=0\,|\,\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{2n}\}.

By the assumption (a), i.e.,
{\rm Ker}(M-I_{2n})\cap{\rm Ker}(\nabla^{2}_{z}H(\mu,\bar{u}))=\{0\}
, we deduce
\xi=0
.
(
4.3
) is proved.

Now by Theorem
C.8
(
[
52
, Theorem 3.7]
or
[
50
, Theorem 5.12]
) one of the following alternatives occurs:

(I)

(\mu,\bar{w})
is not an isolated solution in
\{\mu\}\times L^{2}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})

of
\nabla_{w}\psi_{\kappa}(\mu,w)=0
.

(II)

There exist left and right neighborhoods
\Lambda^{-}
and
\Lambda^{+}
of
\mu
in
\Lambda

and integers
n^{+},n^{-}\geq 0
, such that
n^{+}+n^{-}\geq\frac{1}{2}\dim H^{0}_{\mu}
,
and that for
\lambda\in\Lambda^{-}\setminus\{\mu\}
(resp.
\lambda\in\Lambda^{+}\setminus\{\mu\}
)
the functional
\psi_{\kappa}(\lambda,\cdot)
has at least
n^{-}
(resp.
n^{+}
) distinct critical

S^{1}
-orbits disjoint with
\bar{w}
, which converge to
\bar{w}
in
C^{0}_{M}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})
as
\lambda\to\mu
.

In the case of (I)
, we have a sequence
(w_{j})\subset L^{2}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})\setminus\{\bar{w}\}

such that
\|w_{j}-\bar{w}\|_{2}\to 0
as
j\to\infty
and that
\nabla_{w}\psi_{\kappa}(\mu,w_{j})=0
for each
j\in{\mathbb{N}}
.
Because
\bar{w}
is a fixed point for the
S^{1}
-action, the
S^{1}
-orbits are compact and different
S^{1}
-orbits
are not intersecting, by passing to a subsequence we can assume that any two of
w_{j}
,
j=0,1,\cdots
,
do not belong the same
S^{1}
-orbit. The same claim also holds for
\bar{u}
and

u_{j}:=-(\Lambda_{M,\tau,\kappa I_{2n}})^{-1}w_{j}\neq\bar{u}
,
j=1,2,\cdots
.
Moreover, since
\Lambda_{M,\tau,\kappa I_{2n}}:W^{1,2}_{M}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})\to L^{2}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})

is a Banach space isomorphism,

u_{j}\to\bar{u}
in
W^{1,2}_{M}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})
, and hence
\|u_{j}-\bar{u}\|_{C^{1}}\to 0
by Proposition
1.3
(i) [or its proof].
Since
H(\mu,\cdot)
is
M
-invariant, each
u_{j}
can be extended into a solution
\bar{v}_{j}:=(u_{j})^{M}
of (
1.24
)
with
\lambda=\mu
via (
1.7
), which is not equal to
\bar{v}
. Clearly, any two of
\bar{v}_{j}
,
j=0,1,\cdots
,
are
{\mathbb{R}}
-distinct, and
(\bar{v}_{j})
converges to
\bar{v}
on any compact interval
I\subset{\mathbb{R}}
in
C^{1}
-topology.

In the case of (II)
, note firstly that
\dim H_{\mu}^{0}
is equal to the number of maximal linearly independent solutions of

\displaystyle\dot{u}(t)=J\nabla_{z}^{2}H(\mu,\bar{u})u(t)\;\forall t\in[0,\tau]\quad\hbox{and}\quad u(\tau)=Mu(0).

(4.4)

Since
M^{T}\nabla_{z}^{2}H(\mu,Mz)=\nabla_{z}^{2}H(\mu,z)
, the extension via (
1.7
) establishes an isomorphism
between the solution space of (
4.4
) and that of

\displaystyle\dot{v}(t)=J\nabla_{z}^{2}H(\mu,\bar{v})v(t)\quad\hbox{and}\quad v(t+\tau)=Mv(t),\;\forall t\in{\mathbb{R}}.

Hence
\dim H_{\mu}^{0}
is equal to the number of maximal linearly independent solutions of
(
1.26
) with
\lambda=\mu
.
For
\lambda\in\Lambda^{-}\setminus\{\mu\}
(resp.
\lambda\in\Lambda^{+}\setminus\{\mu\}
) let

S^{1}\cdot w_{\lambda}^{i}
,
i=1,\cdots,n^{-}
(resp.
n^{+}
)
be distinct critical
S^{1}
-orbits of
\psi_{\kappa}(\lambda,\cdot)

disjoint with
\bar{w}
, which converge to
\bar{w}
in
C^{0}_{M}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})
as
\lambda\to\mu
.
Since
\Lambda^{c}_{M,\tau,\kappa I_{2n}}:C^{1}_{M}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})\to C^{0}_{M}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})

is a Banach space isomorphism,

\hbox{$u^{i}_{\lambda}:=-(\Lambda^{c}_{M,\tau,\kappa I_{2n}})^{-1}(w_{\lambda}^{i})\notin S^{1}\cdot\bar{u}=\{\bar{u}\}$,\quad$i=1,\cdots,n^{-}$ (resp. $n^{+}$)},

and
S^{1}\cdot u^{i}_{\lambda}\to\bar{u}
in
C^{1}_{M}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})
as
\lambda\to\mu
.
It follows that
v^{i}_{\lambda}:=(u^{i}_{\lambda})^{M}+v_{\lambda}
,

i=1,\cdots,n^{-}
(resp.
n^{+}
), are
{\mathbb{R}}
-distinct solutions of (
1.24
) under the initial assumptions on
H

in Assumption
1.13
, and
also
{\mathbb{R}}
-distinct from
v_{\lambda}
because each
v_{\lambda}
is a fixed point for the
S^{1}
-action given by
(
4.1
).

Step 2
(
Prove the part after “Moreover”
).
Since any orbit
\mathcal{O}
of the induced
S^{1}
-action on
H_{\mu}^{0}
is either a point or an embedded circle,
our assumption
\dim H^{0}_{\mu}=\nu_{\tau,M}(\gamma_{\mu})\geq 3
shows that
Theorem
C.9
(or
[
52
, Theorem 3.10]
) is available to the functionals

\mathcal{L}_{\lambda}(\cdot)=\psi_{\kappa}(\lambda,\bar{w}+\cdot)
.
Then we obtain either (I) or
one of the following alternatives occurs:

(III)

For every
\lambda\in\Lambda\setminus\{\mu\}
near
\mu\in\Lambda
there is a

S^{1}
-orbit
S^{1}\ast\bar{w}_{\lambda}\neq S^{1}\ast\bar{w}=\{\bar{w}\}
near
\bar{w}\in C^{0}_{M}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})

such that
\nabla_{w}\psi_{\kappa}(\lambda,\bar{w}_{\lambda})=0
and that

S^{1}\ast\bar{w}_{\lambda}\to\bar{w}
in
C^{0}_{M}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})
as
\lambda\to\mu
.

(IV)

For any small
S^{1}
-invariant neighborhood
\mathcal{N}
of
\bar{w}
in
C^{0}_{M}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})

there is an one-sided deleted neighborhood
\Lambda^{0}
of
\mu\in\Lambda
such that
for any
\lambda\in\Lambda^{0}
,
\nabla_{w}\psi_{\kappa}(\lambda,\cdot)=0

has either infinitely many
S^{1}
-orbits of solutions in
\mathcal{N}
,

S^{1}\ast\bar{w}^{j}_{\lambda}
,
j=1,2,\cdots
, or at least two
S^{1}
-orbits of solutions in
\mathcal{N}
with different energy,

S^{1}\ast\hat{w}^{1}_{\lambda}\neq\{\bar{w}\}
and
S^{1}\ast\hat{w}^{2}_{\lambda}\neq\{\bar{w}\}
.
Moreover, these orbits
converge to
\bar{w}
in
C^{0}_{M}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})
as
\lambda\to\mu
.

In the case of (III)
, let
\bar{u}_{\lambda}:=-\Lambda_{M,\tau,\kappa I_{2n}}^{-1}(\bar{w}_{\lambda})
,
which is not in
S^{1}\cdot\bar{u}=\{\bar{u}\}
. Then
\bar{v}_{\lambda}:=(\bar{u}_{\lambda})^{M}+v_{\lambda}

is a solution of (
1.24
) under the initial assumptions on
H

in Assumption
1.13
, and also
{\mathbb{R}}
-distinct from
v_{\lambda}
.

In the case of (IV)
, let
\hat{u}^{i}_{\lambda}:=-\Lambda_{M,\tau,\kappa I_{2n}}^{-1}(\hat{w}_{\lambda})
,
i=1,2
,
and
\bar{u}^{j}_{\lambda}:=-\Lambda_{M,\tau,\kappa I_{2n}}^{-1}(\bar{w}^{j}_{\lambda})
,
j=1,2,\cdots
. Then

\hat{v}^{i}_{\lambda}:=(\hat{u}^{i}_{\lambda})^{M}+v_{\lambda}
,
i=1,2
, and

\bar{v}^{j}_{\lambda}:=(\bar{u}^{j}_{\lambda})^{M}+v_{\lambda}
,
j=1,2,\cdots
, are the desired solutions
if
\mathcal{N}
is small enough. In fact,
since
\Lambda_{M,\tau,\kappa I_{2n}}^{c}:C^{1}_{M}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})\to C^{0}_{M}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})

is a Banach space isomorphism and
\bar{w}=0
, we deduce

\displaystyle\|\hat{v}^{i}_{\lambda}|_{[0,\tau]}-v_{\lambda}|_{[0,\tau]}\|_{C^{1}}=\|\hat{u}^{i}_{\lambda}\|_{C^{1}}\leq\|\Lambda_{M,\tau,\kappa I_{2n}}^{-1}\|\cdot\|\hat{w}^{i}_{\lambda}\|_{C^{0}}=\|\Lambda_{M,\tau,\kappa I_{2n}}^{-1}\|\cdot\|\hat{w}^{i}_{\lambda}-\bar{w}\|_{C^{0}},\;i=1,2,

\displaystyle\|\bar{v}^{j}_{\lambda}|_{[0,\tau]}-v_{\lambda}|_{[0,\tau]}\|_{C^{1}}=\|\bar{u}^{j}_{\lambda}\|_{C^{1}}\leq\|\Lambda_{M,\tau,\kappa I_{2n}}^{-1}\|\cdot\|\bar{w}^{j}_{\lambda}\|_{C^{0}}=\|\Lambda_{M,\tau,\kappa I_{2n}}^{-1}\|\cdot\|\bar{w}^{j}_{\lambda}-\bar{w}\|_{C^{0}},\;\forall j\in\mathbb{N}.

Hence we only need to take the above neighborhood
\mathcal{N}
as
the open ball of radius
\|\Lambda_{M,\tau,\kappa I_{2n}}^{-1}\|/\varepsilon
and center at
\bar{w}=0
in
C^{0}_{M}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})
.
∎

Proof of Corollary
1.16
.

By Corollary
1.15
with
H_{0}=0
and
\hat{H}=H
,
\gamma_{\lambda}(t)=\exp(\lambda tJ{H}^{\prime\prime}(v_{0}))
and therefore

\Sigma_{1}=\{\lambda\in\mathbb{R}\,|\,\nu_{1,M}(\gamma_{\lambda})>0\}=\Gamma(H,v_{0},M)\cup\{0\}

is a discrete set in
\mathbb{R}
, which gives rise to the claim (A). In addition,
for each
\mu\in\Gamma(H,\bar{v},M)

the problem (
1.26
) with
H(\mu,\cdot)=\mu\hat{H}(\cdot)
and
v_{\mu}=\bar{v}
has no nonzero constant solutions
because
{\rm Ker}(\hat{H}^{\prime\prime}(\bar{v}))=\{0\}
.

Suppose
{H}^{\prime\prime}(\bar{v})>0
and
\Gamma(H,\bar{v},M)\cap(0,\infty)\neq\emptyset

(resp.
{H}^{\prime\prime}(\bar{v})<0
and
\Gamma(H,\bar{v},M)\cap(-\infty,0)\neq\emptyset
).
For
\mu\in\Gamma(H,\bar{v},M)\cap(0,\infty)
(resp.
\Gamma(H,\bar{v},M)\cap(-\infty,0)
),
it follows from (
A.35
) (resp. (
A.36
)) that

i_{1,M}(\gamma_{\lambda})=i_{1,M}(\gamma_{\mu})
for
\lambda\leq\mu
close to
\mu
and that

i_{1,M}(\gamma_{\lambda})=i_{1,M}(\gamma_{\mu})+\nu_{1,M}(\gamma_{\mu})
for
\lambda>\mu
close to
\mu
.
Hence for each
\mu\in\Gamma(H,\bar{v},M)
Corollary
1.15
implies that one of the following alternatives occurs:

(1)

The problem

\dot{v}(t)=\mu J\nabla H(v(t))\quad\hbox{and}\quad v(t+1)=Mv(t),\;\forall t\in{\mathbb{R}}

(4.5)

has a sequence of
{\mathbb{R}}
-distinct solutions,
w_{k}
,
k=1,2,\cdots
,
which are
{\mathbb{R}}
-distinct from
\bar{v}
and converges to
\bar{v}
on any compact interval
I\subset{\mathbb{R}}
in
C^{1}
-topology.

(2)

There exist left and right neighborhoods
\Lambda^{-}
and
\Lambda^{+}
of
\mu
in
\mathbb{R}\setminus\{\mu\}

and integers
n^{+},n^{-}\geq 0
, such that
n^{+}+n^{-}\geq\nu_{1,M}(\gamma_{\mu})/2
,
and for
\lambda\in\Lambda^{-}\setminus\{\mu\}
(resp.
\lambda\in\Lambda^{+}\setminus\{\mu\}
),
the problem

\dot{v}(t)=\lambda J\nabla H(v(t))\quad\hbox{and}\quad v(t+1)=Mv(t),\;\forall t\in{\mathbb{R}}

(4.6)

has at least
n^{-}
(resp.
n^{+}
)
{\mathbb{R}}
-distinct solutions
solutions,
w_{\lambda}^{i}
,
i=1,\cdots,n^{-}
(resp.
n^{+}
),
which are
{\mathbb{R}}
-distinct from
v_{0}
and converge to
\bar{v}
on any compact interval
I\subset{\mathbb{R}}
in
C^{1}
-topology as
\lambda\to\mu
.

Moreover, if
\dim{\rm Ker}(\exp(\mu{H}^{\prime\prime}(\bar{v}))-M)\geq 3
,
by the conclusions after “Moreover” in Theorem
1.14

with
H(\lambda,x)=\lambda\hat{H}(x)
and
v_{\lambda}\equiv\bar{v}\;\forall\lambda
, we obtain that
either (1) holds or one of the following alternatives occurs:

(3)

For every
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{\mu\}
near
\mu
,
(
4.6
) has a solution
w_{\lambda}
, which is
{\mathbb{R}}
-distinct from
\bar{v}
and converges to
\bar{v}

on any compact interval
I\subset{\mathbb{R}}
in
C^{1}
-topology as
\lambda\to\mu
.

(4)

For a given
\epsilon>0
there is an one-sided neighborhood
\Lambda^{0}
of
\mu
in
{\mathbb{R}}\setminus\{\mu\}
such that
for any
\lambda\in\Lambda^{0}\setminus\{\mu\}
, (
4.6
) with parameter value
\lambda

has either infinitely many
\mathbb{R}
-distinct solutions
\bar{w}_{\lambda}^{k}

such that each of them is
{\mathbb{R}}
-distinct from
\bar{v}
and
\|\bar{w}_{\lambda}^{k}|_{[0,1]}-\bar{v}\|_{C^{1}}<\epsilon
,
k=1,2,\cdots
, or
at least two
\mathbb{R}
-distinct solutions
\hat{w}_{\lambda}^{1}
and
\hat{w}_{\lambda}^{2}

such that:

a)

each of them is
{\mathbb{R}}
-distinct from
\bar{v}
,

b)

\|\hat{w}_{\lambda}^{i}|_{[0,1]}-\bar{v}\|_{C^{1}}<\epsilon
,
i=1,2
,

c)

\displaystyle\int^{1}_{0}\left[\frac{1}{2}(J\dot{\hat{w}}_{\lambda}^{1}(t),\hat{w}^{1}_{\lambda}(t))_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}+\lambda H(\hat{w}_{\lambda}^{1}(t))\right]dt\neq\int^{1}_{0}\left[\frac{1}{2}(J\dot{\hat{w}}^{2}_{\lambda}(t),\hat{w}_{\lambda}^{2}(t))_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}+\lambda H(\hat{w}_{\lambda}^{2}(t))\right]dt.

Define
v_{k}:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{2n},\;t\mapsto w_{k}(t/\mu)
,
k=1,2,\cdots
,
and
v_{\lambda}^{i}:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{2n},\;t\mapsto w_{\lambda}(t/\lambda)
,
i=1,\cdots,n^{-}
(resp.
n^{+}
).
By (1) and (2) they satisfy (B.i) and (B.ii), respectively.
Similarly, (B.iii) and (B.iv) may follow from (3) and (4), respectively.
∎

4.2
Proofs of
Theorems
1.18
,
1.19
,
1.20
,
1.21

Since
\mathbb{R}
is a non-compact Lie group and the
\mathbb{R}
-action in (
4.2
) is not
C^{1}
,
we cannot directly use the theorems in
[
50
, Sect.5.2]
and
[
52
]
.
But the proof ideas of those theorems may be used to complete our proofs.

If
u\in C^{k}_{M}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})
with
k\geq 1
is nonconstant,
since
u^{M}=(u^{M})^{(0)},\cdots,(u^{M})^{(k)}
are uniformly continuous over any compact interval of
{\mathbb{R}}
it is easy to prove that the map

f:{\mathbb{R}}\to L^{2}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})
given by
f(\theta)=\theta\ast u
is
C^{k}
,
and

f^{(i)}(\theta)=(u^{M})^{(i)}(\theta+\cdot)|_{[0,\tau]},\quad i=1,\cdots,k.

(4.7)

In what follows we assume that
\bar{v}:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{2n}
is a nonconstant solution of (
1.24
) for each
\lambda\in\Lambda
.
Then
\bar{v}
is
C^{3}
. As in Section
4.1
we write
\bar{u}:=\bar{v}|_{[0,\tau]}
.
It is nonconstant. By Proposition
4.1
and the arguments above this proposition we see:

\bullet
either the map

\theta\mapsto\theta\ast\bar{u}
is an one-to-one
C^{2}
immersion from
{\mathbb{R}}
to

W^{1,2}_{M}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})
, (thus the restriction of it to any compact submanifold of
{\mathbb{R}}
is
a
C^{2}
-embedding by an elementary result in general topology,)
and
\{\theta\ast\bar{u}\,|\,\theta\in{\mathbb{R}}\}

is a
C^{2}
-immersed submanifold in
{W}^{1,2}_{M}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})
without self-intersection points;

\bullet
or
\{\theta\ast\bar{u}\,|\,\theta\in{\mathbb{R}}\}
is a
C^{2}
-embedded circle.

Let
\bar{w}:=-\Lambda_{M,\tau,\kappa I_{2n}}(\bar{u})=-J\dot{\bar{u}}-\kappa\bar{u}
.
It belongs to
C^{2}_{M}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})
.
Since
\Lambda_{M,\tau,\kappa I_{2n}}
is a Banach space isomorphism from
{W}^{1,2}_{M}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})
to
L^{2}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})
,
and equivariant with respect to the action in (
4.2
), i.e.,

\Lambda_{M,\tau,\kappa I_{2n}}(\theta\ast u)=\theta\ast(\Lambda_{M,\tau,\kappa I_{2n}}(u))
for any
(\theta,u)\in{\mathbb{R}}\times{W}^{1,2}_{M}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})
,
we have:

Claim 4.2
.

\mathcal{O}:=\{\theta\ast\bar{w}\,|\,\theta\in{\mathbb{R}}\}

is either a
C^{2}
-immersed submanifold in
L^{2}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})
without self-intersection points
or a
C^{2}
-embedded circle in
L^{2}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})
. In particular,
by (
4.7
)

0\neq\frac{d}{d\theta}(\theta\ast\bar{w})=(\dot{\bar{w}})^{M}(\theta+\cdot)|_{[0,\tau]}\quad\forall\theta\in\mathbb{R}\quad\hbox{and so}\quad\dot{\bar{w}}=(\dot{\bar{w}})^{M}|_{[0,\tau]}\neq 0.

Let
H^{\bot}
denote the orthogonal complementary of
\dot{\bar{w}}
in
H:=L^{2}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})
and

B_{H}^{\bot}(\bar{w},\varepsilon):=\{\bar{w}+u\,|\,u\in{H}^{\bot},\;\|u\|_{2}<\varepsilon\},\quad\forall\varepsilon>0.

Clearly,
B_{H}^{\bot}(\bar{w},\varepsilon)
is a
C^{\infty}
submanifold of
L^{2}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})
containing
\bar{w}
, and

T_{\bar{w}}B_{H}^{\bot}(\bar{w},\varepsilon)=H^{\bot}

and
L^{2}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})=T_{\bar{w}}B_{H}^{\bot}(\bar{w},\varepsilon)\oplus({\mathbb{R}}\dot{\bar{w}})
.

Note that the isotropy groups
{{\mathbb{R}}}_{\bar{w}}
and
{{\mathbb{R}}}_{\dot{\bar{w}}}
of the

\mathbb{R}
-action defined by (
4.2
) satisfy
{{\mathbb{R}}}_{\bar{w}}\subset{{\mathbb{R}}}_{\dot{\bar{w}}}
.
For
\theta\in{{\mathbb{R}}}_{\bar{w}}

and
u\in H^{\bot}
,

\|\theta\ast u\|_{2}=\|u\|_{2}
and
(\dot{\bar{w}},\theta\ast u)_{2}=(\theta\ast\dot{\bar{w}},\theta\ast u)_{2}=(\dot{\bar{w}},\ast u)_{2}=0

imply that
B_{H}^{\bot}(\bar{w},\varepsilon)
is
{{\mathbb{R}}}_{\bar{w}}
-invariant.
Since
\Lambda_{M,\tau,\kappa I_{2n}}(\theta\ast\bar{u})=\theta\ast\bar{w}
for any
\theta\in{\mathbb{R}}
,

{{\mathbb{R}}}_{\bar{w}}
is equal to the isotropy group
{{\mathbb{R}}}_{\bar{u}}
of the
{\mathbb{R}}
-action at
\bar{u}\in{W}^{1,2}_{M}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})
.

Proposition 4.3
.

For any
0<\epsilon\leq\varepsilon
,

{\mathbb{R}}\ast B_{H}^{\bot}(\bar{w},\epsilon)
is a neighborhood of the orbit
\mathcal{O}={\mathbb{R}}\ast\bar{w}
.

Because the
{\mathbb{R}}
-action is not
C^{1}
we cannot directly deduce that the orbit
{\mathbb{R}}\ast w

has a transversal intersection with
B_{H}^{\bot}(\bar{w},\epsilon)

for every
w\in L^{2}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})
sufficiently close to
\bar{w}
.
But Proposition
4.3
can be proved with the following:

Proposition 4.4

(
[
9
, Proposition 3.5]
)
.

Let
\mathcal{M}
be a finite-dimensional manifold,
N
a (possibly infinite dimensional)
Banach manifold,
Q\subset N
a Banach submanifold, and
A
a topological
space. Assume that
\chi:A\times\mathcal{M}\to N
is a continuous function such that there exists

a_{0}\in A
and
m_{0}\in\mathcal{M}
with:

(a)

\chi(a_{0},m_{0})\in Q
;

(b)

\chi(a_{0},\cdot):\mathcal{M}\to N
is of class
C^{1}
;

(c)

\partial_{2}\chi(a_{0},m_{0})(T_{m_{0}}\mathcal{M})+T_{\chi(a_{0},m_{0})}Q=T_{\chi(a_{0},m_{0})}N
.

Then, for
a\in A
near
a_{0}
,
\chi(a,\mathcal{M})\cap Q\neq\emptyset
.

Proof of Proposition
4.3
.

Applying Proposition
4.4
to
A=N=L^{2}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})
,
\mathcal{M}={\mathbb{R}}
,
Q=B_{H}^{\bot}(\bar{w},\epsilon)
,

a_{0}=\bar{w}
,
m_{0}=0
and

\chi:A\times\mathcal{M}\to N:(w,\theta)\mapsto\theta\ast w
,
we get that

({\mathbb{R}}\ast w)\cap B_{H}^{\bot}(\bar{w},\epsilon)\neq\emptyset
if
w\in L^{2}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})
is close to
\bar{w}
.
∎

As in the first paragraph of Section
3
we can require that
all modified
H(\lambda,\cdot)

are also
M
-invariant
. (See the proof of Theorem
1.14
in Section
4.1
.)
Following the notations in Section
3
,
we have a family of
C^{1}
and twice Gâteaux-differentiable functionals
on
H=L^{2}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})
,

\psi_{\kappa}(\lambda,u)=\int^{\tau}_{0}\left[\frac{1}{2}(u(t),(A_{M,\tau,\kappa}u)(t))_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}+H_{\kappa}^{\ast}(\lambda;u(t))\right]dt,\quad\lambda\in\Lambda.

(4.8)

Moreover, each
(\psi_{\kappa})_{\lambda}(\cdot):=\psi_{\kappa}(\lambda,\cdot)
is also invariant for the
{\mathbb{R}}
-action given by (
4.2
), and

\displaystyle\nabla(\psi_{\kappa})_{\lambda}(v)=A_{M,\tau,\kappa}v+\nabla_{z}H_{\kappa}^{\ast}(\lambda;v(\cdot)),

(4.9)

\displaystyle B_{\lambda}(v):=D_{v}\nabla(\psi_{\kappa})_{\lambda}(v)=A_{M,\tau,\kappa}+\nabla^{2}_{z}H_{\kappa}^{\ast}(\lambda;v(\cdot))\in\mathscr{L}_{s}(L^{2}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})),

(4.10)

\displaystyle B_{\lambda}(\bar{w})\dot{\bar{w}}=0.

(4.11)

Fortunately, we can directly prove the following result, which naturally holds if the
{\mathbb{R}}
-action in (
4.2
) is
C^{1}
.

Proposition 4.5
.

There exists
\varepsilon>0
such that for any
\lambda\in\Lambda
, if
u\in B_{{H}^{\bot}}(0,\varepsilon)

is a critical point of the restriction of
\mathcal{L}_{\lambda}
to
B_{{H}^{\bot}}(0,\varepsilon)

then it is a critical point of
\mathcal{L}_{\lambda}(\cdot)=\psi_{\kappa}(\lambda,\bar{w}+\cdot)
.
Moreover,
u
is
C^{1}
and
\|u\|_{C^{1}}\to 0
as
\|u\|_{2}\to 0
.

Proof.

We only need to prove sufficiency.
Let
u\in H^{\bot}
be a critical point of
the restriction of
\mathcal{L}_{\lambda}
to
H^{\bot}
. Then

\bar{w}+u
is a critical point of the restriction of
(\psi_{\kappa})_{\lambda}
to
B_{{H}^{\bot}}(\bar{w},\varepsilon)
, i.e.,

d(\psi_{\kappa})_{\lambda}(\bar{w}+u)[\xi]=0\quad\forall\xi\in T_{\bar{w}+u}B_{H}^{\bot}(\bar{w},\varepsilon)=H^{\bot}.

(4.12)

Step 1
(
Prove that
u
is
C^{1}
).
Since

d(\psi_{\kappa})_{\lambda}(\bar{w}+u)[\xi]=(A_{M,\tau,\kappa}(\bar{w}+u)+\nabla_{z}H_{\kappa}^{\ast}(\lambda;\bar{w}+u),\xi)_{2}=0

for all
\xi\in H^{\bot}
,
we have
\mu(\lambda,u)\in{\mathbb{R}}
such that

\nabla(\psi_{\kappa})_{\lambda}(\bar{w}+u)=A_{M,\tau,\kappa}(\bar{w}+u)+\nabla_{z}H_{\kappa}^{\ast}(\lambda;\bar{w}+u)=\mu(\lambda,u)\dot{\bar{w}}.

(4.13)

Note that
\dot{\bar{w}}
is
C^{1}
and that
A_{M,\tau,\kappa}(\bar{w}+u)\in W^{1,2}_{M}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})
by (
3.12
).
The map

\Gamma:[0,\tau]\times\mathbb{R}^{2n}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{2n},\;(t,z)\mapsto\nabla_{z}H_{\kappa}^{\ast}(\lambda;z)-\mu(\lambda,u)\dot{\bar{w}}(t)+(A_{M,\tau,\kappa}(\bar{w}+u))(t)

is continuous, and
C^{1}
in
z
. But
D_{z}\Gamma(t,z)=\nabla^{2}_{z}H_{\kappa}^{\ast}(\lambda;z)
is invertible.
Therefore applying the implicit function theorem to
\Gamma

we derive from (
4.13
) that
\bar{w}+u
and therefore
u
is
C^{0}
.
By (
2.6
),
A_{M,\tau,\kappa}(\bar{w}+u)\in C^{1}_{M}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})

and hence

t\mapsto\mu(\lambda,u)\dot{\bar{w}}(t)-(A_{M,\tau,\kappa}(\bar{w}+u))(t)

is
C^{1}
. Then
\Gamma
is
C^{1}
. Using the implicit function theorem to
\Gamma
again we obtain that
u
is
C^{1}
.

Step 2
(
For any
\epsilon>0
, prove that there exists
\delta>0
such that
\|u\|_{2}<\delta
implies
|\mu(\lambda,u)|<\epsilon

for all
\lambda\in\Lambda
).
Since
\nabla(\psi_{\kappa})_{\lambda}(\bar{w})=0\;\forall\lambda\in\Lambda
, by (
4.13
) we have

\displaystyle\mu(\lambda,u)(\dot{\bar{w}},v)_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\nabla(\psi_{\kappa})_{\lambda}(\bar{w}+u),v)_{2}-(\nabla(\psi_{\kappa})_{\lambda}(\bar{w}),v)_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(A_{M,\tau,\kappa}(u),v)_{2}+(\nabla_{z}H_{\kappa}^{\ast}(\lambda;\bar{w}+u),v)_{2}-(\nabla_{z}H_{\kappa}^{\ast}(\lambda;\bar{w}),v)_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int^{\tau}_{0}\int^{1}_{0}(\nabla^{2}_{z}H_{\kappa}^{\ast}(\lambda,t;\bar{w}(t)+su(t))u(t),v(t))_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}dtds

\displaystyle+(A_{M,\tau,\kappa}(u),v)_{2},\quad\forall v\in H.

It follows from this and (
3.7
) that

|\mu(\lambda,u)|\cdot\|\dot{\bar{w}}\|_{2}\leq\|A_{M,\tau,\kappa}(u)\|_{2}+\frac{1}{c_{1}}\|u\|_{2}.

Then the claim follows because
\|\dot{\bar{w}}\|_{2}>0
and

A_{M,\tau,\kappa}\in\mathscr{L}_{s}(L^{2}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n}))
by (
3.12
).

Step 3
(
For any
\epsilon>0
, prove there exists
\varepsilon>0
such that
\|u\|_{2}\leq\varepsilon

implies
\|u\|_{C^{1}}<\epsilon
).
Since
\nabla(\psi_{\kappa})_{\lambda}(\bar{w})=0\;\forall\lambda\in\Lambda
, by (
4.13
) we have

A_{M,\tau,\kappa}(u)+\nabla_{z}H_{\kappa}^{\ast}(\lambda;\bar{w}+u)-\nabla_{z}H_{\kappa}^{\ast}({\lambda};\bar{w})=\mu(\lambda,u)\dot{\bar{w}}.

(4.14)

Note that this also holds in the sense of pointwise because
\bar{w}
is
C^{2}
,
u
is
C^{1}
and

A_{M,\tau,\kappa}(u)(t)=e^{\kappa tJ}\mathfrak{J}^{-1}\int^{\tau}_{0}e^{-\kappa tJ}Ju(t)dt-e^{\kappa tJ}\int^{t}_{0}e^{-\kappa sJ}Ju(s)ds

(4.15)

is
C^{2}
by (
3.12
)–(
3.13
). It follows that there exists a constant
C_{\kappa}>0
such that

|A_{M,\tau,\kappa}(u)(t)|_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}\leq C_{\kappa}\|u\|_{2},\quad\forall t\in[0,\tau].

(4.16)

Clearly, (
3.7
), the mean value theorem of integrals and (
4.14
) may lead to

\displaystyle\frac{1}{c_{2}}|u(t)|^{2}_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\int^{1}_{0}(\nabla^{2}_{z}H_{\kappa}^{\ast}(\lambda;\bar{w}(t)+su(t))u(t),u(t))_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}ds

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big(\nabla_{z}H_{\kappa}^{\ast}({\lambda};\bar{w}(t)+u(t))-\nabla_{z}H_{\kappa}^{\ast}(\lambda;\bar{w}(t)),u(t)\big)_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\big(\mu(\lambda,u)\dot{\bar{w}}(t)-A_{M,\tau,\kappa}(u)(t),u(t)\big)_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle|\mu(\lambda,u)|_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}\cdot|\dot{\bar{w}}(t)|_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}\cdot|u(t)|_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}+|A_{M,\tau,\kappa}(u)(t)|_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}\cdot|u(t)|_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}

and so

\displaystyle\frac{1}{c_{2}}|u(t)|_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}\leq|\mu(\lambda,u)|_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}\cdot|\dot{\bar{w}}(t)|_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}+C_{\kappa}\|u\|_{2}\quad\forall t\in[0,\tau]

by (
4.16
).
Therefore, since
\dot{\bar{w}}
is
C^{1}
, for any
\epsilon^{\prime}>0
, from this and the result in Step 2 we derive that
there exists
\varepsilon>0
such that

\|u\|_{2}\leq\varepsilon\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\|u\|_{C^{0}}<\epsilon^{\prime}.

(4.17)

Taking the derivatives on two sides of equation in (
4.14
) with respect to
t
we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}A_{M,\tau,\kappa}(u)(t)+\nabla^{2}_{z}H_{\kappa}^{\ast}(\lambda;\bar{w}(t)+u(t))\dot{u}(t)

\displaystyle+\nabla^{2}_{z}H_{\kappa}^{\ast}(\lambda;\bar{w}(t)+u(t))\dot{\bar{w}}(t)-\nabla^{2}_{z}H_{\kappa}^{\ast}(\lambda;\bar{w}(t))\dot{\bar{w}}(t)=\mu(\lambda,u)\ddot{\bar{w}}(t).

It follows from (
3.7
) and this that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{c_{2}}|\dot{u}(t)|_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle|\nabla^{2}_{z}H_{\kappa}^{\ast}(\lambda;\bar{w}(t)+u(t))\dot{u}(t)|_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}

(4.18)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left\|\nabla^{2}_{z}H_{\kappa}^{\ast}(\lambda;\bar{w}(t)+u(t))-\nabla^{2}_{z}H_{\kappa}^{\ast}(\lambda;\bar{w}(t))\right\|_{\mathbb{R}^{2n\times 2n}}|\dot{\bar{w}}(t)|

\displaystyle+\left|\frac{d}{dt}A_{M,\tau,\kappa}(u)(t)\right|_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}+|\mu(\lambda,u)|\cdot|\ddot{\bar{w}}(t)|

When
\|u\|_{C^{0}}\to 0
, (
4.15
) and Step 2 lead to

\max_{t}\left\|\frac{d}{dt}A_{M,\tau,\kappa}(u)(t)\right\|_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}\to 0\quad\hbox{and}\quad|\mu(\lambda,u)|\cdot\|\ddot{\bar{w}}\|_{C^{0}}\to 0,

respectively. Moreover, for a given compact neighborhood
\mathscr{U}
of
\bar{w}([0,\tau])
in
\mathbb{R}^{2n}
,
since
\nabla^{2}_{z}H_{\kappa}^{\ast}(\lambda;z)
is uniformly continuous in
\Lambda\times\mathscr{U}
,
we deduce that

\max_{t}\left\|\nabla^{2}_{z}H_{\kappa}^{\ast}(\lambda;\bar{w}(t)+u(t))-\nabla^{2}_{z}H_{\kappa}^{\ast}(\lambda;\bar{w}(t))\right\|_{\mathbb{R}^{2n\times 2n}}\to 0

uniformly with respect to
\lambda\in\Lambda
as
\|u\|_{C^{0}}\to 0
.
From these and (
4.18
) we deduce that
\|\dot{u}\|_{C^{0}}\to 0
as
\|u\|_{C^{0}}\to 0
.
The desired claim follows from this and (
4.17
).

Step 4
(
Prove
d(\psi_{\kappa})_{\lambda}(\bar{w}+u)=0
).
Note that
\|\dot{\bar{w}}\|_{C^{0}}>0
.
By Step 3 we get
\varepsilon>0
such that
\|u\|_{2}\leq\varepsilon
implies
\|u\|_{C^{1}}<\|\dot{\bar{w}}\|_{C^{0}}
and

\|u\|_{C^{1}}<\|\dot{\bar{w}}\|_{2}/\sqrt{\tau}
. Then
w:=\bar{w}+u
satisfy

\displaystyle\|\dot{w}\|_{C^{0}}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\|\dot{\bar{w}}\|_{C^{0}}-\|\dot{u}\|_{C^{0}}>0\quad\hbox{and}

\displaystyle(\dot{\bar{w}},\dot{w})_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\dot{\bar{w}},\dot{\bar{w}})_{2}+(\dot{\bar{w}},\dot{u})_{2}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\|\dot{\bar{w}}\|^{2}_{2}-\|\dot{\bar{w}}\|_{2}\|\dot{u}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\|\dot{\bar{w}}\|^{2}_{2}-\sqrt{\tau}\|\dot{\bar{w}}\|_{2}\|{u}\|_{C^{1}}>0.

The former shows that
w
is nonconstant, and therefore that
the map
\theta\mapsto\theta\ast w
is a
C^{1}
immersion from
{\mathbb{R}}
to

H=L^{2}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})
. Then for
\nu>0
small enough,
\Delta:=[-\nu,\nu]\ast w
is
a
C^{1}
embedded submanifold and
T_{w}\Delta=\mathbb{R}\dot{w}
. Because
(\dot{\bar{w}},\dot{w})_{2}>0
,
the orthogonal decomposition
{H}=({\mathbb{R}}\dot{\bar{w}})\oplus T_{\bar{w}}B_{H}^{\bot}(\bar{w},\varepsilon)
implies
a direct sum decomposition of Banach spaces

{H}=T_{w}\Delta\dot{+}T_{w}B_{{H}^{\bot}}(\bar{w},\varepsilon).

(4.19)

Since
(\psi_{\kappa})_{\lambda}
is invariant for the
{\mathbb{R}}
-action given by (
4.2
),

d(\psi_{\kappa})_{\lambda}(w)[\xi]=0\;\forall\xi\in T_{w}\Delta
.
From this, (
4.12
) and (
4.19
) it follows that
d(\psi_{\kappa})_{\lambda}(w)=0
.
∎

Consider the following Banach space and functional

\displaystyle X^{\bot}:=\{u\in C^{0}_{M}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})\,|\,(\dot{\bar{w}},u)_{2}=0\},

(4.20)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{\bot}_{\lambda}:H^{\bot}\to{\mathbb{R}},\;u\mapsto\mathcal{L}_{\lambda}(u)=\psi_{\kappa}(\lambda,\bar{w}+u).

(4.21)

They are invariant under the isotropy group
\mathbb{R}_{\bar{w}}
, and
d\mathcal{L}^{\bot}_{\lambda}(0)=0\;\forall\lambda
.
Moreover, by shrinking
\varepsilon>0
if necessary, Proposition
4.5
shows that for
u\in B_{H^{\bot}}(0,\varepsilon)
,

d\mathcal{L}^{\bot}_{\lambda}(u)=0\;\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad d\mathcal{L}_{\lambda}(u)=d(\psi_{\kappa})_{\lambda}(\bar{w}+u)=0.

(4.22)

Denote by
\Pi:H\to H^{\bot}
the orthogonal projection. Then
\Pi(u)=u-\frac{(u,\dot{\bar{w}})_{2}}{(\|\dot{\bar{w}}\|_{2})^{2}}\dot{\bar{w}}
for
u\in H
, and

\nabla\mathcal{L}^{\bot}_{\lambda}(u)=\nabla(\psi_{\kappa})_{\lambda}(\bar{w}+u)-\frac{(\nabla(\psi_{\kappa})_{\lambda}(\bar{w}+u),\dot{\bar{w}})_{2}}{(\|\dot{\bar{w}}\|_{2})^{2}}\dot{\bar{w}}\quad\forall u\in H^{\bot},

(4.23)

where
\nabla(\psi_{\kappa})_{\lambda}(\bar{w}+u)=A_{M,\tau,\kappa}(\bar{w}+u)+\nabla_{z}H_{\kappa}^{\ast}(\lambda,\bar{w}(\cdot)+v(\cdot))
by (
3.14
).
Since
\dot{\bar{w}}
is
C^{1}
, it easily follows from (
4.23
)
that
\nabla\mathcal{L}^{\bot}_{\lambda}(u)\in X^{\bot}
for any
u\in X^{\bot}
, and

\mathfrak{A}_{\lambda}:X^{\bot}\to X^{\bot},\;u\mapsto\nabla\mathcal{L}^{\bot}_{\lambda}(u)

(4.24)

is
C^{1}
. For any
(\lambda,x),(\lambda_{0},x_{0})\in\Lambda\times X^{\bot}
, noting that both
\|\cdot\|_{X}
and
\|\cdot\|_{X^{\bot}}

are
\|\cdot\|_{C^{0}}
, and

\displaystyle\mathfrak{A}_{\lambda}(x)-\mathfrak{A}_{\lambda_{0}}(x_{0})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\nabla(\psi_{\kappa})_{\lambda}(\bar{w}+x)-\nabla(\psi_{\kappa})_{\lambda_{0}}(\bar{w}+x_{0})

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\frac{(\nabla(\psi_{\kappa})_{\lambda}(\bar{w}+x)-\nabla(\psi_{\kappa})_{\lambda_{0}}(\bar{w}+x_{0}),\dot{\bar{w}})_{2}}{(\|\dot{\bar{w}}\|_{2})^{2}}\dot{\bar{w}}

we deduce

\displaystyle\|\mathfrak{A}_{\lambda}(x)-\mathfrak{A}_{\lambda_{0}}(x_{0})\|_{X^{\bot}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|\nabla(\psi_{\kappa})_{\lambda}(\bar{w}+x)-\nabla(\psi_{\kappa})_{\lambda_{0}}(\bar{w}+x_{0})\|_{X}

\displaystyle+
