\displaystyle\sqrt{\tau}\|\nabla(\psi_{\kappa})_{\lambda}(\bar{w}+x)-\nabla(\psi_{\kappa})_{\lambda_{0}}(\bar{w}+x_{0})\|_{X}\|\dot{\bar{w}}\|_{C^{0}}.

By Step 2 of the proof of Theorem
1.4
(II),

\Lambda\times X\ni(\lambda,v)\mapsto\nabla(\psi_{\kappa})_{\lambda}(v)=\nabla_{v}\psi_{\kappa}(\lambda,v)\in X
is continuous.
This and (
4.2
) lead to

Claim 4.6
.

\Lambda\times X^{\bot}\ni(\lambda,x)\mapsto\mathfrak{A}_{\lambda}(x)\in X^{\bot}
is continuous.

From (
3.14
) and (
3.15
) we may deduce that

\nabla\mathcal{L}^{\bot}_{\lambda}
has a Gâteaux derivative at
u\in H^{\bot}
,

\mathfrak{B}_{\lambda}(u)\in\mathscr{L}_{s}(H^{\bot})
, given by

\mathfrak{B}_{\lambda}(u)v=B_{\lambda}(u+\bar{w})v-\frac{(B_{\lambda}(u+\bar{w})v,\dot{\bar{w}})_{2}}{(\|\dot{\bar{w}}\|_{2})^{2}}\dot{\bar{w}}\quad\forall v\in H^{\bot},

(4.25)

where
B_{\lambda}(u+\bar{w})=A_{M,\tau,\kappa}+\nabla^{2}_{z}H_{\kappa}^{\ast}({\lambda};u(\cdot)+\bar{w}(\cdot))

by (
3.15
). Since
P_{\lambda}(v)=\nabla^{2}_{z}H_{\kappa}^{\ast}({\lambda};v(\cdot))

(resp.
Q_{\lambda}(v)=A_{M,\tau,\kappa}
) is a positive definite (resp. compact self-adjoint) operator on
H
,

\mathfrak{P}_{\lambda}(u):=\Pi\circ P_{\lambda}(u+\bar{w})|_{H^{\bot}}\quad\hbox{(resp.\quad$\mathfrak{Q}_{\lambda}(u):=\Pi\circ Q_{\lambda}(u+\bar{w})|_{H^{\bot}}$)}

(4.26)

is a positive definite (resp. compact self-adjoint) operator on
H^{\bot}
. Moreover, it is clear that

\mathfrak{B}_{\lambda}(u)=\mathfrak{P}_{\lambda}(u)+\mathfrak{Q}_{\lambda}(u)
.

Proposition 4.7
.

(H^{\bot},X^{\bot},\mathcal{L}^{\bot}_{\lambda},\mathfrak{A}_{\lambda},\mathfrak{B}_{\lambda})

satisfies
[
50
, Hypothesis 1.1]
(
[
52
, Hypothesis 3.1]
) and
[
50
, Hypothesis 1.3]
(
[
52
, Hypothesis 3.2]
).

Proof.

It remains to prove that

(H^{\bot},X^{\bot},\mathcal{L}^{\bot}_{\lambda},\mathfrak{A}_{\lambda},\mathfrak{B}_{\lambda})

satisfies (D1) in
[
50
, Hypothesis 1.1]
(
[
52
, Hypothesis 3.1]
) and (C) in

[
50
, Hypothesis 1.3]
(
[
52
, Hypothesis 3.2]
), that is,

\{u\in H^{\bot}\,|\,\mathfrak{B}_{\lambda}(0)u=su,\,s\leq 0\}\subset X^{\bot}\quad\hbox{and}\quad\{u\in H^{\bot}\,|\,\mathfrak{B}_{\lambda}(0)u\in X^{\bot}\}\subset X^{\bot}.

(4.27)

In fact, if
u\in H^{\bot}
and
\varrho\leq 0
satisfy
\mathfrak{B}_{\lambda}(0)u=\varrho u
,
then
B_{\lambda}(\bar{w})u=\varrho u
by (
4.25
) and (
4.11
).
The first inclusion in (
3.22
) leads to
u\in X
and so
u\in X^{\bot}
.
Similarly, if
u\in H^{\bot}
is such that
v:=\mathfrak{B}_{\lambda}(0)u\in X^{\bot}
,
(
4.25
) and (
4.11
) lead to
B_{\lambda}(\bar{w})u=v
, and so the second claim
by the second inclusion in (
3.22
).
∎

Note that (
4.11
) implies
{\mathbb{R}}\dot{\bar{w}}\subset{\rm Ker}(B_{\lambda}(\bar{w}))
. By (
3.23
) and (
3.24
) we get

\displaystyle m^{-}(\mathcal{L}^{\bot}_{\lambda},0)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle m^{-}(({\psi}_{\kappa})_{\lambda},\bar{w})=i_{\tau,M}(\gamma_{\lambda})-i_{\tau,M}(\gamma_{\kappa I_{2n}})-\nu_{\tau,M}(\gamma_{\kappa I_{2n}}),

(4.28)

\displaystyle m^{0}(\mathcal{L}^{\bot}_{\lambda},0)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle m^{0}(({\psi}_{\kappa})_{\lambda},\bar{w})-1=\dim{\rm Ker}(\gamma_{\lambda}(\tau)-M)-1.

(4.29)

Proof of Theorem
1.18
.

In the proof of Theorem
1.4
we proved that

\{\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}(\cdot):=(\psi_{\kappa})_{\lambda}(\bar{w}+\cdot)\,|\,\lambda\in\Lambda\}

satisfies the conditions of
Theorem
C.6
(
[
50
, Theorem 3.1]
) with
H=X=L^{2}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})
.
Thus if
\lambda_{k}\to\mu
and
(x_{k})\subset H^{\bot}
converges
0

it follows from (
4.26
) that

\displaystyle\|\mathfrak{P}_{\lambda_{k}}(x_{k})h-\mathfrak{P}_{\mu}(0)h\|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\|\Pi\circ P_{\lambda_{k}}(x_{k}+\bar{w})h-\Pi\circ P_{\mu}(\bar{w})h\|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\|P_{\lambda_{k}}(x_{k}+\bar{w})h-P_{\mu}(\bar{w})h\|\to 0,\quad\forall h\in H^{\bot}.

Similarly, we have
\|\mathfrak{Q}_{\lambda_{k}}(0)-\mathfrak{Q}_{\mu}(0)\|_{\mathscr{L}(H^{\bot})}\leq\|Q_{\lambda_{k}}(\bar{w})-Q_{\mu}(\bar{w})\|_{\mathscr{L}(H^{\bot})}\to 0
.
Moreover, for
x\in H^{\bot}
near
0
it is easily seen that

\displaystyle(\mathfrak{P}_{\lambda}(x)h,h)_{H}=(P_{\lambda}(x+\bar{w})h,h)_{H},\quad\forall h\in H^{\bot},

\displaystyle\|\mathfrak{Q}_{\lambda}(x)-\mathfrak{Q}_{\lambda}(0)\|_{\mathscr{L}(H^{\bot})}\leq\|Q_{\lambda}(x+\bar{w})-Q_{\lambda}(\bar{w})\|_{\mathscr{L}(H)}.

These imply that the conditions (i)-(ii) in
[
50
, Theorem 3.1]
(cf. Theorem
C.6
)
can be satisfied with
\mathfrak{P}_{\lambda}
and
\mathfrak{Q}_{\lambda}
near
0\in H^{\bot}
.
Hence Theorem
C.6
(
[
50
, Theorem 3.1]
) is applicable to

\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}:=\mathcal{L}^{\bot}_{\lambda}
near
0\in H^{\bot}
.

Let
(\lambda_{k},v_{k})
be as in Theorem
1.18
. Then
\lambda_{k}\to\mu
,
\|v_{k}|_{[0,\tau]}-\bar{v}|_{[0,\tau]}\|_{C^{0}}\to 0

and each
w_{k}:=-\Lambda_{M,\tau,\kappa I_{2n}}(v_{k}|_{[0,\tau]})
is a critical point of
(\psi_{\kappa})_{\lambda_{k}}
on
L^{2}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})
.
By Proposition
1.3
,
\|v_{k}|_{[0,\tau]}-\bar{v}|_{[0,\tau]}\|_{C^{1}}\to 0

and hence
\|w_{k}-\bar{w}\|_{C^{0}}\to 0
.
Take
0<\epsilon_{m}<\varepsilon
such that
\epsilon_{m}\to 0
.
Since each
{\mathbb{R}}\ast B_{{H}^{\bot}}(\bar{w},\epsilon_{m})
is a neighborhood of the orbit
\mathcal{O}={\mathbb{R}}\ast\bar{w}
by Proposition
4.3
,
we have a subsequence
(w_{k_{m}})
of
(w_{k})
such that
w_{k_{m}}\in\mathbb{R}\ast B_{{H}^{\bot}}(\bar{w},\epsilon_{m})
for all
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
that is, there exists a
\theta_{m}\in{\mathbb{R}}
such that
u_{m}:=\theta_{m}\ast w_{k_{m}}\in B_{{H}^{\bot}}(\bar{w},\epsilon_{m})\subset B_{{H}^{\bot}}(\bar{w},\varepsilon)
for each
m\in\mathbb{N}
.
Note that
\|u_{m}-\bar{w}\|_{2}<\epsilon_{m}\to 0
and that each
u_{m}
is a critical point of
\psi_{\kappa}(\lambda_{k_{m}},\cdot)
on

L^{2}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})
.
The latter implies that each
u_{m}-\bar{w}
is also a critical point of
\mathcal{L}_{\lambda_{k_{m}}}^{\bot}
.
Since all
v_{k}
are
{\mathbb{R}}
-distinct, any two of all
w_{k}
belong to different
{\mathbb{R}}
-orbits. It follows that all
u_{m}-\bar{w}
are distinct each other.
These show that
(\mu,0)
is a bifurcation point of
\nabla\mathcal{L}^{\bot}_{\lambda}(u)=0
in
\Lambda\times H^{\bot}
.
By Theorem
C.6
(
[
50
, Theorem 3.1]
) we get
m^{0}(\mathcal{L}^{\bot}_{\mu},0)>0
.
From (
4.29
) it follows that

\dim{\rm Ker}(\gamma_{\lambda}(\tau)-M)>1
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.19
.

Follow the notations above. In the proof of Theorem
1.4
(II) we proved that

\{\mathcal{L}_{\lambda}(\cdot)=\psi_{\kappa}(\lambda,\bar{w}+\cdot)\,|\,\lambda\in\Lambda\}
satisfies the conditions of Theorem
C.4

on
H=L^{2}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})
and
X=C^{0}_{M}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})
.
By Claim
4.6
, Proposition
4.7
and the proof of Theorem
1.18
,
the conditions of Theorem
C.4
are also satisfied for
\{(H^{\bot},X^{\bot},\mathcal{L}^{\bot}_{\lambda},\mathfrak{A}_{\lambda},\mathfrak{B}_{\lambda})\,|\,\lambda\in\Lambda\}
.

Note that using (
4.28
)-(
4.29
) we can translate the assumptions (a)-(c) of Theorem
1.19

as follows:

(
{\rm a}^{\prime}
)

The orbit
{\cal O}:={\mathbb{R}}\ast\bar{w}
is an embedded circle
(i.e.,
\mathbb{R}_{\bar{w}}
is an infinite cyclic subgroup of
{\mathbb{R}}
with generator
p>0
).

(
{\rm b}^{\prime}
)

m^{0}(\mathcal{L}^{\bot}_{\mu},0)\geq 1
, for each
k\in\mathbb{N}
,
\lambda_{k}^{-}\neq\lambda_{k}^{+}
,

[m^{-}(\mathcal{L}^{\bot}_{\lambda_{k}^{-}},0),m^{-}(\mathcal{L}^{\bot}_{\lambda_{k}^{-}},0)+m^{0}(\mathcal{L}^{\bot}_{\lambda_{k}^{-}},0)]\cap[m^{-}(\mathcal{L}^{\bot}_{\lambda_{k}^{+}},0),m^{-}(\mathcal{L}^{\bot}_{\lambda_{k}^{+}},0)+m^{0}(\mathcal{L}^{\bot}_{\lambda_{k}^{+}},0)]=\emptyset,

and either
m^{0}(\mathcal{L}^{\bot}_{\lambda_{k}^{+}},0)=0
or
m^{0}(\mathcal{L}^{\bot}_{\lambda_{k}^{-}},0)=0
.

(
{\rm c}^{\prime}
)

For any critical point
w
of
\psi_{\kappa}(\mu,\cdot)
which sits in
X
,
if there exists a sequence
(s_{k})
of reals such that
s_{k}\cdot w

converges to
\bar{w}
in
X
, then
w
is periodic, and so
\mathbb{R}\ast w
is closed.
(Clearly, this holds if
M^{l}=id_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}
for some
l\in\mathbb{N}
.)

By (
{\rm b}^{\prime}
) we may use Theorem
C.4
to get
m^{0}(\mathcal{L}^{\bot}_{\mu},0)>0

and a sequence
\{(\lambda_{k},u_{k})\}_{k\geq 1}
in
\hat{\Lambda}\times H^{\bot}

converging to
(\mu,0)
, where
\hat{\Lambda}:=\{\mu,\lambda^{+}_{k},\lambda^{-}_{k}\,|\,k\in\mathbb{N}\}
, such that each
u_{k}

is a nonzero solution of
\nabla\mathcal{L}^{\bot}_{\lambda_{k}}(u)=0
,
k=1,2,\cdots
.
Clearly, we can assume
u_{k}\in B_{H^{\bot}}(0,\varepsilon)\;\forall k\in\mathbb{N}
.
By Proposition
4.5

w_{k}:=\bar{w}+u_{k}\in B_{H}^{\bot}(\bar{w},\varepsilon)
is
C^{1}
, satisfies
d(\psi_{\kappa})_{\lambda_{k}}(w_{k})=0
and
\|w_{k}-\bar{w}\|_{C^{1}}\to 0
.

(4.30)

From now on we
use (
{\rm a}^{\prime}
), i.e., the orbit
\mathcal{O}={\mathbb{R}}\ast\bar{w}
is the
C^{1}
embedded circle
.
Since it is transversely intersecting with
B_{H}^{\bot}(\bar{w},\varepsilon)
at
\bar{w}
.
By shrinking
\varepsilon>0
we may assume

\mathcal{O}\cap B_{H}^{\bot}(\bar{w},\varepsilon)=\{\bar{w}\}.

It follows from this and (
4.30
) that there exists
k_{0}>0
such that for each
k>k_{0}
,

\mathbb{R}\ast w_{k}
is an immersed
C^{1}
submanifold which
transversely intersects with
B_{H}^{\bot}(\bar{w},\varepsilon)
at
w_{k}
. Hence
\mathbb{R}\ast w_{k}\neq\mathcal{O}
for any
k>k_{0}
.
(Otherwise,
\mathcal{O}
and
B_{H}^{\bot}(\bar{w},\varepsilon)
have at least two distinct intersecting
points
w_{k}
and
\bar{w}
in
B_{H}^{\bot}(\bar{w},\varepsilon)
.)

Finally, we conclude that
\{\mathbb{R}\ast w_{k}\,|\,k\in\mathbb{N}\}
is an infinite set.
(Thus
(w_{k})
has a subsequence which only consists of
{\mathbb{R}}
-distinct elements, also denoted by
(w_{k})
for brevity.
For each
k=1,2,\cdots
, let
v_{k}
be defined by
-(\Lambda_{M,\tau,\kappa I_{2n}})^{-1}w_{k}
via
(
1.7
). Then
\{(\lambda_{k},v_{k})\}_{k\geq 1}
is the required one. The proof is completed.)
Otherwise, passing to a subsequence we may assume that all
w_{k}
belong to an
{\mathbb{R}}
-orbit , i.e.,

w_{k}=s_{k}\ast\hat{w}
for some
s_{k}\in\mathbb{R}
,
where
\hat{w}\in X
satisfies
d(\psi_{\kappa})_{\lambda_{k}}(\hat{w})=d(\psi_{\kappa})_{\lambda_{k}}({w}_{k})=0
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
.
Since
\Lambda\times X\ni(\lambda,v)\mapsto\nabla_{v}\psi_{\kappa}(\lambda,v)\in X
is continuous, and
\lambda_{k}\to\mu
,
we obtain
d(\psi_{\kappa})_{\mu}(\hat{w})=0
.
Clearly,
\bar{w}
sits in the intersection of
\mathcal{O}
and the closure of
\mathbb{R}\ast\hat{w}
. By the assumption
(
{\rm c}^{\prime}
),
\mathbb{R}\ast\hat{w}
is closed and so equal to
\mathcal{O}
, which contradicts the claim that

\mathcal{O}\neq\mathbb{R}\ast w_{k}=\mathbb{R}\ast\hat{w}
for any
k>k_{0}

in the last paragraph.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.20
.

The first two paragraphs in the proof of Theorem
1.19
are still valid unless
we replace
\Lambda
and
\hat{\Lambda}
by
\alpha([0,1])
, and
(
{\rm b}^{\prime}
) by

(
{\rm d}^{\prime}
)

[m^{-}(\mathcal{L}^{\bot}_{\lambda^{-}},0),m^{-}(\mathcal{L}^{\bot}_{\lambda^{-}},0)+m^{0}(\mathcal{L}^{\bot}_{\lambda^{-}},0)]\cap[m^{-}(\mathcal{L}^{\bot}_{\lambda^{+}},0),m^{-}(\mathcal{L}^{\bot}_{\lambda^{+}},0)+m^{0}(\mathcal{L}^{\bot}_{\lambda^{+}},0)]=\emptyset
,
and either
m^{0}(\mathcal{L}^{\bot}_{\lambda^{+}},0)=0
or
m^{0}(\mathcal{L}^{\bot}_{\lambda^{-}},0)=0
.

By Theorem
C.5
there exists a sequence
\{(t_{k},u_{k})\}_{k\geq 1}
in
[0,1]\times H^{\bot}

converging to
(\bar{t},0)
, such that each
u_{k}

is a nonzero solution of
\nabla\mathcal{L}^{\bot}_{\alpha(t_{k})}(u)=0
,
k=1,2,\cdots
.
Moreover,
\alpha(\bar{t})\neq\lambda^{+}
(resp.
\mu\neq\lambda^{-}
) if
m^{0}(\mathcal{L}^{\bot}_{\lambda^{+}},0)=0
(resp.
m^{0}(\mathcal{L}^{\bot}_{\lambda^{-}},0)=0
). As in the proof of Theorem
1.19

we may assume
u_{k}\in B_{H^{\bot}}(0,\varepsilon)\;\forall k\in\mathbb{N}
and have
(
4.30
). Letting
w_{k}:=\bar{w}+u_{k}
for each
k\in\mathbb{N}
and repeating other arguments in the proof of Theorem
1.19
the required results can be obtained.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.21
.

Part (I) follows from Theorem
1.19
directly.
In order to prove Parts (II) and (III),
following the first paragraph of the proof of Theorem
1.19
and
comparing conditions in Theorem
C.4

it is easily seen that
(\mathcal{L}^{\bot}_{\lambda},H^{\bot},X^{\bot})
with
\lambda\in\Lambda
satisfy
the conditions in Theorem
C.4
except for the condition (f) with
\lambda^{\ast}=\mu
.
As in the proof of Theorem
1.7
we may use
(
4.28
)-(
4.29
) and the assumptions about Morse indexes in Theorem
1.21

to check that the condition (f) can be satisfied, that is,

m^{0}(\mathcal{L}^{\bot}_{\mu},0)=\nu_{\tau}(\gamma_{\mu})-1>0
, and

m^{-}(\mathcal{L}^{\bot}_{\lambda},0)
takes values
m^{-}(\mathcal{L}^{\bot}_{\mu},0)=i_{\tau}(\gamma_{\mu})
and
m^{-}(\mathcal{L}^{\bot}_{\mu},0)+m^{0}(\mathcal{L}^{\bot}_{\mu},0)=i_{\tau}(\gamma_{\mu})+\nu_{\tau}(\gamma_{\mu})-1

as
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
varies in two deleted half neighborhoods of
\mu
.

Since
M=I_{2n}
, the
\mathbb{R}
-action on
H
is actually a
S^{1}=\mathbb{R}/(\tau\mathbb{Z})
action,

[\theta]\diamond x=(\theta+k\tau)\ast x,\quad\forall x\in H,\;[\theta]=\theta+\tau\mathbb{Z}\in S^{1},\;k\in\mathbb{Z}.

This is a continuous action on
H
(resp.
X
) via Hilbert (resp. Banach) isometry isomorphisms.

Claim 4.8
.

The isotropy group of the above
S^{1}
-action at
\bar{w}
is

S^{1}_{\bar{w}}=\left\{\left[\frac{l\tau}{p}\right]\,\Bigg|\,l=0,\cdots,p-1\right\}\cong\mathbb{Z}_{p}:=\mathbb{Z}/p\mathbb{Z},

and
[\theta]\diamond\dot{\bar{w}}=\dot{\bar{w}}
for any
[\theta]\in S^{1}_{\bar{w}}
.

Proof.

Recall that
\bar{u}=\bar{v}|_{[0,\tau]}
and
\bar{w}=-\Lambda_{I_{2n},\tau,\kappa I_{2n}}\bar{u}
.
Suppose
[\theta]\diamond\bar{w}=\bar{w}
, i.e.,
(\theta+k\tau)\ast\bar{w}=\bar{w}
.
Note that
\Lambda_{I_{2n},\tau,\kappa I_{2n}}
is the equivariant isomorphism.
Hence
(\theta+k\tau)\ast\bar{u}=\bar{u}
. That is,

(\bar{u})^{I_{2n}}(\theta+k\tau+t)=\bar{u}(t),\;\forall 0\leq t\leq\tau.

Since
(\bar{u})^{I_{2n}}=\bar{v}
and
\bar{v}
has the minimal period
\tau/p
, this means that

\bar{v}(\theta+t)=\bar{v}(t)\;\forall 0\leq t\leq\tau
and so
\bar{v}(\theta+t)=\bar{v}(t)\;\forall t\in\mathbb{R}
. It follows that

\theta\in\bigcup^{p-1}_{l=0}\left(\mathbb{Z}\tau+\frac{l\tau}{p}\mathbb{Z}\right).

Moreover, it is easily checked that such a
\theta
satisfies
[\theta]\diamond\bar{w}=\bar{w}
.
The first claim follows.

Since
\bar{u}
is
C^{2}
, and
\bar{w}=-\Lambda_{M,\tau,\kappa I_{2n}}(\bar{u})=-J\dot{\bar{u}}-\kappa\bar{u}
,
we deduce
\dot{\bar{w}}=-\Lambda_{M,\tau,\kappa I_{2n}}(\dot{\bar{u}})
.
Note that
\tau/p
is also a period of
\dot{\bar{v}}
and
\dot{\bar{u}}=\dot{\bar{v}}|_{[0,\tau]}
.
It must hold that
[\theta]\diamond\dot{\bar{w}}=\dot{\bar{w}}
for any
[\theta]\in S^{1}_{\bar{w}}
.
∎

Recall that
H^{\bot}
is the orthogonal complementary of
\dot{\bar{w}}
in
H=L^{2}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})
and

X^{\bot}=X\cap H^{\bot}
. By the second conclusion in Claim
4.8

spaces
H^{\bot},X^{\bot}
and each functional
\mathcal{L}^{\bot}_{\lambda}
are
S^{1}_{\bar{w}}
-invariant.

Since
\{[\theta]\diamond\bar{u}\,|\,[\theta]\in S^{1}\}=\{\theta\ast\bar{u}\,|\,\theta\in{\mathbb{R}}\}
is a
C^{2}
-embedded circle,
by Claim
4.2
the orbit
\mathcal{O}:=\{[\theta]\diamond\bar{w}\,|\,[\theta]\in S^{1}\}

is a
C^{2}
-embedded circle in
L^{2}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})
and

T_{\bar{w}}\mathcal{O}=\mathbb{R}\dot{\bar{w}}
.
Let
\pi:N{\cal O}\to{\cal O}
be the normal bundle
of
{\cal O}
in
H
. It is a
C^{1}

Hilbert vector bundle over
{\cal O}
and its fibre
N{\cal O}_{\bar{w}}
at
\bar{w}
is equal to
H^{\bot}
.
Let
N{\cal O}(\varepsilon):=\{(w,x)\in N{\cal O}\,|\,\|x\|_{2}<\varepsilon\}
and define

{\rm EXP}:N{\cal O}(\varepsilon)\to H,\;(w,x)\mapsto w+x.

Clearly,
{\rm EXP}
is equivariant, i.e.,

[\theta]\diamond({\rm EXP}(w,x))={\rm EXP}([\theta]\diamond w,[\theta]\diamond x)
for
all
[\theta]\in S^{1}
. By shrinking
\varepsilon>0
,
{\rm EXP}
gives rise to a
C^{1}

diffeomorphism
\digamma
from
N{\cal O}(\varepsilon)
to
an open neighborhood
{\cal N}({\cal O},\varepsilon)
of
{\cal O}
in
H
.
Let
x,y\in N{\cal O}(\varepsilon)_{\bar{w}}=B_{H}^{\bot}(\bar{w},\varepsilon)
such that

[\theta]\diamond(\bar{w}+x)=\bar{w}+y\quad\hbox{for some}\;[\theta]\in S^{1}=\mathbb{R}/(\tau\mathbb{Z}).

Then
[\theta]\diamond\bar{w}+[\theta]\diamond x=\bar{w}+y
, i.e.,

{\rm EXP}([\theta]\diamond\bar{w},[\theta]\diamond x)={\rm EXP}(\bar{w},y)
.
It follows that
[\theta]\diamond\bar{w}=\bar{w}
and
[\theta]\diamond x=y
.
The former shows
[\theta]\in S^{1}_{\bar{w}}
, and the latter implies that
x
and
y

sit in the same
S^{1}_{\bar{w}}
-orbit. Therefore we have proved:

Claim 4.9
.

If
x,y\in N{\cal O}(\varepsilon)_{\bar{w}}=B_{H}^{\bot}(\bar{w},\varepsilon)
belong to
distinct
S^{1}_{\bar{w}}
-orbits, then
S^{1}\diamond(\bar{w}+x)\neq S^{1}\diamond(\bar{w}+y)
.

Claim 4.10
.

The fixed point set of
S^{1}_{\bar{w}}
-action on

{\rm Ker}(\mathfrak{B}_{\lambda}(0))
,

\{x\in{\rm Ker}(\mathfrak{B}_{\lambda}(0))\,|\,[\theta]\diamond x=x\;\forall[\theta]\in S^{1}_{\bar{w}}\},

is a linear subspace of dimension
\nu_{\tau/p}(\gamma_{\mu})-1
.

Proof.

Since
B_{\lambda}(\bar{w})\dot{\bar{w}}=0
, by (
4.17
)
we have

{\rm Ker}(\mathfrak{B}_{\lambda}(0))=\{x\in H^{\bot}\,|\,B_{\lambda}(\bar{w})x=0\}.

Note that
[\tau/p]
is a generator of
S^{1}_{\bar{w}}
.
x\in{\rm Ker}(\mathfrak{B}_{\lambda}(0))

is a fixed point of
S^{1}_{\bar{w}}
-action if and only if
[\tau/p]\diamond x=x
, i.e.,
(\tau/p)\ast x=x
.
By (
4.9
) and (
4.10
),

\displaystyle(\Lambda_{I_{2n},\tau,{\kappa I_{2n}}})^{-1}\bar{w}+\nabla(H_{\kappa}^{\ast})_{\lambda}(\bar{w}(\cdot))=A_{I_{2n},\tau,\kappa}\bar{w}+\nabla(H_{\kappa}^{\ast})_{\lambda}(\bar{w}(\cdot))=0,

\displaystyle(\Lambda_{I_{2n},\tau,{\kappa I_{2n}}})^{-1}x+(H_{\kappa}^{\ast})^{\prime\prime}_{\lambda}(\bar{w}(\cdot))x=A_{M,\tau,\kappa}x+(H_{\kappa}^{\ast})^{\prime\prime}_{\lambda}(\bar{w}(\cdot))x=0.

Let
\bar{u}=-(\Lambda_{I_{2n},\tau,{\kappa I_{2n}}})^{-1}\bar{w}
. Then
\bar{u}=\nabla(H_{\kappa}^{\ast})_{\lambda}(\bar{w}(\cdot))

and so
\bar{w}=\nabla(H_{\kappa})_{\lambda}(\bar{u}(\cdot))
and

I_{2n}=(H_{\kappa}^{\ast})^{\prime\prime}_{\lambda}(\bar{w}(\cdot))(H_{\kappa})^{\prime\prime}_{\lambda}(\bar{u}(\cdot)).

It follows from these that
y:=(\Lambda_{I_{2n},\tau,{\kappa I_{2n}}})^{-1}x
satisfies

\dot{y}(t)=JH_{\lambda}^{\prime\prime}(\bar{u}(t))y(t),\quad 0\leq t\leq\tau.

Moreover,
(\tau/p)\ast y=y
implies
y^{I_{2n}}
has a period
\tau/p
.
Hence the desired claim follows.
∎

Proof of (II)
. Since
m^{0}(\mathcal{L}^{\bot}_{\mu},0)=\nu_{\tau}(\gamma_{\mu})-1>1
,
Theorem
C.7
(
[
52
, Theorem 3.6]
) implies that one of the following alternatives occurs:

(
{\rm i}^{\prime}
)

(\mu,0)
is not an isolated solution in
\{\mu\}\times H^{\bot}
of the equation

\mathfrak{A}_{\lambda}(x)=0
.

(
{\rm ii}^{\prime}
)

For every
\lambda\in\Lambda
near
\mu
there is a nontrivial solution
x_{\lambda}
of

\mathfrak{A}_{\lambda}(x)=0
in
X^{\bot}
,
which converges to
0
in
X^{\bot}
as
\lambda\to\mu
.

(
{\rm iii}^{\prime}
)

For any neighborhood
\mathcal{N}
of
0
in
X^{\bot}
, which may be assumed to be
S^{1}_{\bar{w}}
-invariant,
there is an one-sided neighborhood
\Lambda^{0}
of
\mu
in
\Lambda
such that
for any
\lambda\in\Lambda^{0}\setminus\{\mu\}
,
\mathfrak{A}_{\lambda}(x)=0

has at least two nontrivial solutions
x_{\lambda}^{1}
and
x_{\lambda}^{2}
in
\mathcal{N}
with different energy
if
\mathfrak{A}_{\lambda}(x)=0
has only finitely many nontrivial solutions in
\mathcal{N}
.

By (
{\rm i}^{\prime}
) we have a sequence
(x_{k})\subset H^{\bot}\setminus\{0\}

converging to
0
in
H^{\bot}
such that
\nabla\mathcal{L}^{\bot}_{\mu}(x_{k})=0
for each
k
.
Since
S^{1}_{\bar{w}}
is a finite group, by passing to a subsequence (if necessary)
we can assume that all
S^{1}_{\bar{w}}\diamond x_{k}
are distinct each other.
By Proposition
4.5
and Claim
4.9
, all
S^{1}\diamond(\bar{w}+x_{k})=\mathbb{R}\ast(\bar{w}+x_{k})

are distinct critical orbits of
\psi_{\kappa}(\mu,\cdot)
and
\bar{w}+x_{k}\to\bar{w}
in
H
.
For each
k=1,2,\cdots
, set

u_{k}:=-(\Lambda_{I_{2n},\tau,\kappa I_{2n}})^{-1}(\bar{w}+x_{k})\quad\hbox{and}\quad v_{k}:=(u_{k})^{I_{2n}}.

Then
\|u_{k}-\bar{v}|_{[0,\tau]}\|_{1,2}\to 0
, and therefore
\|u_{k}-\bar{v}|_{[0,\tau]}\|_{C^{1}}\to 0
by Proposition
1.3
.
Hence
(v_{k})
satisfy (II.1).

For every
\lambda\in\Lambda\setminus\{0\}
, let
x_{\lambda}
be as in (
{\rm ii}^{\prime}
).
Set
u_{\lambda}:=-(\Lambda_{I_{2n},\tau,\kappa I_{2n}})^{-1}(\bar{w}+x_{\lambda})
and
v_{\lambda}:=(u_{\lambda})^{I_{2n}}
.
As above these
v_{\lambda}
satisfy (II.2).

By (
{\rm iii}^{\prime}
), for any
\lambda\in\Lambda^{0}\setminus\{\mu\}
,
\mathfrak{A}_{\lambda}(x)=0
has

\bullet
either infinitely many distinct nontrivial solutions in

\mathcal{N}
, and therefore infinitely many distinct nontrivial
S^{1}_{\bar{w}}
-orbit of solutions in

\mathcal{N}
(because of the finiteness of
S^{1}_{\bar{w}}
),
S^{1}_{\bar{w}}\diamond\bar{x}_{\lambda}^{k}
,
k=1,2,\cdots
,

\bullet
or at least two nontrivial
S^{1}_{\bar{w}}
-orbit of solutions in
\mathcal{N}
,

S^{1}_{\bar{w}}\diamond x^{1}_{\lambda}
and
S^{1}_{\bar{w}}\diamond x^{2}_{\lambda}
, such that
\psi_{\kappa}(\lambda,x^{1}_{\lambda})\neq\psi_{\kappa}(\lambda,x^{2}_{\lambda})
.

As above we define
\bar{u}^{k}_{\lambda}:=-(\Lambda_{I_{2n},\tau,\kappa I_{2n}})^{-1}(\bar{w}+\bar{x}^{k}_{\lambda})

and
\bar{v}^{k}_{\lambda}:=(\bar{u}^{k}_{\lambda})^{I_{2n}}
,
k=1,2,\cdots
, and

u^{i}_{\lambda}:=-(\Lambda_{I_{2n},\tau,\kappa I_{2n}})^{-1}(\bar{w}+x^{i}_{\lambda})
and
v^{i}_{\lambda}:=(u^{i}_{\lambda})^{I_{2n}}
,
i=1,2
.
Then
\bar{v}^{k}_{\lambda}
and
v^{i}_{\lambda}
satisfy (II.3).

Proof of (III)
.
Since
\nu_{\tau/p}(\gamma_{\mu})=1
, by Claim

the fixed point set of

-action on

\bullet

, one of (

) and the following (

) occurs:

There exist left and right neighborhoods

and

of

and integers

, such that

,

\mathfrak{A}_{\lambda}(x)=0
has at least

(resp.

in

.

\bullet
If

is a prime, one of (
{\rm i}^{\prime}
) and the following (
{\rm v}^{\prime}
) occurs:

(
{\rm v}^{\prime}
)

The conclusions obtained by
replacing
\nu_{\tau}(\gamma_{\mu})
with

in
(

).

As above, the desired conclusions follow from these and Claim
4.9
.
∎

5
Proofs of Theorems
1.23
,
1.24
,
1.26
and
Corollaries
1.25
,
1.28
,
1.29

Let
\textsf{L}_{\tau}
be the Hilbert subspace of
L^{2}(S_{\tau};{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})
as in (
A.39
).
Below (
A.39
) we have showed that the operator
\Lambda_{I_{2n},\tau,0}
on
L^{2}(S_{\tau};\mathbb{R}^{2n})

defined by (
2.3
) with domain
W^{1,2}(S_{\tau};\mathbb{R}^{2n})

restricts to a closed linear and self-adjoint operator

\tilde{\Lambda}_{I_{2n},\tau,0}

on
\textsf{L}_{\tau}
with domain
\textsf{W}_{\tau}
, and

\sigma(\tilde{\Lambda}_{I_{2n},\tau,0})=\frac{2\pi}{\tau}\mathbb{Z}

consists of only eigenvalues, and each eigenvalues has multiplicity
n
. Then
for each real
\kappa\notin\frac{2\pi}{\tau}\mathbb{Z}
,

\tilde{\Lambda}_{I_{2n},\tau,\kappa I_{2n}}:\textsf{W}_{\tau}\to\textsf{L}_{\tau},\;v\mapsto\tilde{\Lambda}_{I_{2n},\tau,0}v+\kappa v=J\frac{d}{dt}v+\kappa v

(5.1)

is a Banach space isomorphism.

Solutions of (
1.37
) are critical points of the functional

\Phi_{\lambda}(v)=\int^{\tau}_{0}\left[\frac{1}{2}(J\dot{v}(t),v(t))_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}+H(\lambda,t,{v}(t))\right]dt

on the Hilbert space
\textsf{W}_{\tau}
.

Note that the function
\bar{H}:\Lambda\times{\mathbb{R}}\times{{\mathbb{R}}}^{2n}\to{\mathbb{R}}
by

\bar{H}(\lambda,t,z)={H}(\lambda,t,z+u_{\lambda}(t))-(z,\nabla_{z}{H}(\lambda,t,u_{\lambda}(t)))_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}

satisfies Assumption
1.22
, and for each
\lambda\in\Lambda
,
\bar{v}\equiv 0\in\mathbb{R}^{2n}
satisfies

\dot{v}(t)=J\nabla_{z}\bar{H}(\lambda,t,v(t)),\quad v(t+\tau)=v(t)\quad\hbox{and}\quad v(-t)=Nv(t)\;\forall t\in{\mathbb{R}}.

(5.2)

As done above the proof of Theorem
1.4
(I) in Section
3
, replacing
H
by
\bar{H}

in what follows we can assume that
H
satisfies inequalities in (
3.3
) and (
3.4
)
for all
(\lambda,t,z)\in\Lambda\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{2n}
, and
only need to prove Theorems
1.23
–
1.24

in the case where
v_{\lambda}\equiv 0
for all

\lambda\in\Lambda
.

Then we may choose
\kappa\notin\frac{2\pi}{\tau}\mathbb{Z}
,

c_{i}>0
,
i=1,2,3
such that each

H_{\kappa}(\lambda,t,z):=H(\lambda,t,z)-\frac{\kappa}{2}|z|^{2}

satisfies (ii)-(iii) in the proof of Theorem
1.4
(I).
Consider the dual action
\Psi_{\kappa}(\lambda,\cdot):\textsf{W}_{\tau}\to{\mathbb{R}}
defined by (
3.9
).
We obtain a family of
C^{1}
and twice Gâteaux-differentiable functionals

\psi_{\kappa}(\lambda,\cdot)=\Psi_{\kappa}(\lambda,\cdot)\circ(-\tilde{\Lambda}_{I_{2n},\tau,\kappa I_{2n}})^{-1}:\textsf{L}_{\tau}\to{\mathbb{R}}

given by

\psi_{\kappa}(\lambda,v)=\int^{\tau}_{0}\left[\frac{1}{2}\left(v(t),((\tilde{\Lambda}_{I_{2n},\tau,\kappa I_{2n}})^{-1}v)(t)\right)_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}+H_{\kappa}^{\ast}(\lambda,t,v(t))\right]dt,

(5.3)

such that
v\in\textsf{W}_{\tau}
is a critical point of
\Psi_{\kappa}(\lambda,\cdot)
if and only if

w:=-\tilde{\Lambda}_{I_{2n},\tau,\kappa I_{2n}}v
is a critical point of
\psi_{\kappa}(\lambda,\cdot)
.
In particular,
(\mu,0)\in\Lambda\times\textsf{W}_{\tau}
is a bifurcation point of
the problem (
1.37
) if and only if
(\mu,0)\in\Lambda\times\textsf{L}_{\tau}

is a bifurcation point of
\nabla_{w}\psi_{\kappa}(\lambda,w)=0
.

Let
\psi_{\kappa,\lambda}(v)=\psi_{\kappa}(\lambda,v)
.
As in Remark
2.12
we can compute

\displaystyle({\psi}_{\kappa,\lambda})^{\prime\prime}(0)[\xi,\eta]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int^{\tau}_{0}\left[\left((\tilde{\Lambda}_{I_{2n},\tau,\kappa I_{2n}})^{-1}\xi)(t),\eta(t)\right)_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}+\left(\nabla^{2}_{z}H_{\kappa}(\lambda,t;0)\xi(t),\eta(t)\right)_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}\right]dt

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int^{\tau}_{0}\left[\left((\tilde{\Lambda}_{I_{2n},\tau,\kappa I_{2n}})^{-1}\xi)(t)+[\nabla^{2}_{z}H_{\kappa}(\lambda,t,0)]^{-1}\xi(t),\eta(t)\right)_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}\right]dt

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\textsf{Q}_{B_{\lambda},\kappa I_{2n}}(\xi,\eta),\quad\forall\xi,\eta\in\textsf{{L}}_{\tau},

where
\textsf{Q}_{B_{\lambda},\kappa I_{2n}}
is given by (
A.41
) with
B_{\lambda}(t)=\nabla^{2}_{z}H(\lambda,t,0)
.
By Theorem
A.7
we obtain

\displaystyle m^{-}({\psi}_{\kappa,\lambda},0)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mu_{1,\tau}(\gamma_{\lambda})-n[\kappa\tau/(2\pi)],

(5.4)

\displaystyle m^{0}({\psi}_{\kappa,\lambda},0)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\nu_{1,\tau}(\gamma_{\lambda}).

(5.5)

Let
\iota:\textsf{W}_{\tau}\to\textsf{L}_{\tau}
be the inclusion. Then

\tilde{A}_{I_{2n},\tau,\kappa}:\textsf{L}_{\tau}\to\textsf{L}_{\tau},\;v\mapsto\iota\circ(\tilde{\Lambda}_{I_{2n},\tau,\kappa I_{2n}})^{-1}v,

(5.6)

is a compact self-adjoint operator, and

\psi_{\kappa}(\lambda,\cdot)
has the gradient

\nabla_{v}\psi_{\kappa}(\lambda,v)=\tilde{A}_{I_{2n},\tau,\kappa}v+\nabla_{z}H_{\kappa}^{\ast}(\lambda,\cdot;v(\cdot)).

(5.7)

Moreover,
\textsf{L}_{\tau}\ni v\mapsto\nabla_{v}\psi_{\kappa}(\lambda,v)\in\textsf{L}_{\tau}

has a Gâteaux derivative

B_{\lambda}(v):=D_{v}\nabla_{v}\psi_{\kappa}(\lambda,v)=\tilde{A}_{I_{2n},\tau,\kappa}+\nabla^{2}_{z}H_{\kappa}^{\ast}(\lambda,\cdot;v(\cdot))\in\mathscr{L}_{s}(\textsf{L}_{\tau}).

(5.8)

Let
P_{\lambda}(v)=\nabla^{2}_{z}H_{\kappa}^{\ast}(\lambda,\cdot;v(\cdot))

and
Q_{\lambda}(v)=\tilde{A}_{I_{2n},\tau,\kappa}
.

Proof of Theorem
1.23
(I).

As in the proof of Theorem
1.4

we can show that Theorem
C.6
(
[
50
, Theorem 3.1]
) is applicable to

H=X=\textsf{L}_{\tau}
and
\mathcal{F}_{\lambda}(\cdot)=\psi_{\kappa}(\lambda,\cdot)
.
∎

Consider Banach subspaces of
C^{k}(S_{\tau};{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})
,
\textsf{C}^{k}_{\tau}=C^{k}(S_{\tau};{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})\cap\textsf{L}_{\tau}
,

k=0,1,\cdots
.

Proof of Theorem
1.23
(II).

Note that
\tilde{\Lambda}_{I_{2n},\tau,\kappa I_{2n}}
gives rise to a Banach space isomorphism from

\textsf{C}^{1}_{\tau}
to
\textsf{C}^{0}_{\tau}
, denoted by
\tilde{\Lambda}^{c}_{I_{2n},\tau,\kappa I_{2n}}
.
As in the proof of Theorem

5.4
) and (

)
we can prove that
\mathcal{L}_{\lambda}(\cdot)=\psi_{\kappa}(\lambda,\cdot)

with

satisfies the conditions of Theorem
C.4

with
\lambda^{\ast}=\mu
and
H=\textsf{L}_{\tau}
and

.
∎

with

and

and

.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.26
.

Step 1
. The Lie group
G=\mathbb{Z}_{2}=\{[0],[1]\}

orthogonally acts on
\textsf{L}_{\tau}
and
\textsf{W}_{\tau}
by

[0]\cdot u=u
and
[1]\cdot u=N\cdot u
, where
(N\cdot u)(t)=N(u(t))
.
Clearly, this action induces an isometric action on each space
\textsf{C}_{\tau}^{k}
.
The set of fixed points of this action,
{\rm Fix}_{G}
, is equal to
\{u\in\textsf{L}_{\tau}\,|\,u(-t)=u(t),{\rm a.e.}\,t\in\mathbb{R}\}
.
Clearly, each functional
\psi_{\kappa}(\lambda,\cdot)
defined by (
5.3
) is invariant under this
G
-action.
As in the proof of Theorem
1.14
we can obtain that
the functional
\mathcal{L}_{\lambda}(\cdot)=\psi_{\kappa}(\lambda,\bar{w}+\cdot)
with
\bar{w}:=-\tilde{\Lambda}_{I_{2n},\tau,K}\bar{v}
satisfies conditions in Theorem
C.7
(
[
52
, Theorem 3.6]
or
[
50
, Theorem 4.6]
)
with
H=\textsf{L}_{\tau}
and
X=\textsf{C}_{\tau}^{0}
.

Since
\bar{v}
is even,
\bar{v}(t)=\bar{v}(-t)=N\bar{v}(t)
for all
t
, that is,
\bar{v}\in{\rm Fix}_{G}
.
Then
\bar{w}
belongs to
{\rm Fix}_{G}
and so

\mathcal{L}_{\lambda}
is
G
-invariant.
The assumption (b) implies that the fixed point set of the induced
G
-action on
H_{\mu}^{0}
is
\{0\}
.
Combing the assumption (a) we can use Theorem
C.8
(
[
52
, Theorem 3.7]
or
[
50
, Theorem 5.12]
) to derive
that one of the claims (i) and (ii) in Theorem
1.26
holds.

Step 2
. The Lie group
G=\mathbb{Z}_{2}\times\mathbb{Z}_{2}=\{([0],[0]),([0],[1]),([1],[0]),([1],[1])\}

orthogonally acts on
\textsf{L}_{\tau}
and
\textsf{W}_{\tau}
by

([0],[0])\cdot u=u,\quad([0],[1])\cdot u=N\cdot u,\quad([1],[0])\cdot u=-u,\quad([1],[1])\cdot u=-(N\cdot u),

and the fixed point set of the induced
G
-action on
H_{\mu}^{0}
is
\{0\}
.
Clearly, each functional
\mathcal{L}_{\lambda}(\cdot)
is invariant for this action, using
Theorem
C.8
(
[
52
, Theorem 3.7]
or
[
50
, Theorem 5.12]
)
and
[
52
, Remark 3.9]
(
[
50
, Remark 5.14]
)
we may directly obtain either (iii) or (iv).
∎

Proof of Corollary
1.28
.

Suppose
\nu_{1,\tau}(\gamma_{\mu})>0
.
As in the proof of Corollary
1.9
,
by modifying values of
H_{0}
and
\hat{H}
outside a neighborhood
U
of
0\in\mathbb{R}^{2n}

we can choose
\kappa\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\frac{2\pi}{\tau}\mathbb{Z}
and

c_{i}>0
,
i=1,2,3
such that

H_{\kappa}(\lambda,z):=H_{0}(z)+\lambda\hat{H}(z)-\frac{\kappa}{2}|z|^{2}

satisfies

\displaystyle c_{1}I_{2n}\leq(H_{0})^{\prime\prime}(z)+\lambda\hat{H}^{\prime\prime}(z)-\kappa I_{2n}\leq c_{2}I_{2n}\quad\hbox{and}

\displaystyle c_{1}|z|^{2}-c_{3}\leq H_{\kappa}(\lambda,z)\leq c_{2}|z|^{2}+c_{3}

for all
(\lambda,t,z)\in[\mu-1,\mu+1]\times[0,\tau]\times{\mathbb{R}}^{2n}
.
Let
\psi_{\kappa,\lambda}(u):=\psi_{\kappa}(\lambda,u)
be given by (
5.3
). Then

{\psi}^{\prime\prime}_{\kappa,\lambda}(0)[\xi,\eta]=\textsf{Q}_{B_{\lambda},\kappa I_{2n}}(\xi,\eta)\quad\hbox{for all $\xi,\eta\in\textsf{{L}}_{\tau}$,}

where
\textsf{Q}_{B_{\lambda},\kappa I_{2n}}
is given by (
A.41
) with

B_{\lambda}(t)=(H_{0})^{\prime\prime}(0)+\lambda\hat{H}^{\prime\prime}(0)
. Therefore

m^{-}({\psi}_{\kappa,\lambda},0)=m^{-}(\textsf{Q}_{B_{\lambda},\kappa I_{2n}})\quad\hbox{and}\quad m^{0}({\psi}_{\kappa,\lambda},0)=m^{0}(\textsf{Q}_{B,\kappa I_{2n}}).

If
\hat{H}^{\prime\prime}(0)>0
(resp.
\hat{H}^{\prime\prime}(0)<0
), by Theorem
A.8
we deduce that

\displaystyle m^{-}(\textsf{Q}_{B_{\lambda_{2}},\kappa I_{2n}})\geq m^{-}(\textsf{Q}_{B_{\lambda_{1}},\kappa I_{2n}})+m^{0}(\textsf{Q}_{B_{\lambda_{1}},\kappa I_{2n}})\quad

(resp.
m^{-}(\textsf{Q}_{B_{\lambda_{1}},\kappa I_{2n}})\geq m^{-}(\textsf{Q}_{B_{\lambda_{2}},\kappa I_{2n}})+m^{0}(\textsf{Q}_{B_{\lambda_{2}},\kappa I_{2n}})
)

for any
\mu-1\leq\lambda_{1}<\lambda_{2}\leq\mu+1
. These imply that

\{\lambda\in[\mu-1,\mu+1]\,|\,m^{0}(\textsf{Q}_{B_{\lambda},\kappa I_{2n}})>0\}
is a finite set.
It follows from this and Theorem
A.7
that
\{\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}\,|\,\nu_{1,\tau}(\gamma_{\lambda})>0\}
is a discrete set in
{\mathbb{R}}
.
The first claim is proved.

As in the proof of Corollary
1.9
we can use
Theorem
A.8
to derive that

\displaystyle m^{-}(\textsf{Q}_{B_{\lambda},\kappa I_{2n}})=m^{-}(\textsf{Q}_{B_{\mu},\kappa I_{2n}})\;\forall\lambda\in[\mu-\rho,\mu),

\displaystyle m^{-}(\textsf{Q}_{B_{\lambda},\kappa I_{2n}})=m^{-}(\textsf{Q}_{B_{\mu},\kappa I_{2n}})+m^{0}(\textsf{Q}_{B_{\mu},\kappa I_{2n}})\;\forall\lambda\in(\mu,\mu+\rho]

if
\hat{H}^{\prime\prime}(0)>0
and
\rho>0
is small enough, and

\displaystyle m^{-}(\textsf{Q}_{B_{\lambda},\kappa I_{2n}})=m^{-}(\textsf{Q}_{B_{\mu},\kappa I_{2n}})+m^{0}(\textsf{Q}_{B_{\mu},\kappa I_{2n}})\;\;\hbox{for all\; $\lambda\in[\mu-\rho,\mu)$},

\displaystyle m^{-}(\textsf{Q}_{B_{\lambda},\kappa I_{2n}})=m^{-}(\textsf{Q}_{B_{\mu},\kappa I_{2n}})\;\;\hbox{for all\;$\lambda\in(\mu,\mu+\rho]$}

if
\hat{H}^{\prime\prime}(0)<0
and
\rho>0
is small enough.
These and Theorem
A.7
lead to (
1.28
) and (
1.28
).
Other conclusions follow from Theorem
1.26
.
∎

Proof of Corollary
1.29
.

The first claim follows from Corollary
1.28
.
Suppose
{H}^{\prime\prime}(0)>0
and
\mu\in\Delta(H)_{\tau}\cap(0,\infty)
.
Let
\gamma_{\lambda}(t)=\exp(\lambda tJ{H}^{\prime\prime}(0))
.
It follows from (
1.28
) that

\mu_{1,1}(\gamma_{\lambda})=\mu_{1,1}(\gamma_{\mu})
for
\lambda\leq\mu
close to
\mu
and that

\mu_{1,1}(\gamma_{\lambda})=\mu_{1,1}(\gamma_{\mu})+\nu_{1,1}(\gamma_{\mu})
for
\lambda>\mu
close to
\mu
.
By the third conclusion of Corollary
1.28
with
H_{0}=0
and
\hat{H}=H
we obtain the desired claims.
The final result may follow from the fourth conclusion of Corollary
1.28
with
H_{0}=0
and
\hat{H}=H
.
∎

6
Proofs of Theorems
1.33
,
1.34
and
1.37

6.1
Proofs of Theorems
1.33
,
1.34

As in (
3.1
) we define

\bar{H}(\lambda,t,z)={H}(\lambda,t,z+u_{\lambda}(t))-(z,\nabla_{z}{H}(\lambda,t,u_{\lambda}(t)))_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}

for
(\lambda,t,z)\in\Lambda\times[0,\tau]\times{{\mathbb{R}}}^{2n}
.
Then
\bar{H}
still satisfies Assumption
1.32
, and it holds that

\left.\begin{array}[]{ll}\nabla_{z}\bar{H}(\lambda,t,z)=\nabla_{z}H(\lambda,t,z+u_{\lambda}(t))-\nabla_{z}{H}(\lambda,t,u_{\lambda}(t)),\\
\nabla^{2}_{z}\bar{H}(\lambda,t,z)=\nabla^{2}_{z}H(\lambda,t,z+u_{\lambda}(t)).\end{array}\right\}

(6.1)

It is easy to see that
w:[0,\tau]\to\mathbb{R}^{2n}
satisfies (
1.54
) if and only if
v:=w-u_{\lambda}

satisfies the Hamiltonian boundary value problem

\left.\begin{array}[]{ll}\dot{u}(t)=J\nabla_{z}\bar{H}(\lambda,t,u(t))\;\forall t\in[0,\tau],\\
u(0)\in L,\quad u(\tau)\in L^{\prime}.\end{array}\right\}

(6.2)

In particular,
v\equiv 0\in\mathbb{R}^{2n}
satisfies (
6.2
) for each
\lambda\in\Lambda
.
For the vertical Lagrangian subspace
L_{0}:=\{0\}\times\mathbb{R}^{n}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2n}
,
we may choose an orthogonal symplectic matrix
O\in{\rm Sp}(2n)
such that
OL_{0}=L
.
Define
L^{\prime\prime}=O^{-1}L^{\prime}
and

K:\Lambda\times[0,\tau]\times{{\mathbb{R}}}^{2n}\to{\mathbb{R}},\;(\lambda,t,z)\mapsto\bar{H}(\lambda,t,Oz).

Then
K
also satisfies Assumption
1.32
, and

\nabla_{z}K(\lambda,t,z)=O^{-1}\nabla_{z}\bar{H}(\lambda,t,Oz),\quad\nabla^{2}_{z}K(\lambda,t,z)=O^{-1}\nabla^{2}_{z}\bar{H}(\lambda,t,Oz)O.

(6.3)

It follows that
v:[0,\tau]\to\mathbb{R}^{2n}
satisfies (
6.2
) if and only if
w(t):=O^{-1}v(t)

satisfies the Hamiltonian boundary value problem

\left.\begin{array}[]{ll}\dot{u}(t)=J\nabla_{z}K(\lambda,t,u(t))\;\forall t\in[0,1],\\
u(0)\in L_{0},\quad u(\tau)\in L^{\prime\prime}.\end{array}\right\}

(6.4)

In particular,
w\equiv 0\in\mathbb{R}^{2n}
satisfies (
6.4
) for each
\lambda\in\Lambda
.

Let
\alpha_{\lambda}:[0,\tau]\to{\rm Sp}(2n)
be the fundamental matrix solution of

\dot{u}(t)=J\nabla^{2}_{z}K(\lambda,t,0)u(t)
. Since
(
6.1
) and (
6.3
) imply
\nabla^{2}_{z}K(\lambda,t,0)=O^{-1}\nabla^{2}_{z}\bar{H}(\lambda,t,0)O=O^{-1}\nabla^{2}_{z}H(\lambda,t,u_{\lambda}(t))O
, we have
\alpha_{\lambda}(t)=O^{-1}\gamma_{\lambda}(t)O
and hence

i^{L^{\prime}}_{L}(\gamma_{\lambda})=i^{L^{\prime\prime}}_{L_{0}}(\alpha_{\lambda})\quad\hbox{and}\quad\nu^{L^{\prime}}_{L}(\gamma_{\lambda})=\nu^{L^{\prime\prime}}_{L_{0}}(\alpha_{\lambda})=\dim(\gamma(\tau)L\cap L^{\prime})

(6.5)

by
[
38
, Definition 2.4]
.
Note that
v:[0,\tau]\to\mathbb{R}^{2n}
satisfies (
6.4
) if and only if

u(t):=Ov(t)+u_{\lambda}(t)
satisfies (
1.54
).
Therefore
from now on we can assume
:

in Assumption
1.32
,
L=L_{0}
,
u_{\lambda}=0
,
w_{\lambda}=0=w_{\lambda}^{\prime}
for all
\lambda\in\Lambda
.

(6.6)

Under assumptions in (
6.6
), we only concern solutions of
(
1.54
) near
0\in\mathbb{R}^{2n}
. Because of Remark
3.1
,

\Lambda
may be assumed to be compact and sequential compact.
Therefore by modifying
H
outside a large ball in what follows we may assume that

\|\nabla^{2}_{z}H(\lambda,t,x)\|_{\mathbb{R}^{2n\times 2n}}\leq C(H)\quad\forall(\lambda,t,x)

(6.7)

and so

\|\nabla_{z}H(\lambda,t,x)\|_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}\leq C(H)\|x\|_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}+C(H)^{\prime}\quad\forall(\lambda,t,x),

(6.8)

where
C(H)
and
C(H)^{\prime}
are positive constants.
Because of these, using
[
51
, Proposition C.1]
we derive that the maps

L^{2}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})\to L^{2}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n}),\;u\mapsto\nabla_{z}H(\lambda,\cdot;u(\cdot))

(6.9)

have an uniform bound on any bounded subset and are uniform continuous at any
\bar{u}\in L^{2}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})

with respect to
\lambda\in\Lambda
.
Let
u_{\lambda_{i}}\in L^{2}([0,1],\mathbb{R}^{2n})
,
i=1,2
, satisfy
\|u_{\lambda_{2}}-u_{\lambda_{1}}\|_{2}\to 0
as
\lambda_{2}\to\lambda_{1}
.
Then

\displaystyle\left(\int^{\tau}_{0}|\nabla_{z}H(\lambda_{2},t,u_{\lambda_{2}}(t))-\nabla_{z}H(\lambda_{1},t,u_{\lambda_{1}}(t))|^{2}dt\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\left(\int^{\tau}_{0}|\nabla_{z}H(\lambda_{2},t,u_{\lambda_{2}}(t))-\nabla_{z}H(\lambda_{2},t,u_{\lambda_{1}}(t))|^{2}dt\right)^{1/2}

\displaystyle+\left(\int^{\tau}_{0}|\nabla_{z}H(\lambda_{2},t,u_{\lambda_{1}}(t))-\nabla_{z}H(\lambda_{1},t,u_{\lambda_{1}}(t))|^{2}dt\right)^{1/2}.

Clearly, as
\lambda_{2}\to\lambda_{1}
the first term of the right side converges to zero because of
the uniform continuity of the maps in (
6.9
) at any
u_{\lambda_{1}}\in L^{2}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})

with respect to
\lambda\in\Lambda
. From (
6.8
) and Lebesgue dominated convergence theorem
it follows that the second term of the right side converges to zero as
\lambda_{2}\to\lambda_{1}
.
Hence we obtain the first claim of the following.

Lemma 6.1
.

The map

\Lambda\times L^{2}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})\to L^{2}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n}),\;(\lambda,u)\mapsto\nabla_{z}H(\lambda,\cdot;u(\cdot))

(6.10)

is continuous. Therefore if
u_{\lambda_{i}}\in C^{1}([0,\tau],\mathbb{R}^{2n})
,
i=1,2
, satisfy
\dot{u}_{\lambda_{i}}=J\nabla_{z}H(\lambda_{i},t,u_{\lambda_{i}}(t))
,
and
\|u_{\lambda_{2}}-u_{\lambda_{1}}\|_{2}\to 0
as
\lambda_{2}\to\lambda_{1}
, then

u_{\lambda_{2}}\to u_{\lambda_{1}}
in
C^{1}([0,\tau],\mathbb{R}^{2n})
as
\lambda_{2}\to\lambda_{1}
.

Proof.

By the first claim,
\|\dot{u}_{\lambda_{2}}-\dot{u}_{\lambda_{1}}\|_{2}\to 0
as
\lambda_{2}\to\lambda_{1}
.
Then
\|u_{\lambda_{2}}-u_{\lambda_{1}}\|_{1,2}\to 0
and so
\|u_{\lambda_{2}}-u_{\lambda_{1}}\|_{C^{0}}\to 0
.
By Assumption
1.32
, (
6.1
) and (
6.3
) imply that

\Lambda\times[0,\tau]\times\mathbb{R}^{2n}\ni(\lambda,t,z)\to\nabla_{z}H(\lambda_{2},t,z)\in\mathbb{R}^{2n}

is continuous. It follows from
\dot{u}_{\lambda_{i}}=J\nabla_{z}H(\lambda_{i},t,u_{\lambda_{i}}(t))
that

\|\dot{u}_{\lambda_{2}}-\dot{u}_{\lambda_{1}}\|_{C^{0}}\to 0
as
\lambda_{2}\to\lambda_{1}
.
∎

Following
[
38
, §4]
the problem is reduced to one in finitely dimensional spaces.
For each
s\geq 0
consider a Hilbert space

\displaystyle H^{s}_{L_{0}}=\left\{x\in L^{2}([0,\tau],\mathbb{R}^{2n})\,\Bigg|\begin{array}[]{ll}x\stackrel{{\scriptstyle L^{2}}}{{=}}\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}-J\exp\left(\frac{m\pi tJ_{2n}}{\tau}\right)a_{m},\;a_{m}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\oplus\{0\},\\
\sum_{m\in\mathbb{Z}}(1+|m|^{2s})|a_{m}|^{2}<\infty\end{array}\right\}

with the following inner product

\langle x,y\rangle_{H^{s}_{L_{0}}}={\tau}\langle x_{0},y_{0}\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}+\sum_{k\neq 0}|k|^{2s}x_{k}y_{k},\quad x,y\in H^{s}_{L_{0}}.

(6.12)

Denote the associated norm by
\|\cdot\|_{H^{s}_{L_{0}}}
.
Then
H^{0}_{L_{0}}
and
H^{1}_{L_{0}}
are Hilbert subspaces of
L^{2}([0,\tau],\mathbb{R}^{2n})
and
W^{1,2}([0,\tau],\mathbb{R}^{2n})
, respectively.
Hence we also write
\langle x,y\rangle_{H^{s}_{L_{0}}}
as

\langle x,y\rangle_{2}
(resp.
\langle x,y\rangle_{1,2}
) for
s=0
(resp.
s=1
), and

\|\cdot\|_{H^{s}_{L_{0}}}
as

\|\cdot\|_{2}
(resp.
\|\cdot\|_{1,2}
) for
s=0
(resp.
s=1
) below.

Take an orthogonal symplectic matrix
P\in{\rm Sp}(2n)
such that
PL_{0}=L^{\prime}
.
We have a matrix
M
such that
P=e^{M}
.
Consider Hilbert subspaces of
L^{2}([0,\tau],\mathbb{R}^{2n})
and
W^{1,2}([0,\tau],\mathbb{R}^{2n})

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{0}=\left\{x\in L^{2}([0,\tau],\mathbb{R}^{2n})\,\Big|\,[0,\tau]\ni t\mapsto\exp\left(-\frac{t}{\tau}M\right)x(t)\;\hbox{belongs to}\;H^{0}_{L_{0}}\right\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{1}=\left\{x\in L^{2}([0,\tau],\mathbb{R}^{2n})\,\Big|\,[0,\tau]\ni t\mapsto\exp\left(-\frac{t}{\tau}M\right)\;\hbox{belongs to}\;H^{1}_{L_{0}}\right\}

and a unbounded self-adjoint operator
\mathbb{A}
in
\mathcal{W}_{0}
with domain
D(\mathbb{A})=\mathcal{W}_{1}
, which is given by

\langle\mathbb{A}x,y\rangle_{2}=\int^{\tau}_{0}(-J\dot{x}(t)+JMx(t),y(t))_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}dt.

The range of
\mathbb{A}
is closed, the resolution of
\mathbb{A}
is compact and the spectrum of

\mathbb{A}
only consists of eigenvalues, more precisely
\sigma(\mathbb{A})=\frac{\pi}{\tau}\mathbb{Z}
.
Note that
{\rm Ker}(\mathbb{A})=\{[0,\tau]\ni t\mapsto e^{\frac{t}{\tau}M}a\,|\,a\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\oplus\{0\}\}
.

For each
\lambda\in\Lambda
, define functionals
\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}:\mathcal{W}_{0}\to\mathbb{R}
and
f_{\lambda}:(\mathcal{W}_{1},\|\cdot\|_{2})\to\mathbb{R}
by

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}(x)=\int^{\tau}_{0}H(\lambda,t,x(t))dt+\frac{1}{2}\int^{\tau}_{0}(JMx(t),x(t))_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}dt,

(6.13)

\displaystyle f_{\lambda}(x)=\frac{1}{2}\bigl(\mathbb{A}x,x\bigr)_{2}-\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}(x).

(6.14)

(
6.7
) and (
6.8
) imply that
both functionals are of class
C^{1}
, and that the
L^{2}
-gradient
\nabla^{L^{2}}\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}
of
\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}
defined by

\displaystyle(\nabla^{L^{2}}\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}(x),y)_{2}=\int^{\tau}_{0}(\nabla_{z}H(\lambda,t,x(t)),y(t))_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}dt+\int^{\tau}_{0}(JMx(t),y(t))_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}dt\quad\forall x,y\in\mathcal{W}_{0}

is Gâteaux differentiable. It is easily checked that the Gâteaux derivative
D\nabla^{L^{2}}\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}(x)\in\mathcal{L}_{s}(\mathcal{W}_{0})
at
x\in\mathcal{W}_{0}
is given by

\displaystyle(D\nabla^{L^{2}}\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}(x)y,z)_{2}=\int^{\tau}_{0}(\nabla^{2}_{z}H(\lambda,t,x(t)y(t)),z(t))_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}dt+\int^{\tau}_{0}(JMy(t),z(t))_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}dt

(6.15)

for all
y,z\in\mathcal{W}_{0}
. From (
6.7
) and (
6.15
) we derive that
\|D\nabla^{L^{2}}\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}(x)\|_{\mathcal{L}_{s}(\mathcal{W}_{0})}\leq C(H)+\|M\|_{\mathbb{R}^{2n\times 2n}}
.
Moreover the
L^{2}
-gradient of
f_{\lambda}
is given by

\displaystyle\nabla^{L^{2}}f_{\lambda}(x)=\mathbb{A}x-\nabla_{z}H(\lambda,\cdot,x(\cdot))-JMx,\quad\forall x\in\mathcal{W}_{1},

(6.16)

and
x\in(\mathcal{W}_{1},\|\cdot\|_{2})
is a critical point of
f_{\lambda}
if and only if
it satisfies (
1.54
) with the assumptions in (
6.6
).

Let
\Pi_{0}:\mathcal{W}_{0}\to{\rm Ker}(\mathbb{A})

be the orthogonal projection. Define
\underline{\mathbb{A}}=\mathbb{A}+\Pi_{0}
, and denote by
E_{s}
the spectral resolution of

\underline{\mathbb{A}}
. Choose
\beta\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\sigma(\underline{\mathbb{A}})
such that
\beta>2(C(H)+\|M\|_{\mathbb{R}^{2n\times 2n}}+1)
, and define

\displaystyle P=\int_{-\beta}^{\beta}dE_{s},\qquad P^{+}=\int_{\beta}^{+\infty}dE_{s},\qquad P^{-}=\int_{-\infty}^{-\beta}dE_{s},

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{0}^{+}:=P^{+}(\mathcal{W}_{0}),\quad\mathcal{W}_{0}^{-}:=P^{-}(\mathcal{W}_{0}),\quad Z:=P(\mathcal{W}_{0}).

Then
\mathcal{W}_{0}=\mathcal{W}_{0}^{+}\oplus\mathcal{W}_{0}^{-}\oplus Z
, and
\dim Z<\infty
. Bounded self-adjoint linear operators on
\mathcal{W}_{0}
,

\displaystyle S^{+}=\int_{\beta}^{\infty}s^{-1/2}dE_{s},\quad S^{-}=\int^{-\beta}_{-\infty}(-s)^{-1/2}dE_{s},\quad R=\int_{\alpha}^{\beta}|s|^{-1/2}dE_{s},

have ranges
\mathcal{W}_{0}^{+}
,
\mathcal{W}_{0}^{-}
and
Z
, respectively, are pairwise commuting, and
S^{+}|_{\mathcal{W}_{0}^{+}}
,

S^{-}|_{\mathcal{W}_{0}^{-}}
and
R|_{Z}
are injective.

According to Amann
[
2
]
, Amann-Zehnder
[
3
]
, Chang
[
13
]
, Long
[
42
]

and Liu-Wang-Lin
[
38
, Theorems 4.1, 4.2]
we may use Lemma
6.1
to obtain:

Theorem 6.2
.

There exist
(x(\cdot,\cdot),y(\cdot,\cdot))\in C(\Lambda\times Z,\mathcal{W}_{0}^{+}\times\mathcal{W}_{0}^{-})
satisfying the following:

(i)

The continuous map
\mathfrak{u}(\cdot,\cdot):\Lambda\times Z\to\mathcal{W}_{0}
defined by

\mathfrak{u}(\lambda,z)=S^{+}x(\lambda,R^{-1}z)+S^{-}y(\lambda,R^{-1}z)+z

takes values in
\mathcal{W}_{1}
, each
\mathfrak{u}(\lambda,\cdot):Z\to\mathcal{W}_{0}
is a
C^{1}
embedding, and

d_{z}\mathfrak{u}(\lambda,z)
is continuous in
(\lambda,z)
.

(ii)

For each
\lambda\in\Lambda
, the functional
a_{\lambda}:Z\to\mathbb{R}
defined by

\displaystyle a_{\lambda}(z)=f_{\lambda}(\mathfrak{u}(\lambda,z))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\bigl(\mathbb{A}\mathfrak{u}(\lambda,z),\mathfrak{u}(\lambda,z)\bigr)_{2}-\mathcal{H}_{\lambda}(\mathfrak{u}(\lambda,z))

(6.17)

is
C^{2}
, and

\displaystyle a^{\prime}_{\lambda}(z)=\mathbb{A}z-P\mathcal{H}^{\prime}_{\lambda}(\mathfrak{u}(\lambda,z))=\mathbb{A}\mathfrak{u}(\lambda,z)-\mathcal{H}^{\prime}_{\lambda}(\mathfrak{u}(\lambda,z)),

(6.18)

\displaystyle a^{\prime\prime}_{\lambda}(z)=\mathbb{A}|_{{Z}}-Pd\mathcal{H}^{\prime}_{\lambda}(\mathfrak{u}(\lambda,z))d_{z}\mathfrak{u}(\lambda,z)=[\mathbb{A}-d\mathcal{H}^{\prime}_{\lambda}(\mathfrak{u}(\lambda,z))]d_{z}\mathfrak{u}(\lambda,z).

(6.19)

(It follows that

\displaystyle\Lambda\times{Z}\ni(\lambda,z)\mapsto a^{\prime}_{\lambda}(z)\in{Z}\quad\hbox{and}\quad\Lambda\times{Z}\ni(\lambda,z)\mapsto a^{\prime\prime}_{\lambda}(z)\in\mathscr{L}({Z})

(6.20)

are continuous. Note that (i) implies
\Lambda\times{Z}\ni(\lambda,z)\mapsto a_{\lambda}(z)\in\mathbb{R}
to be continuous.)

(iii)

For each
\lambda\in\Lambda
, the map
Z\ni z\mapsto\mathfrak{u}(\lambda,z)\in\mathcal{W}_{0}
gives an one-one correspondence
between the critical points of
a_{\lambda}
and those of
f_{\lambda}
.
(The condition (
6.6
) implies that
\mathfrak{u}(\lambda,0)=0
and
0\in Z
is
a critical point of
a_{\lambda}
for any
\lambda\in\Lambda
.)

(iv)

If
z\in Z
is a critical point of
a_{\lambda}
and

\beta_{\lambda,z}:[0,\tau]\to{\rm Sp}(2n)
is the fundamental matrix solution of

\dot{v}(t)=J\nabla^{2}_{z}H(\lambda,t,\mathfrak{u}(\lambda,z)(t))v(t)
, then

m^{0}(a_{\lambda},z)=\nu_{L_{0}}^{L^{\prime}}(\beta_{\lambda,z})\quad\hbox{and}\quad m^{-}(a_{\lambda},z)=i_{L_{0}}^{L^{\prime}}(\beta_{\lambda,z}).

In particular,
m^{0}(a_{\lambda},0)=\nu_{L_{0}}^{L^{\prime}}(\gamma_{\lambda})=\dim(\gamma_{\lambda}(\tau)L_{0}\cap L^{\prime})
and
m^{-}(a_{\lambda},0)=i_{L_{0}}^{L^{\prime}}(\gamma_{\lambda})
.

Proof of Theorem
1.33
.

(I)
.
Under the assumptions in (
6.6
) let
(\mu,0)
is a bifurcation point along sequences of the problem (
1.54
)
with respect to the trivial branch
\{(\lambda,0)\,|\,\lambda\in\Lambda\}
.
That is, there exists a sequence
(\lambda_{k})\subset\Lambda
converging to
\mu
and
solutions
v_{k}\neq 0
of (
1.54
) with
\lambda=\lambda_{k}

such that
v_{k}\to 0
in
C^{1}([0,\tau],\mathbb{R}^{2n})
by Proposition
1.3
.
Then
v_{k}
is a critical point of
f_{\lambda_{k}}
in

(\mathcal{W}_{1},\|\cdot\|_{2})
and
\|v_{k}\|_{2}\to 0
as
k\to\infty
.
Therefore for each
k\in\mathbb{N}
by Theorem
6.2
(iii) we have an unique
z_{k}\in Z
such that

v_{k}=\mathfrak{u}(\lambda_{k},z_{k})=S^{+}x(\lambda_{k},R^{-1}z_{k})+S^{-}y(\lambda_{k},R^{-1}z_{k})+z_{k}.

This implies that
\|z_{k}\|_{2}\leq\|v_{k}\|_{2}\to 0
. Clearly,
z_{k}\neq 0
by (i) and (iii) in Theorem
6.2
.
These show that
(\mu,0)\in\Lambda\times Z
is a bifurcation point along sequences of
a^{\prime}_{\lambda}(z)=0

with respect to the trivial branch
\{(\lambda,0)\,|\,\lambda\in\Lambda\}\subset\Lambda\times Z
.
Suppose
\nu^{L^{\prime}}_{L_{0}}(\gamma_{\mu})=\dim(\gamma_{\mu}(\tau)L_{0}\cap L^{\prime})>0
.
Theorem
6.2
(iv) gives rise to
m^{0}(a_{\mu},0)=0
, that is,
a_{\mu}^{\prime\prime}(0):Z\to Z
is invertible.
By Theorem
6.2
(iii) we may use the implicit function theorem for

\Lambda\times{Z}\ni(\lambda,z)\mapsto a^{\prime}_{\lambda}(z)\in{Z}
near
(\mu,0)

to yield
z_{k}=0
for
k
large enough. A contradiction is obtained,

(II)
. As above we can derive from the assumptions and Theorem
6.2
(iv) that for each
k\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle[m^{-}(a_{\lambda_{k}^{-}},0),m^{-}(a_{\lambda_{k}^{-}},0)+m^{0}(a_{\lambda_{k}^{-}},0)]\cap[m^{-}(a_{\lambda_{k}^{+}},0),m^{-}(a_{\lambda_{k}^{+}},0)+m^{0}(a_{\lambda_{k}^{+}},0)]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[i^{L^{\prime}}_{L}(\gamma_{\lambda_{k}^{-}}),i^{L^{\prime}}_{L}(\gamma_{\lambda_{k}^{-}})+\nu^{L^{\prime}}_{L}(\gamma_{\lambda_{k}^{-}})]\cap[i^{L^{\prime}}_{L}(\gamma_{\lambda_{k}^{+}})i^{L^{\prime}}_{L}(\gamma_{\lambda_{k}^{+}})+\nu^{L^{\prime}}_{L}(\gamma_{\lambda_{k}^{+}})]=\emptyset

and either
m^{0}(a_{\lambda_{k}^{+}},0)=\nu^{L^{\prime}}_{L}(\gamma_{\lambda_{k}^{+}})=0
or
m^{0}(a_{\lambda_{k}^{-}},0)=\nu^{L^{\prime}}_{L}(\gamma_{\lambda_{k}^{-}})=0
.
By Theorem
C.4
there exists a sequence
\{(\lambda_{k},z_{k})\}_{k\geq 1}

in
\hat{\Lambda}\times Z\setminus\{(\mu,0)\}
converging to
(\mu,0)
such that
z_{k}\neq 0
and
a^{\prime}_{\lambda_{k}}(z_{k})=0
for each
k\in\mathbb{N}
.
Let
v_{k}=\mathfrak{u}(\lambda_{k},z_{k})=S^{+}x(\lambda_{k},R^{-1}z_{k})+S^{-}y(\lambda_{k},R^{-1}z_{k})+z_{k}
for each
k\in\mathbb{N}
.
Then by Theorem
6.2
we obtain that
v_{k}\neq 0
,
v_{k}\to 0
in
(\mathcal{W}_{1},\|\cdot\|_{2})
and
f^{\prime}_{\lambda_{k}}(v_{k})=0
for each
k\in\mathbb{N}
.
The expected result is proved.

(III)
. As above we may obtain

[m^{-}(a_{\lambda^{-}},0),m^{-}(a_{\lambda^{-}},0)+m^{0}(a_{\lambda^{-}},0)]\cap[m^{-}(a_{\lambda^{+}},0),m^{-}(a_{\lambda^{+}},0)+m^{0}(a_{\lambda^{+}},0)]=\emptyset

and either
m^{0}(a_{\lambda^{+}},0)=\nu^{L^{\prime}}_{L}(\gamma_{\lambda^{+}})=0
or
m^{0}(a_{\lambda^{-}},0)=\nu^{L^{\prime}}_{L}(\gamma_{\lambda^{-}})=0
.
Then by Theorem
C.5
we obtain a
\mu\in\Lambda
such that
(\mu,0)
is a bifurcation point along sequences of
a_{\lambda}(z)=0

in
\Lambda\times Z
. Moreover, if
m^{0}(a_{\lambda^{+}},0)=0
(resp.
m^{0}(a_{\lambda^{-}},0)=0
), then
\mu\neq\lambda^{+}
(resp.
\mu\neq\lambda^{-}
).
Finally, the desired result follows from these as above.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.34
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
1.34
with additional conditions in (
6.6
),
following the notations in the proof of Theorem
1.33
we obtain that

m^{0}(a_{\mu},0)\neq 0

and
m^{0}(a_{\lambda},0)=0
for each
\lambda\in\Lambda\setminus\{\mu\}
near
\mu
, and that

m^{-}(a_{\lambda},0)
take, respectively, values
m^{-}(a_{\mu},0)
and

m^{-}(a_{\mu},0)+m^{0}(a_{\mu},0)

as
\lambda\in\Lambda
varies in two deleted half neighborhoods of
\mu
.
Therefore
[
52
, Theorem 3.6]
(or
[
52
, Theorem 4.10]
) is applicable to
\{a_{\lambda}\,|\,\lambda\in\Lambda\}
.
The corresponding conclusions are easily translated into those of Theorem
1.34
with additional conditions in (
6.6
).
∎

6.2
Proof of Theorem
1.37

Define
G:(0,\tau]\times[0,1]\times\mathbb{R}^{2n}\to\mathbb{R}
by

G(\lambda,t,z)=\lambda H\big(z+(1-t)u(0)+tu(\lambda)\big)+\big(z+(1-t)u(0)+tu(\lambda),J(u(\lambda)-u(0))\big)_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}.

Then
\nabla_{z}G(\lambda,t,z)=\lambda\nabla H\bigr(z+(1-t)u(0)+tu(\lambda)\bigl)+J(u(\lambda)-u(0))
and

\nabla_{z}^{2}G(\lambda,t,z)=\lambda\nabla^{2}H\bigr(z+(1-t)u(0)+tu(\lambda)\bigl)
.
For
\lambda\in(0,\tau]
it is easily proved that
z:[0,\lambda]\to\mathbb{R}^{2n}
satisfies

\left.\begin{array}[]{ll}\dot{v}(t)=J\nabla_{z}H(v(t))\;\forall t\in[0,\lambda],\\
v(0)\in u(0)+L,\quad v(\lambda)\in u(\lambda)+L\end{array}\right\}

(6.21)

if and only if
w:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}^{2n}
defined by
w(t):=z(\lambda t)-(1-t)u(0)-tu(\lambda)
satisfies

\left.\begin{array}[]{ll}\dot{w}(t)=J\nabla_{z}G(\lambda,t,w(t))\;\forall t\in[0,1],\\
w(0)\in L,\quad w(1)\in L.\end{array}\right\}

(6.22)

In particular, since
u|_{[0,\lambda]}
satisfies (
6.21
), the path

w_{\lambda}:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}^{2n},\;t\mapsto u(\lambda t)-(1-t)u(0)-tu(\lambda)

(6.23)

satisfies (
6.22
). Let
B(t)=\nabla^{2}H(u(t))
for
t\in[0,\tau]
, and let

\gamma:[0,\tau]\to{\rm Sp}(2n)
be the fundamental matrix solution of

\dot{v}(t)=JB(t)v(t)
on
[0,\tau]
. Then it is easily checked that

B_{\lambda}(t):=\nabla^{2}_{z}G(\lambda,t,w_{\lambda}(t))=\lambda\nabla^{2}H(u(\lambda t))=\lambda B(\lambda t)\quad\forall t\in[0,1],

(6.24)

and
[0,1]\ni t\mapsto\gamma_{\lambda}(t):=\gamma(\lambda t)

is the fundamental matrix solution of
\dot{v}(t)=JB_{\lambda}(t)v(t)
on
[0,1]
.

We shall obtain conclusions of Theorem
1.37
by applying Theorem
1.33
(I) and Theorem
1.34
to (
6.22
).
Note that
(i^{L}_{L},\nu^{L}_{L})=(i_{L},\nu_{L})
.

Step 1
(
Prove
(A)). Suppose that
\mu\in(0,\tau]
is a bifurcation instant for
(H,u,L)
.
By the above definition there exists a sequence
(\tau_{k})\subset(0,\tau]
converging to
\mu

such that for each
k\in\mathbb{N}
the boundary value problem
(
1.59
) has a solution
v_{k}\neq u|_{[0,\tau_{k}]}
such that
\|v_{k}-u\|_{C^{1}([0,\tau_{k}],\mathbb{R}^{2n})}\to 0
as
k\to\infty
. Then

w^{k}:[0,1]\to\mathbb{R}^{2n},\;t\mapsto v_{k}(\tau_{k}t)-(1-t)u(0)-tu(\tau_{k})

is not equal to
w_{\tau_{k}}
, and satisfies (
6.22
) and

\|w^{k}-w_{\tau_{k}}\|_{C^{1}([0,1],\mathbb{R}^{2n})}\leq(1+\tau)\|v_{k}-u\|_{C^{1}([0,\tau_{k}],\mathbb{R}^{2n})}+\to 0

as
k\to\infty
. Since both
u
and
\dot{u}
are uniformly continuous on
[0,\tau]
, it is not hard to prove that

\|w_{\tau_{k}}-w_{\mu}\|_{C^{1}([0,1],\mathbb{R}^{2n})}\to 0
as
k\to\infty
. Hence
\|w^{k}-w_{\mu}\|_{C^{1}([0,1],\mathbb{R}^{2n})}\to 0
.
These show that
(\mu,w_{\mu})
is a bifurcation point of (
6.22
).
By Theorem
1.33
(I) we deduce

\nu_{L}(\gamma|_{[0,\mu]})=\dim(\gamma(\mu)L)\cap L=\nu_{L}(\gamma_{\mu})>0

and therefore Theorem
1.37
(A).

Step 2
(
Prove
(B)).
If all
B_{22}(t)
(resp. all
B_{11}(t)
) are positive definite, it was claimed in
[
37
, Lemma 5.1]
that

\displaystyle i_{L_{0}}(\gamma)=\sum_{0<\lambda<\tau}\nu_{L_{0}}(\gamma_{\lambda})=\sum_{0<\lambda<\tau}\dim((\gamma(\lambda)L_{0})\cap L_{0})

(6.25)

\displaystyle\hbox{(resp.}\quad i_{L_{1}}(\gamma)=\sum_{0<\lambda<\tau}\nu_{L_{1}}(\gamma_{\lambda})=\sum_{0<\lambda<\tau}\nu_{L_{0}}\dim((\gamma(\lambda)L_{1})\cap L_{1})\quad\hbox{)}.

(6.26)

Clearly, the positive definiteness of
B(t)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}B_{11}(t)&B_{12}(t)\\
B_{21}(t)&B_{22}(t)\\
\end{array}\right)

implies that both
B_{22}(t)
and
B_{11}(t)
are positive definite.
Choose an orthogonal symplectic matrix
O\in{\rm Sp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
such that
OL_{0}=L
.
Then
\Upsilon(t):=O^{-1}\gamma(t)O
is the fundamental matrix solution of

\dot{v}(t)=JO^{T}B(t)Ov(t)
on
[0,\tau]
. Note that all
O^{T}B(t)O
are also positive definite. By (
1.56
) and
(
6.25
) we obtain

i_{L}(\gamma)=i_{L_{0}}(\Upsilon)=\sum_{0<\lambda<\tau}\dim((\Upsilon(\lambda)L_{0})\cap L_{0})=\sum_{0<\lambda<\tau}\dim((\gamma(\lambda)L)\cap L)

(6.27)

because
\dim((\Upsilon(\lambda)L_{0})\cap L_{0})=\dim((O^{-1}\gamma(\lambda)OL_{0})\cap L_{0})=\dim(\gamma(\lambda)L)\cap L)
.
This implies the first claim in Theorem
1.37
(B).

Let
\mu\in(0,\tau)
be such that
\dim(\gamma(\mu)L)\cap L>0
.
Then there exists a small
\varepsilon>0
such that

\dim(\gamma(\lambda)L)\cap L=0
for all
\lambda\in[\mu-\varepsilon,\mu+\varepsilon]\setminus\{\mu\}
.
Note that (
6.27
) implies

i_{L}(\gamma_{\lambda})=\sum_{0<s<1}\dim((\gamma_{\lambda}(s)L)\cap L)=\sum_{0<\theta<\lambda}\dim((\gamma(\theta)L)\cap L)

and so

\displaystyle i_{L}(\gamma_{\lambda})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}i_{L}(\gamma_{\mu})&\forall\lambda\in(\mu-\varepsilon,\mu),\\
i_{L}(\gamma_{\mu})+\nu_{L}(\gamma_{\mu})&\forall\lambda\in(\mu,\mu+\varepsilon).\end{array}\right.

We can apply Theorem
1.34
to (
6.22
).
Then the corresponding conclusions (i), (ii) and (iii) of Theorem
1.34
about
(
6.22
) are equivalent to (B.1), (B.ii) and (B.iii), respectively.

With (
6.25
) and (
6.26
),
the final conclusions can be derived as above.
\Box

Appendix A
Maslov-type index and Morse index

Let
{\rm Sp}(2n,\mathbb{R})^{0}=\{M\in{\rm Sp}(2n)\,|\,D(M)=0\}
, where

D(M)=(-1)^{n-1}\operatorname{det}\left(M-I_{2n}\right)
.
The complementary set
{\rm Sp}(2n,\mathbb{R})^{*}:={\rm Sp}(2n,\mathbb{R})\backslash{\rm Sp}(2n,\mathbb{R})^{0}

has exactly two path-connected components
{\rm Sp}(2n,\mathbb{R})^{\pm}=\{M\in{\rm Sp}(2n,\mathbb{R})\,|\,\pm D(M)<0\}
,
which contain, respectively,

M_{n}^{+}={\rm blockdiag}(2I_{n},\frac{1}{2}I_{n})\quad\hbox{and}\quad M_{n}^{-}={\rm blockdiag}(-2,2I_{n-1},-\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2}I_{n-1}).

Every loop in these two components is contractible in
{\rm Sp}(2n)

(
[
17
, Lemma 1.7]
and
[
66
, Lemma 3.2]
).

Let
\mathcal{P}_{\tau}(2n)=\{\gamma\in C([0,\tau],{\rm Sp}(2n,{\mathbb{R}}))\,|\,\gamma(0)=I_{2n}\}

and let
{\cal P}^{\ast}_{\tau}(2n)
consist of
\gamma\in\mathcal{P}_{\tau}(2n)
such that

\gamma(\tau)\in{\rm Sp}(2n,\mathbb{R})^{\ast}
.
If
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}\in\mathcal{P}_{\tau}(2n)
satisfy
\gamma_{1}(\tau)=\gamma_{2}(0)
, define

\gamma_{2}\ast\gamma_{1}:[0,\tau]\to{\rm Sp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
by

\gamma_{2}\ast\gamma_{1}(t)=\gamma_{1}(2t)
for
t\in[0,\tau/2]
, and
\gamma_{2}\ast\gamma_{1}(t)=\gamma_{2}(2t-\tau)
for

t\in[\tau/2,\tau]
.

Recall that each
S\in{\rm Sp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
has a unique decomposition
PU
, where
P=\sqrt{SS^{T}}
and

U=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}U_{1}&-U_{2}\\
U_{2}&U_{1}\\
\end{array}\right)

such that
\mathfrak{u}(S):=U_{1}+\sqrt{-1}U_{2}
is a unitary matrix, i.e.,
\mathfrak{u}(S)\in U(n,{\mathbb{C}})
.
Therefore each path
\gamma\in C([a,b],{\rm Sp}(2n,\mathbb{R}))
corresponds to a unique continuous path

\mathfrak{u}_{\gamma}:[a,b]\to U(n,{\mathbb{C}})
given by
\mathfrak{u}_{\gamma}(t):=\mathfrak{u}(\gamma(t))
.
By the lifting criterion in the cover space theory
there exists a continuous real function
\triangle_{\gamma}
on
[a,b]

such that
\det\mathfrak{u}_{\gamma}(t)=\exp(\sqrt{-1}\triangle_{\gamma}(t))
for
t\in[a,b]
, and

\triangle(\gamma):=\triangle_{\gamma}(b)-\triangle_{\gamma}(a)
is uniquely determined by
\gamma
.

Conley and Zehnder
[
17
]
(for
n\geq 2
)
and Long and Zehnder
[
44
]
(for
n=1
) assign an integer

i_{\rm CZ}(\gamma)=\triangle({\beta\ast\gamma})/\pi

to each
\gamma\in\mathcal{P}^{\ast}_{\tau}(2n)
,
the so-called
Conley-Zehnder index
of
\gamma
,
where
\beta
is a path in
{\rm Sp}(2n)^{\ast}
connecting
\gamma(\tau)
to
M^{+}_{n}
or
M^{-}_{n}
.
An alternative exposition was presented in
[
66
]
.

There exist two ways to extend the Conley-Zehnder index to paths in
\mathcal{P}_{\tau}(2n)\setminus\mathcal{P}^{\ast}_{\tau}(2n)
.
Long
[
42
,
43
]
defined the
Maslov-type index
of
\gamma\in\mathcal{P}_{\tau}(2n)
to be a pair of integers
(i_{\tau}(\gamma),\nu_{\tau}(\gamma))
,

\displaystyle{i}_{\tau}(\gamma)=\inf\{i_{\rm CZ}(\beta)\,|\,\beta\in{\cal P}^{\ast}_{\tau}(2n)\;\hbox{is sufficiently $C^{0}$ close to $\gamma$
in}\;{\cal P}_{\tau}(2n)\}

and
\nu_{\tau}(\gamma):=\dim{\rm Ker}(\gamma(\tau)-I_{2n})
.
It was proved in
[
42
]
that
{i}_{\tau}
has the following
homotopy invariance
: Two paths
\gamma_{0}
and
\gamma_{1}
in
\mathcal{P}_{\tau}(2n)

have the same Maslov-type indexes
i_{\tau}(\gamma_{0})=i_{\tau}(\gamma_{1})

if there is a map
\delta\in C([0,1]\times[0,\tau],{\rm Sp}(2n,{\mathbb{R}}))
such that
\delta(0,\cdot)=\gamma_{0}
,
\delta(1,\cdot)=\gamma_{1}
,

\delta(s,0)=I_{2n}
and
\nu_{\tau}(\delta(s,\cdot))
is constant for
0\leq s\leq 1
.

For
a<b
and any path
\gamma\in C([a,b],{\rm Sp}(2n,{\mathbb{R}}))
, choose

\beta\in{\cal P}_{1}(2n)
with
\beta(1)=\gamma(a)
, and define

\phi\in{\cal P}_{1}(2n)
by
\phi(t)=\beta(2t)
for
0\leq t\leq 1/2
,
and
\phi(t)=\gamma(a+(2t-1)(b-a))
for
1/2\leq t\leq 1
.
Long showed in
[
42
]
that the difference
i_{1}(\phi)-i_{1}(\beta)
only
depends on
\gamma
, and called

i(\gamma,[a,b]):=i_{1}(\phi)-i_{1}(\beta)

(A.1)

the
Maslov-type index of
\gamma
. Clearly,
i(\gamma,[0,1])=i_{1}(\gamma)
for any
\gamma\in{\cal P}_{1}(2n)
.

In order to introduce another extension way of
i_{\rm CZ}
by Robbin-Salamon
[
64
]
,
Let
(F,\Omega)
be the symplectic space

(\mathbb{R}^{2n}\oplus\mathbb{R}^{2n},(-\omega_{0})\oplus\omega_{0})

and let
\mathscr{L}(F,\Omega)
denote the manifold of Lagrangian subspaces of
(F,\Omega)
.
For
M\in{\rm Sp}(2n,{\mathbb{R}})
, both

W:=\{(x^{T},x^{T})^{T}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{4n}\,|\,x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2n}\}
and
{\rm Gr}(M):=\{(x^{T},(Mx)^{T})^{T}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{4n}\,|\,x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2n}\}

belong to
\mathscr{L}(F,\Omega)
.
The Robbin-Salamon index
\mu^{\rm RS}
defined in
[
64
]
assigns a half integer

\mu^{\rm RS}(\Lambda,\Lambda^{\prime})
to every
pair of Lagrangian paths
\Lambda,\Lambda^{\prime}:[a,b]\to\mathscr{L}(F,\Omega)
.
The
Conley-Zehnder index
of
\gamma\in C([a,b],{\rm Sp}(2n,{\mathbb{R}}))
was defined in
[
65
, Remark 5.35]
by

\displaystyle\mu_{\rm CZ}(\gamma)=\mu^{\rm RS}({\rm Gr}\left(\gamma),W\right).

(A.2)

By
[
64
, Remark 5.4]
it holds that

\displaystyle\mu_{\rm CZ}(\gamma)=i_{\rm CZ}(\gamma),\quad\forall\gamma\in\mathcal{P}^{\ast}_{\tau}(2n).

(A.3)

There exists a precise relation between
i(\gamma,[a,b])
and
\mu_{\rm CZ}(\gamma)

for each
\gamma\in C([a,b],{\rm Sp}(2n,{\mathbb{R}}))
.
Recall that the Cappell-Lee-Miller index
\mu^{\rm CLM}_{F}
characterized by properties
I-VI of
[
11
, pp. 127-128]
assigns an integer
\mu^{\rm CLM}_{F}(\Lambda,\Lambda^{\prime})
to every
pair of Lagrangian paths
\Lambda,\Lambda^{\prime}:[a,b]\to\mathscr{L}(F,\Omega)
.
It was proved in
[
45
, Corollary 2.1]
that

\displaystyle i_{\tau}(\gamma)=\mu^{\rm CLM}_{F}(W,{\rm Gr}(\gamma),[0,\tau])-n,\quad\forall\gamma\in\mathcal{P}_{\tau}(2n).

(A.4)

Then for any
\gamma\in C([a,b],{\rm Sp}(2n,{\mathbb{R}}))
,
(
A.1
), (
A.4
) and the path additivity of
\mu^{\rm CLM}_{F}
lead to

\displaystyle i(\gamma,[a,b])=\mu^{\rm CLM}_{F}(W,{\rm Gr}\left(\gamma),[a,b]\right)

(A.5)

and therefore

\displaystyle i(\gamma,[a,b])=\mu_{\rm CZ}(\gamma)-\frac{1}{2}(\dim{\rm Ker}(I_{2n}-\gamma(b))-\dim{\rm Ker}(I_{2n}-\gamma(a)))

(A.6)

by (
A.2
) and
[
45
, Theorem 3.1]
. In particular,

\displaystyle i_{\tau}(\gamma)=\mu_{\rm CZ}(\gamma)-\frac{1}{2}\dim{\rm Ker}(I_{2n}-\gamma(\tau)),\quad\forall\gamma\in\mathcal{P}_{\tau}(2n).

(A.7)

For
\phi
and
\beta
in (
A.1
), since
\mu_{\rm CZ}

is additive with respect to the catenation of paths
we have also

\displaystyle\mu_{\rm CZ}(\gamma)=\mu_{\rm CZ}(\phi)-\mu_{\rm CZ}(\beta).

(A.8)

Both indexes
i
and
\mu_{\rm CZ}
have also
vanishing, product and the following:

(Homotopy)

If a continuous path
\ell:[0,1]\to C([a,b],{\rm Sp}(2n,{\mathbb{R}}))
is such that

\displaystyle s\mapsto\dim{\rm Ker}(I_{2n}-\ell(s)(a))\quad\hbox{and}\quad s\mapsto\dim{\rm Ker}(I_{2n}-\ell(s)(b))

(A.9)

are constant functions on
[0,1]
, then

\displaystyle i(\ell(s),[a,b])=i(\ell(0),[a,b])\quad\forall s\in[0,1],

(A.10)

\displaystyle\mu_{\rm CZ}(\ell(s))=\mu_{\rm CZ}(\ell(0))\quad\forall s\in[0,1].

(A.11)

(Naturality)

For any
\phi,\gamma\in C([a,b],{\rm Sp}(2n,{\mathbb{R}}))
it holds that

\displaystyle i(\phi\gamma\phi^{-1},[a,b])=i(\gamma,[a,b])\quad\hbox{and}\quad\mu_{\rm CZ}(\phi\gamma\phi^{-1})=\mu_{\rm CZ}(\gamma).

(A.12)

In fact, we have continuous maps
[0,1]\times[0,1]\to{\rm Sp}(2n,{\mathbb{R}}),(s,t)\mapsto\beta_{s}(t)
given by

\beta_{s}(t)=\beta_{0}\left(\frac{t}{1-s}\right)\;\hbox{for}\;0\leq t\leq 1-s,\qquad\beta_{s}(t)=\ell\left(t-1+s\right)(a)\;\hbox{for}\;1-s\leq t\leq 1,

and
[0,1]\times[0,1]\to{\rm Sp}(2n,{\mathbb{R}}),(s,t)\mapsto\phi_{s}(t)
defined by

\phi_{s}(t)=\beta_{s}\left(2t\right)\;\hbox{for}\;0\leq t\leq 1/2,\qquad\phi_{s}(t)=\ell(s)\left(a+(2t-1)(b-a)\right)\;\hbox{for}\;1/2\leq t\leq 1.

Then
\beta_{s}(0)=\phi_{s}(0)=I_{2n}
for all
s\in[0,1]
, and
\nu_{1}(\beta_{s})=\dim{\rm Ker}(I_{2n}-\ell(s)(a))
and

\nu_{1}(\phi_{s})=\dim{\rm Ker}(I_{2n}-\ell(s)(b))
are constant functions on
[0,1]
.
By (
A.1
),
i(\beta_{s},[0,1])=i_{1}(\beta_{s})
for all
s\in[0,1]
.
The homotopy invariance of
{i}_{1}
leads to (
A.10
), and therefore (
A.11
) by (
A.6
) and (
A.9
).

In order to prove (
A.12
), we consider a continuous path
\ell:[0,1]\to C([a,b],{\rm Sp}(2n,{\mathbb{R}}))
given by
\ell(s)(t)=\phi(st)\gamma(t)(\phi(st))^{-1}
.
Then (
A.11
) implies
\mu_{\rm CZ}(\phi\gamma\phi^{-1})=\mu_{\rm CZ}(\phi(0)\gamma(\phi(0))^{-1})
. Note that

\displaystyle{\rm Gr}(\phi(0)\gamma(\phi(0))^{-1})(t)=\{(x^{T},(\phi(0)\gamma(t)(\phi(0))^{-1}x)^{T})^{T}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{4n}\,|\,x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2n}\}=\Xi{\rm Gr}(\gamma)(t),

where
\Xi:(F,\Omega)\to(F,\Omega)
is the symplectic isomorphism given by

\Xi(x\oplus y)=(\phi(0)x)\oplus(\phi(0)y)
. Since
\Xi W=W
,
by (
A.2
) and the naturality property of
\mu^{\rm RS}
(
[
65
, §5]
) we obtain

\displaystyle\mu_{\rm CZ}(\phi(0)\gamma(\phi(0))^{-1})=\mu^{\rm RS}(\Xi{\rm Gr}(\gamma),W)=\mu^{\rm RS}({\rm Gr}(\gamma),W)=\mu_{\rm CZ}(\gamma).

The first equality in (
A.12
) may follow from this and (
A.6
).

Let
U_{1}=\{0\}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
and
U_{2}={\mathbb{R}}^{n}\times\{0\}
.
For
\gamma\in{\cal P}_{\tau}(2n)
with

\gamma(\tau/2)={\scriptscriptstyle\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}A&B\\
C&D\end{array}\right)}
, where
A,B,C,D\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n\times n}
,
Long, Zhang and Zhu
[
46
]
defined

\displaystyle\mu_{k,\tau}(\gamma)=\mu^{\rm CLM}_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2n}}\left(U_{k},\gamma U_{k},[0,\tau/2]\right),\quad k=1,2,

(A.13)

\displaystyle\nu_{1,\tau}(\gamma)=\dim{\rm Ker}(B)\quad{\rm and}\quad\nu_{2,\tau}(\gamma)=\dim{\rm Ker}(C).

(A.14)

Dong
[
19
]
and Liu
[
36
]
extended
the Maslov-type index
(i_{\tau}(\gamma),\nu_{\tau}(\gamma))
of
\gamma\in\mathcal{P}_{\tau}(2n)

to the case relative to a given symplectic matrix
M\in{\rm Sp}(2n,\mathbb{R}))

via different methods, denoted by

(i_{\tau,M}(\gamma),\nu_{\tau,M}(\gamma))\quad\hbox{and}\quad(i_{\tau}^{M}(\gamma),\nu_{\tau}^{M}(\gamma))

respectively. (The former was written as
(i_{M}(\gamma),\nu_{M}(\gamma))
in
[
19
]
).
They are more suitable and convenient for dual variational methods and saddle point reduction ones, respectively.
If
M=I_{2n}
, by
[
19
]
and
[
36
, Definition 2.5 and Definition 2.6]
there holds

(i_{\tau,M}(\gamma),\nu_{\tau,M}(\gamma))=(i_{\tau}^{M}(\gamma),\nu_{\tau}^{M}(\gamma))=(i_{\tau}(\gamma),\nu_{\tau}(\gamma)).

(A.15)

Let
[a]
denote the largest integer no more than
a\in{\mathbb{R}}
, and let

\xi
be any element in
\mathcal{P}_{\tau}(2n)
satisfying
\xi(\tau)=M^{-1}
,
Dong defined

\left.\begin{array}[]{ll}&\nu_{\tau,M}(\gamma)=\dim{\rm Ker}(\gamma(\tau)-M)\quad\hbox{and}\\
&i_{\tau,M}(\gamma)=[i_{\tau}((\gamma M^{-1})\ast\xi)-\triangle({\xi})/\pi]\end{array}\right\}

(A.16)

(
[
19
, Definitions 2.1, 2.2]
), and Liu (
[
40
, Definition 2.7 and Remark 2.8]
) defined

\left.\begin{array}[]{ll}&\nu_{\tau}^{M}(\gamma)=\dim{\rm Ker}(\gamma(\tau)-M)\quad\hbox{and}\\
&i^{M}_{\tau}(\gamma)=i_{\tau}((M^{-1}\gamma)\ast\xi)-i_{\tau}({\xi})\end{array}\right\}

(A.17)

if
M\neq I_{2n}
, and
(i_{\tau}^{M}(\gamma),\nu_{\tau}^{M}(\gamma))=(i_{\tau}(\gamma),\nu_{\tau}(\gamma))

if
M=I_{2n}
.
(Note that
i_{\tau}((M^{-1}\gamma)\ast\xi)
may be replaced by
i_{\tau}((\gamma M^{-1})\ast\xi))
,
see the proof of (
A.19
) below.
It was shown in
[
40
, Remark 2.8]
that when
M=I_{2n}
the right side of the second equality in (
A.17
) is
i_{\tau}(\gamma)+n
.)
A direct observation leads to a precise relation between
i_{M,\tau}
and
i^{M}_{\tau}
as follows.

Proposition A.1
.

For any
(M,\gamma)\in({\rm Sp}(2n,{\mathbb{R}})\setminus\{I_{2n}\})\times\mathcal{P}_{\tau}(2n)

and any
\xi\in\mathcal{P}_{\tau}(2n)
satisfying
\xi(\tau)=M^{-1}

it holds that

i_{\tau,M}(\gamma)=[i^{M}_{\tau}(\gamma)+i_{\tau}(\xi)-\triangle({\xi})/\pi]=i^{M}_{\tau}(\gamma)+[i_{\tau}(\xi)-\triangle({\xi})/\pi].

(A.18)

(
[i_{\tau}(\xi)-\triangle({\xi})/\pi]

only depends on
M
.
)

Proof.

Clearly,
M^{-1}\xi M\in\mathcal{P}_{\tau}(2n)
and
(M^{-1}\xi M)(\tau)=M^{-1}

for any
\xi\in\mathcal{P}_{\tau}(2n)
satisfying
\xi(\tau)=M^{-1}
.
By (
A.17
) and
[
42
, Corollary 6.5]

\displaystyle i^{M}_{\tau}(\gamma)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle i_{\tau}\left((M^{-1}\gamma)\ast(M^{-1}\xi M)\right)-i_{\tau}(M^{-1}{\xi}M)

(A.19)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle i_{\tau}\left(M^{-1}((\gamma M^{-1})\ast\xi))M\right)-i_{\tau}({\xi})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle i_{\tau}\left((\gamma M^{-1})\ast\xi)\right)-i_{\tau}({\xi}).

Substituting this in the second equality in (
A.16
) we obtain (
A.18
).
∎

i_{\tau,M}
and
i^{M}_{\tau}
may be different. For the symplectic path

\xi_{2n,\tau}:[0,\tau]\to{\rm Sp}(2n,\mathbb{R}),\;t\mapsto I_{2n}.

(A.20)

A direct computation yields

i_{\tau}^{-J_{n}}(\xi_{2n,\tau})=0
and
i_{\tau,-J_{n}}(\xi_{2n,\tau})=[n/2]
.

For any two
m\times m
real symmetric matrices
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
, we write
A_{1}\leq A_{2}

(resp.
A_{1}<A_{2}
) if
A_{2}-A_{1}
is positive semi-definite (resp. positive definite). For any

B_{1},B_{2}\in L^{\infty}([0,\tau];\mathscr{L}_{s}(\mathbb{R}^{2n}))

we define
B_{1}\leq B_{2}
(resp.
B_{1}<B_{2}
) if
B_{1}(t)\leq B_{2}(t)
for a.e.
t\in[0,\tau]

(resp. if
B_{1}\leq B_{2}
and
B_{1}(t)<B_{2}(t)
on a subset of
[0,\tau]
with positive measure).

For
B,B_{1},B_{2}\in L^{\infty}([0,\tau];\mathscr{L}_{s}(\mathbb{R}^{2n}))
with
B_{1}<B_{2}
,
it was defined in
[
19
, Definitions 3.1, 3.2]
that

\left.\begin{array}[]{ll}\nu_{\tau,M}(B):=\nu_{M}(\Upsilon_{B})\quad\hbox{and}\\
I_{\tau,M}(B_{1},B_{2}):=\sum_{s\in[0,1)}\nu_{\tau,M}((1-s)B_{1}+sB_{2}).\end{array}\right\}

(A.21)

where
\Upsilon_{B}:[0,\tau]\to{\rm Sp}(2n,\mathbb{R})
is the fundamental matrix solution of the linear system

\dot{Z}(t)=JB(t)Z(t)
with
\Upsilon_{B}(0)=I_{2n}
.

For any
B_{1},B_{2}\in L^{\infty}([0,\tau];\mathscr{L}_{s}(\mathbb{R}^{2n}))
, and
s\in{\mathbb{R}}
such that
sI_{2n}>B_{1}
and
sI_{2n}>B_{2}
,

I_{\tau,M}(B_{1},B_{2}):=I_{\tau,M}(B_{1},sI_{2n})-I_{\tau,M}(B_{2},sI_{2n})

(A.22)

is was called the
relative Morse index
between
B_{1}
and
B_{2}
(
[
19
, Definition 3.5]
).
The
M
-index
of
B\in L^{\infty}([0,\tau];\mathscr{L}_{s}(\mathbb{R}^{2n}))

was defined in
[
19
, Definition 3.8]
by

j_{\tau,M}(B):=i_{\tau,M}(\xi_{2n})+I_{\tau,M}(0,B),

(A.23)

where
\xi_{2n}
is as in (
A.20
).
Then
j_{\tau,M}(0)=i_{\tau,M}(\xi_{2n})
(
[
19
, Remark 3.9]
).

Theorem A.2

(
[
19
, Theorem 4.1]
)
.

j_{\tau,M}(B)=i_{\tau,M}(\Upsilon_{B})
for any
B\in L^{\infty}([0,\tau];\mathscr{L}_{s}(\mathbb{R}^{2n}))
.

For
B\in L^{\infty}([0,\tau];\mathscr{L}_{s}(\mathbb{R}^{2n}))
, let
A\in L^{\infty}([0,\tau];\mathscr{L}_{s}(\mathbb{R}^{2n}))

satisfy
B-A\geq\varepsilon I_{2n}
for
\varepsilon>0
small enough, and let
C(t)=(B(t)-A(t))^{-1}
. Consider the quadratic form

q_{M,B|A}(u,v)=\frac{1}{2}\int^{\tau}_{0}\Bigl(((\hat{\Lambda}_{M,\tau,A})^{-1}u)(t),v(t))_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}+(C(t)u(t),v(t))_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}\Bigr)\,dt

(A.24)

on the Hilbert space
\tilde{L}^{2}_{M,A}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})=R(\Lambda_{M,\tau,A})
(cf. § 2.1).
By
[
27
, Th.7.1]
,
\tilde{L}^{2}_{M,A}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})

is expressible in a unique manner as the direct sum of three linear subspaces

E_{M}^{-}(B|A),\quad E_{M}^{0}(B|A),\quad E_{M}^{+}(B|A)

having the following properties:

(a)

E_{M}^{-}(B|A)
,
E_{M}^{0}(B|A)
,
E_{M}^{+}(B|A)
are mutually
L^{2}
-orthogonal and

q_{M,B|A}
-orthogonal;

(b)

q_{M,B|A}
is negative on
E_{M}^{-}(B|A)
, zero on
E_{M}^{0}(B|A)
, and positive on
E_{M}^{+}(B|A)
.

Moreover, since
2q_{M,B|A}(u,v)=((\hat{\Lambda}_{M,\tau,A})^{-1}u,v)_{L^{2}}+(\bar{C}u,v)_{L^{2}}

for a compact self-adjoint operator
\bar{C}:\tilde{L}^{2}_{M,A}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})\to\tilde{L}^{2}_{M,A}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})
defined by

(\bar{C}u,v)_{L^{2}}=\int^{\tau}_{0}(C(t)u(t),v(t))_{\mathbb{R}^{2n}}\,dt,

namely

q_{M,B|A}
is a Legendre form by
[
27
, Theorem 11.6]
, the subspaces
E_{M}^{-}(B|A)
and
E_{M}^{0}(B|A)
have finite dimensions (cf.

[
27
, Theorem 11.3]
or Corollary on page 311 of
[
28
]
).
Dong
[
19
]
also proved these directly and called

j_{\tau,M}(B|A):=\dim E_{M}^{-}(B|A)\quad\hbox{and}\quad\nu_{\tau,M}(B|A):=\dim E_{M}^{0}(B|A)

(A.25)

M
-index
and
M
-nullity of
B
with respect to
A
, respectively.
Let
m^{-}(q_{M,B|A})
and
m^{0}(q_{M,B|A})
denote the Morse index and the nullity
of
q_{M,B|A}
on
\tilde{L}^{2}_{M,A}([0,\tau];{\mathbb{R}}^{2n})
, respectively.
Then

m^{0}(q_{M,B|A})=\nu_{\tau,M}(B|A)=\nu_{\tau,M}(B)=\nu_{\tau,M}(\Upsilon_{B}),

(A.26)

where the second equality comes from
[
19
, Proposition 3.14]
.
By
[
19
, Remark 3.24]
and Theorem
A.2
we have also

\left.\begin{array}[]{ll}j_{\tau,M}(B|A)&=j_{\tau,M}(B)-j_{\tau,M}(A)-\nu_{\tau,M}(A)\\
&=i_{\tau,M}(\Upsilon_{B})-i_{\tau,M}(\Upsilon_{A})-\nu_{\tau,M}(\Upsilon_{A}).\end{array}\right\}

(A.27)

It follows from these, and
[
27
, Theorem 9.1]
or
[
19
, Lemma 3.16]
that

m^{-}(q_{M,B|A})=j_{\tau,M}(B|A)=i_{\tau,M}(\Upsilon_{B})-i_{\tau,M}(\Upsilon_{A})-\nu_{\tau,M}(\Upsilon_{A}).

(A.28)

For
B_{1},B_{2}\in L^{\infty}([0,\tau];\mathscr{L}_{s}(\mathbb{R}^{2n}))
with
B_{2}>B_{1}>A+\varepsilon I_{2n}

for a small enough
\varepsilon>0
, it was proved in

[
19
, Lemma 3.18, Proposition 3.25]
that

\displaystyle j_{\tau,M}(B_{2}|A)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle j_{\tau,M}(B_{1}|A)+\nu_{\tau,M}(B_{1}|A),

(A.29)

\displaystyle j_{\tau,M}(B_{2})

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle j_{\tau,M}(B_{1})+\nu_{\tau,M}(B_{1}).

(A.30)

The former is also a special case of
[
27
, Theorem 9.4]
.

Remark A.3
.

When
M=I_{2n}
and
A=\ell I_{2n}
with
\ell\in\mathbb{R}\setminus 2\pi\mathbb{Z}
,
based on the arguments in
[
25
]
and the saddle point reduction (cf.
[
